A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

Climate projections indicate that temperatures in the Upper and Middle Ouémé Basin of Benin have steadily increased since the 1960s, and will continue to rise during the rest of this century, by as much as 3°C. Predictions concerning rainfall vary, but all point toward a longer period of droughts and more frequent dry spells (in particular in the Upper Ouémé Basin). These changes are expected to have an impact on agricultural production, especially in the rainy season in the Middle Ouémé. Changes in temperature, rainfall patterns, and shifts in seasonality require urgent adaptation strategies for agriculture and livestock farming. The project aims at reducing the vulnerability of smallholder farmers to climate change by addressing a range of damages caused by floods and flash floods (in particular in Middle Ouémé), improve agricultural productivity and enhance food security.

The Ouémé Basin is a key area for agriculture production, which is Benin's main economic sector. This sector employs 61% of Ouémé population, out of which 85% are smallholders. Many crops (annual and perennial) produced in the area include maize, cassava, yam, cashews. The sector is particularly vulnerable to climate change but also has


---
**Page 7**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE A

an important climate potential, representing almost half of Basin emissions. Increasing intensity and frequency of extreme weather events are impacting the livelihoods of small-scale farmers, agro-ecosystems are becoming more vulnerable to these changes, and the region is experiencing a shift in vegetation from savanna to forest along the transition zone of Glazigou*. As a result, farmers turn to unsustainable and high carbon practices such as clearing of forests for agriculture, burning of agricultural residues, and deforestation. This leads to increased soil erosion and sale of charcoal – thereby increasing pressure on the biophysical environment, reducing productivity and enhancing emissions. The project will address these issues by implementing an integrated climate-resilient management approach (i.e., integrated climate-resilient management (ICRM) approach in the Upper and Middle Ouémé Basin; the vicious cycle of climate change and poverty is broken through this approach which is being implemented across all 15 municipalities within the whole Ouémé Basin.

The proposed Ouémé Basin Climate-Resilience Initiative (OBCRI) project aims to scale up prioritized climate-resilient agricultural practices, build on existing climate-smart agriculture (CSA) initiatives and programs, and overall improve land and water management in 95,000 ha (including 86,640 ha from GCF and 3,360 ha from MAF co-financing), by addressing the drivers of vulnerability to climate change and other environmental disturbances and extreme weather events, in the Ouémé Basin through implementing a mix of hard and soft climate-smart interventions. The project will be implemented across all 15 municipalities within the Upper and Middle Ouémé River basin, systematically addressing the barriers to promoting long-term change. The project will also develop and scale-up the adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices in the Ouémé Basin through a mix of hard and soft interventions. The project will focus on three complementary components: Component 1: Low carbon, climate resilient Crop production enhanced and Ecosystem services restored in the Upper and Middle Ouémé River basin; Component 2: Climate-resilient and gender-sensitive value chains supported for farmers in the Upper and Middle Ouémé River Basin; and Component 3: Capacity building of local actors to promote climate-smart agriculture (CSA) practices. The project will be implemented over a six year period at a total cost of US$ 35,517k.

**Component 1**: Low carbon, climate resilient Crop production enhanced and Ecosystem services restored in the Upper and Middle Ouémé River basin. This component aims to enhance crop productivity while building resilience to climate change impacts for maize, cassava, peppers, chilies, okra, green vegetables, tomatoes, yam, shea, cocoa, coffee, cashew, mangoes, and other crops. The component will also support the restoration of degraded banks with trees planting. The component will work to support capacity development of key stakeholders including farmers, land users, local government officials, extension workers, and research institutions on climate-smart agriculture traditions and innovative prioritized practices and techniques for low carbon climate-resilient agriculture (CRA). This will be achieved through the implementation of a mix of hard and soft interventions such as building dams, agriforestry, improved water management, as well as ecosystem restoration, water sources and marshes protection. The component will also support the scaling up of the Afforestation (Tafi) to ensure the continuation of training beyond the project’s lifespan.

**Component 2**: Climate-resilient and gender-sensitive value chains supported for farmers in the Upper and Middle Ouémé River Basin. This component aims to enhance the productivity, profitability and market demand for the following agriculture products value chains: maize, cassava, shea, mango and others. These value chains were identified as having strong potential for increasing income generation and providing food security for smallholder farmers and producers in the Upper and Middle Ouémé River basin who have strong incentives to adopt and outsource these crops. The scope of this component will be extended to two crops high-value crops: cocoa and coffee, which are grown by a significant part of the local diet – locally-relevant and resilient varieties and climate-smart practices to address the climate scenarios identified for the Upper and Middle Ouémé River basin. This component will support the development of market demand - especially for those crops whose value chains will be strengthened; and why it do not pose threats to the environment, biodiversity or human health. The component will also support the scaling up of training in marketing and finance for farmers and cooperatives and developing networks between farmers and sellers. The component will also support the development of a market-based approach to promote the climate-smart value chain approach, by demonstrating the economic benefits and market opportunities to private sector stakeholders.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 5

and to access support from micro-finance institutions (MFI) through the collaboration with the National Agricultural Development Fund (FIDA), farmers will be supported to sell climate-resilient agricultural products, as well as other income-generating activities. The project will also provide technical assistance and training for MFIs in order to support and scale up their economic activities. The introduction of appropriate tools on climate-resilience assessment and risk management will be provided by the project. In addition, FIDA will be supported to implement the microfinance products promoted by the project in the beneficiary communities. FAO has secured the support of the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development (MARD) for the implementation of these activities.

Component 3: An enabling institutional and financial environment established to promote and upscale low-carbon climate-resilient land use and water management in Benin's Basin. This component will support the development of an umbrella institution for climate change adaptation at the local level, the Office de la Climat et de l'Adaptation au Changement (OICR) in the Ouémé Basin in coordination with the Project Management Unit (PMU). These interventions will be led by the Direction des Eaux et de l'Assainissement du Benin (DEAB), the Direction de l'Agriculture, de la Forêt et du Département d'Aménagement et de Gestion des Eaux du Bassin de l'Ouémé (DEAGE) - SDAGE. This project will serve as an umbrella institution for climate change adaptation at the local level. The OICR platform (governance mechanism) will blend the conditions to promote and upscale low-carbon climate-resilient land use and water management in Benin's Basin.

For this purpose, it will be linked to the Direct Access Entity Funds National pour l'Environnement (FNEC). FNEC will be responsible for the implementation of the project. The OICR will also be supported by the PMU, which will be strengthened to leverage more funding specifically financing climate change-related projects. Under Component 3, FNEC will provide technical assistance and training on climate-resilient land use and water management that will benefit from OICR's intervention so that we further implement climate-resilient agriculture and ecosystem restoration activities in the Ouémé Basin. The project will be implemented over a period of three years with a time project. FNEC's capacity to design, identify, implement and monitor climate change related projects will be enhanced through this component.

Knowledge capitalized and disseminated will foster replication and scaling up of climate-resilient agro-ecosystems and water management practices in the Ouémé Basin. The OICR will also provide technical assistance embedded at community level via the selection of local facilitators who will be empowered to conduct local monitoring based on the indicators defined by the project. This will contribute to the institutionalization of climate governance and institutionalization of project interventions. The project will also reach out to other basin's authorities: FNEC, DEAB, DEAGE, SDAGE, and the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MENED) across the Ouémé Basin. Gender and social protection safeguards will be integrated, especially via the increased participation of women in the project activities.

The OICR will also provide technical assistance to other localities over time by ensuring the targeted beneficiaries have the know-how, motivation and investments to sustainably maintain and keep living in the services delivered by the project.

The EX-ACT analysis was conducted and results show a positive environmental impact due to the implementation of this project. The project is expected to generate 10,524 jobs directly and indirectly over the period corresponding to the generation of 1,783,633 USD in GES; or over 20 years.

All references to MCICD and FNEC as an executing entity shall be and shall be interpreted as references to the Ouémé Basin Climate Change Adaptation Project (OBCAP), as the case may be. FAO and MCICD will function as the Executing Entities for the OBCAP, FNEC, which is a legal entity which operates under the terms of MCICD, will also function as an executing entity for the activities they co-finance. Care will be taken to limit the involvement within MCICD and FNEC of the project implementing partners, such as FAO, SDAGE, DEAB, and other entities. The OBCAP UN will ensure coordination among Ministries and EEs, and the other project implementing partners, as well as between the executing entities and the project's stakeholders. EEs will collaborate with each other from not only civil society/ community-based organizations and private companies.

The project is estimated to have a total budget of USD 69 million and costs per beneficiary of about USD1037. As a funding Investment partner, the Government of Benin has requested that FAO function as the Accredited Entity and as one of the Executing Entities for the project.

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1 Country Context (1000 words approximately 2 pages)

Benin is a Low Income developing country of West Africa, extremely vulnerable to climate change, and is listed as Least Developed Country in the Department of Economic and Social Affairs*. In Benin, the Ouémé Basin, which has been identified as one of the most important ecosystems for biodiversity conservation in Benin. The basin area are maize, cassava, cowpeas, chilies, okra, green vegetables, tomatoes, yam, shea, mango and cashew. The basin covers an area of more than 472,181 km², straddling 46 communes spread over eight departments of Benin. The Basin is characterized by a tropical climate with two main seasons: the rainy season (June to October) and the dry season (November to May). Recurrent and increasing droughts, heavy rains, floods, flash floods and shifting rainfall patterns, which have been recorded in recent years, are among the major challenges faced by the basin. The Basin is home to a population of millions of vulnerable people, depending on the goods and services of the Ouémé Basin ecosystems. This is particularly true for the communes of Ouidah, Cotonou, Porto-Novo, Atakpame, Kétou, Parakou, Tanguiéta, Abomey-Calavi and Zogodoyombo and Zagazogna (Middle Ouémé). The municipalities were identified as highly exposed to climate change.

#### 1. Emission Profile of Benin

Benin has prepared its Intended Nationally Determined Contribution (INDC) under the Paris Agreement in June 2017, which includes a detailed emission profile and intentions in terms of decarbonizing its economy and investing in renewable energy sources. The country’s total GHG emissions are projected to be 45.8 Mt CO₂ eq in 2030 (69% of the baseline), with an average annual growth rate of 1.7%. The largest share is from land use, land-use change and forestry (LULUCF) activities (45.9%), waste (9.0%) and industrial processes (11.4%). Considering LULUCF, the balance of GHG emissions (11.4% of total) comes mainly from deforestation and forest degradation (38.7 Mt CO₂ eq in 2030), which is equivalent to 52.0 Mt CO₂ eq in 1995 down from 61.4 Mt CO₂ eq in 2005, that is a decrease of 20.6%, and (38.4) Mt CO₂ eq in 2010.

In terms of projection of greenhouse gas emissions, in the BAU case, the tendency of overall emissions without LULUCF mitigation measures would be to increase by about 5% per year from 2017 to 2030 (Figure 1). 2. Historical climate trends in Upper and Middle Ouémé

Temperatures over the period 1951-2009 in the Upper Ouémé Basin show a steady increase, at 0.2°C for the maximum temperature and 0.3°C for the minimum temperatures. Similar trends are observed in Middle Ouémé, based on data from 1953 to 2016, with an overall increase of the maximum temperatures by 1.40°C, and of the minimum temperatures by 0.78°C. The analysis of the annual temperature trend for the Upper Ouémé Basin (maximum, mean and minimum) over the last sixty years, indicates an overall increasing temperature trend of 0.23°C per decade in the maximum temperature, 0.15°C per decade in the mean temperature and 0.18°C per decade in the minimum temperature. The highest increase in temperatures during this period was observed at the synoptic station.

Figure 1: Annual temperature trend at Sogbogo and Cagogo, in the Upper Ouémé Basin

![](image_url)

Figure 2: Annual temperature trend in the project areas (in Middle Ouémé Basin (Gazoum, Tchigodjohou and Zagouakro))

![](image_url)

Changes in temperature parameters (heat and cold/humiditati) have been noticed by farmers in all five communes of the Ouémé basin. According to surveys carried out, temperature has increased during dry seasons, leading to more frequent heat waves. The majority of respondents (80%) reported that they are experiencing more heatwaves than before. The respondents confirmed that temperature has significantly increased between slight and high (+1 to +3) in all the communes surveyed. These results are consistent with scientific data indicating higher historical increases of temperatures in Upper Ouémé compared to Middle Ouémé.

### Variations in Temperature parameters

With regards to changes in precipitations, the historical (1930-2011) spatio-temporal variability of the annual rainfall over the Ouemé basin is characterized by a decrease from south to north. The highest precipitation values are observed in the northern regions (800–900 mm/year) (fig. 3). In the upper basin, the highest precipitations during the pre-monsoon season (June-July) are observed over the Ouémé and Mono basins with a mean of about 15-20 mm/day with less or no rainfall. The historical (1963-2005) analysis of the Simple Precipitation intensity index (SDII) from the GLOBIOMS dataset shows that the SDII is higher in the upper part of the basin (Ouémé), from 13–15 mm/day for Glazoué and from 13-15 mm/day for the middle part of the Ouémé, while it decreases to less than 8 mm/day over the lower part of the basin. No trend in the SDII is observed over the Ouémé basin, except at the Bonou outlet (lower basin) and other few stations where an increasing trend is observed, while much decreasing trends of the annual total precipitation (PREPTOT) in the basin is observed.

Although there is a scarcity of certainty based on scientific data regarding changes in precipitation, group discussions held with farmers indicate that the rainfall pattern has changed over time. The past rainy season was bimodal and very difficult. In addition, the rainfall pattern, bimodal in the past in the Southern part (middle Ouémé, Glazoué), is now perceived to be unimodal, with only one rainfall season instead of two.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 10

and magnitude of such floods with significant losses and damages. The increased frequency of extreme weather events in Benin has negatively impacted livelihoods and damaged infrastructures. Indeed, the years 1980, 1986, 1991, 1995, 1997, 2003, 2004, 2005, 2006, 2007 and 2010 are considered as the most affected years. The Benin River basin particularly devastatingly given their spatial extent (55 communes affected out of the 77 of the country) and the number of people affected (348,900). This has resulted in a highly increased flooding frequency reaching one flooding event every three years during the last three decades.

In the upper valley, Olivier et al. (2017)** on the basis of a frequency analysis of the flows of the Ouémé at Bétérou station, found that the average annual flow was 385 m³/s with a standard deviation of 496 m³/s over the period from five years, with amount values greater than 39.2 m³/s. The corresponding flood flows are of the order of 429 and 628 m³/s respectively for return periods of two and three years. Similarly, the frequent river floods are caused by floods with a recurrence interval of less than five years (return period <5 years). For example, the impact of the 2010 floods was estimated at more than 127 billion CFA francs and a decrease in the GDP growth rate of about 3.8%. The frequency analysis of the flow of the Ouémé at Bétérou station shows that there is an increase by 0.8 points (1%). Indicators suggested that floods are occurring more frequently and are more intense than they used to.

Dry spells periods: The historical (1986-2016) rainfall data were analyzed for three different locations/regions of the basin: (i) Djougou (Upper Ouémé), (ii) Glazoué (Lower Ouémé) and (iii) Zagandoa (Middle Ouémé). The analysis shows that the Upper Ouémé region experienced more dry spells than other regions. For example, the number of dry days in the Upper Ouémé basin was found to have the highest number of dry days and the lowest SRI (hottest year). In general, all the three regions experienced more dry spells during 2015-2016 compared with previous years.

Water resources Analysis: The inflow water anomalies in the middle/lower Ouémé basin show high variations with the exception of the period from 1987 to 1993 and the severe positive phase in 1995, 2012 and 2013 (fig. 6). Such data are not available for the Upper Ouémé region.

Strong warnings: According to the findings, NDCs, strong winds are also expected to increase with climate change Overall, field interviews cross-checking scientific data tend to confirm that farmers in the Upper and Middle Ouémé are increasingly affected by droughts, dry spells (especially in Copoigni and Djougou), floods (especially in Zagandoa and Zogodzomy), heat waves and strong winds, which have become more frequent, especially over the recent years.

## Climate change projections

The Upper and Middle Ouémé Basin have experienced steady increase in temperatures since the 1960s; and this trend will continue under climate change conditions. Projections in both project areas indicate a general upward trend in daily maximum temperature, with an average increase of about +2°C by mid-21st century.

In terms of precipitation, climate models indicate that mean annual rainfall is likely to increase until the 21st century, then decline after this period. In the Upper Ouémé Basin, where the main rainy season takes place between March/April and November in the Upper and Middle Ouémé, will become more intense with a higher frequency of heavy rainfalls during the middle and end of the rainy season.

In the Middle Ouémé Basin, where the start of the main rainy season is in April/May, climate models indicate a decrease in daily precipitation for the 2035-2049 and 2070-2099 periods, but an increase in monthly precipitation. This means that there will be more frequent heavy rainfalls during the middle of the rainy season with a gradual disappearance. Conversely, models predict more frequent heavy rainfall episodes during the middle and end of the rainy season.

The frequency of extreme rainfall events and an increased occurrence and intensity of floods during the middle and end of the rainy season are expected in both project areas as well as in Glazoué and Zagnagado.

It is important to note that these shifts in the main and short rainy seasons in Benin have already been observed, especially since 2015 (Majan et al., 2018).

A more recent analysis of the future climate (2020-2050) by Majo et al. (2017:19), in the Ouémé basin confirmed that in the RCP8.5 4.5 projection scenario, rainfall is projected to decrease, whereas the trend will remain very constant for RCP 8.5. In average, the annual rainfall will decrease significantly by 1.33 mm according to RCP 4.5, whereas it will increase slightly (0.27 mm) in the case of RCP 8.5. The same analysis also shows that the wet season will be shorter and more intense.

However, analysis of the future (2015-2050) total annual precipitation by Majo et al., (2017), showed no clear trend for RCP4.5 or RCP8.5 scenarios in the Ouémé basin. The results are consistent with those obtained from the analysis shown with the future climate trends per stations (Djougou and Glazoué-Save for upper Ouémé and Zagnagado for middle Ouémé) (Majan et al., 2018).

Cocker et al (2016) in the lower valley, emphasize that the total groundwater recharge will be significantly reduced by 4.3 km³ and 8.9 km³ under RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5 scenarios respectively.

For example, for a 100% rainfall water received within the lower Ouemé basin, about 31-78% is lost as evapotranspiration in the upper catchment (Lallemant et al., 2016). Climate change events in the Upper Ouémé Basin have greater impacts in the Middle Ouémé Basin, and lower areas.

While both Upper and Middle Ouémé are experiencing hotter and more climate with late and heavy rains, long periods of droughts and increased evapotranspiration, the Lower Ouémé is expected to experience a decrease as torrential rains and higher temperatures, already observed in the upper catchment, lead to strong erosion of the river bed and significant floods in the lower areas. More information on climate change projections are available in Annex 2 Section 3.

### 4. Impacts of climate change on water, crops and value chains

Climate change will increasingly affect water availability. The Ouémé discharge flow water will decrease in the future (2020-2050) with probability of exceedance less than 10% compared to the observed data (1971–2005). This is equivalent to a mean annual decrease of 6.83 m³ per day under RCP 4.5, but RCP 8.5 reveals a more significant increase by 1.59 m³/day (fig. 7).

As underlined in a study conducted by Bao in 2017, more frequent extreme events such as extended droughts and pluies in the rainy season, will increasingly decrease water availability at 20% to 39% for 2050. With regards to the hydrological system, the Middle Ouemé River Basin is expected to experience a reduction of mean monthly flow rates during the dry and rainy seasons, increased temperatures and silting[1]. Likewise, a study from Gilles RC Essou and François Brissette (2013) has shown that climate change will have an impact on the Ouémé River Basin, accompanied by a reduction in mean monthly flows of the river both in the dry and in the rainy seasons.

Concerning the hydrographic network, in the middle basin of the Ouémé, it is likely that temporary or seasonal rivers will be more frequent. The increase in temperature and evapotranspiration especially in the Upper Ouémé will have a negative impact on water resources by reducing the amount of water available for consumption. The quality of water resources will also be affected by climate change. Decreased run-off may lead to increased concentrations of sediments, which can cause an increase in suspended sediment loads and thus increase the erodibility of surface water. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND WINDING-UP PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 14

## B

### Agriculture

Agriculture productivity in the Dadougou Basin will be increasingly negatively affected by climate change impacts. Agroecological areas in Upper Guinea are particularly threatened by the expansion of desert areas, as climate scenarios predict a decrease in precipitation and an increase in temperature. This is discussed further in Section 3 on B3.3 Climatic hazards: climate change impact on crops and effective adaptation measures by municipality (Bamako). The expansion of desert areas will lead to soil degradation, reduced crop yields, and increased water stress impacting, and will increasingly impact, crop production. The rise in temperatures, especially in Upper Guinea, will be accompanied by a decrease in the duration of the rainy season and an increase in evaporation rates, which will reduce ecosystems and reduce yields. Higher evapotranspiration and intermediate dry spells will also lead to shorter growing seasons for crops such as rice, millet, sorghum, and maize. The expansion of desert areas will also be accompanied by lack of rains. Increased temperatures could also increase the duration and intensity of wildfires.

At the same time, climate change impacts on water resources could result in a 40-60% reduction in the availability of water for irrigation in Upper Guinea. This is particularly true in the high agricultural southern zone is endangered by the depletion of useful nutrients in the soil because of increased frequency and duration of dry spells. The increase in temperature will also lead to an increase in evapotranspiration, which means that use of soil nutrients induced by floods is a high risk for agricultural productivity. Moreover, high rainfall surpluses and low rainfall deficits are expected in Upper Guinea. This will result in the following impacts: (i) during the main rainy seasons will force a shift of the beginning of agricultural activities to April or May instead of March; while the short rainy season will be extended from October to December; (ii) the increase in the duration and intensity of the dry season and end the rainy season show a significant increase in the amount of rainfall, leading to floods. Finally, an increase in temperature could also lead to increased evapotranspiration rates during the main rainy seasons (due to prevalence of rain-fed agriculture – see below), and this may affect productivity if appropriate adaptation measures are not taken. The projected impacts of climate change on water resources in Upper Guinea is presented in Annex 2 Section 4.

### 5. Vulnerability and adaptive capacity of smallholder farmers in Upper and Middle Ouémé

The population of Upper and Middle Ouémé does not have the capacity to adapt to climate change impacts and threats on their livelihoods, particularly in rural areas[5]. The municipalities of Cotonou (Upper Ouémé), Glazoué, Zogbodomey and Zaganaado (Middle Ouémé) are characterised by a high population density. In these municipalities, the majority of households live from agriculture as their main source of income as man (and only) economic activity, which is essentially rain-fed subsistence farming in small farms. Population growth rates have been increasing over time and are projected to continue rising. The proportion of households compared to 38% at national level[6] It is also an important driver of climate change vulnerability, which increases the pressure on land resources and water availability. In addition, poverty levels are high (50%) and climate change exacerbates poverty, particularly in less developed countries, increasing poverty traps and annihilating poverty alleviation efforts. The majority of households have limited access to food security and are unable to produce enough food and income to meet their nutritional and income needs[7]. Field visits on the project sites have indicated limited knowledge about climate change impacts and adaptation strategies among farmers. However, several small-scale adaptation projects have been conducted in Benin as described in Section 6, some in rural areas with positive results but others in urban areas where many farmers have been migrated and they are not yet able to adopt large scale adaptation interventions.

Climate change challenges existing agricultural practices and calendars, thereby shifting seasonal patterns, increasing the risk of crop failure and reducing yields. The majority of households still use traditional farming methods as practiced by existing practices. Like the rest of Benin, traditional family type of agriculture is the main economic activity, applied on small farms (less than 1 ha) in Upper Ouémé[8]. In Middle Ouémé, the dominant agricultural system is agroforestry, characterised by low productivity and low yields. Farmers use basic agricultural equipment, such as hoe, and the soil is cultivated manually or with a hand tractor. The main crops are maize (Zea mays), sorghum (Sorghum bicolor) and beans (Phaseolus vulgaris). In Upper Ouémé, the dominant agricultural system is agroforestry, involving large areas of land under trees (mainly fruit trees such as mangoes, cashew nuts and oil palm) and under vines (for tea trees (Vitellaria paradoxa)) of the agroecosystems constitute important sources of income. The main crops are maize (Zea mays), sorghum (Sorghum bicolor) and beans (Phaseolus vulgaris). For irrigation is limited it is estimated that 14% or 360,000 hectares of arable land in the Ouémé basin can be irrigated. Despite this, only a small proportion of farmers use irrigation systems[9]. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 16

In a nutshell, food insecurity and high poverty in the Upper and Middle Ouémé Basin, as well as lack of capacity, economic and financial opportunities to support small-scale climate-resilient agriculture, drive the local communities’ vulnerability to climate change. The combination of these factors with population growth and related needs will lead to serious impact of forest resources in the Ouémé Basin. Non-climatic causes such as deforestation (deforestation, soil erosion and degradation), as a result of intense agricultural activities and/or natural phenomena like floods and droughts, are also contributing to this vulnerability.

Climate change is likely to exacerbate these threats, mainly through fuel wood usage and charcoal fabrication, hunting, interacting with climate stress, are likely to amplify the pressure on forest resources. The increasing demand for firewood and charcoal as a result of population growth, as yields decline because of changing climate patterns, communities increasingly turn to over extraction of wood for charcoal production. This is further exacerbated by the lack of alternative energy sources in the region.

The ongoing depletion of the natural resources and land degradation, which is in turn exacerbated by lack of appropriate policies and institutional frameworks, are likely to increase vulnerability to floods and strong winds. The increasing vulnerability to floods and flash foods especially in Middle Ouémé, water scarcity especially in Upper Ouémé, and soil erosion and desertification are some of the observed climate change effects.

In the context of climate change, decrease in ecosystem goods and services, which are essential for food security and livelihoods, is likely to increase vulnerability to floods and strong winds. The increasing vulnerability to floods and flash foods especially in Middle Ouémé, water scarcity especially in Upper Ouémé, and soil erosion and desertification are some of the observed climate change effects.

In order to address these threats, it is important to adopt sustainable agricultural practices such as conservation agriculture (CA) techniques, including the use of diversified drought-tolerant crop varieties, conserving farming rainwater concentration systems, and using appropriate land management practices. These measures aim at improving yields in light of losses and damage caused by climate change effects.

The proposed project will contribute to reducing vulnerability to floods and strong winds through protecting from increasing heat, floods and strong winds.

### Impacts of COVID-19
Field missions indicated that the main COVID-19 impact took place during the beginning of the pandemic (March-June 2020) due to hand lock-down measures implemented by the government. During that time, there was a scarcity or lack of inputs for production and processing activities in the agroforestry sector. In addition, the actors interviewed indicated that they had noted an increase in the price of inputs such as fertilizers, pesticides, seeds, etc., which affected their ability to produce and process cashew nuts. This led to a reduction in the supply of inputs. Finally, several processing units temporarily closed due to the volatility of raw nut prices and the lack of demand for processed cashew products on the market.

Limited water management capacity, and not enough irrigation systems are also an issue. In addition, there is no market information available for farmers; they are often not aware of various potential buyers operating in their area, therefore relying on the same buyers with whom they have been working individually rather than in cooperatives. Negotiation and financial skills and work through existing networks with pre-identified buyers while also improving communication between producers and buyers of agro-products. The project will provide training to farmers on how to ensure adequate production and quality levels in consideration of climate change impacts through CRA and sensitisation workshops, as well as the development of a market information system (MIS) for mangoes. More information related access to markets is available in Annex 18 Section 2.3.4. The project will also provide training on negotiation skills and financial management to farmers, especially for the more vulnerable groups of society, such as women and youth. The financing schemes tend to focus on Alternative finance (AF) instruments that are not always suitable for smallholder farmers in developing countries where access to agricultural production is still perceived as high. As a result, micro-finance institutions have high requirements with regard to collateral and guarantees. This makes it difficult for smallholder farmers to obtain loans from MFIs and to individual farmers than to organisational groups. An investigation conducted by MAFEP on the reason why many smallholder farmers are not able to access credit is available in Annex 18 Section 2.3.4. Smallholder farmers are scared and uncomfortable to be in debt without any guarantees; if high-interest rates of credit, if lack of stable income or lack of land, to name a few. In addition, they have little or no assets (and/or do not know about the ones available) potential).

Limited access to credits impedes the full development potential of very value intensive crops in Benin that could contribute to other economic sectors such as food processing and agro-industry. The lack of the seasonal credit market, the high cost of money to buy inputs (improved seeds, fertilisers, pesticides), and pay for labour, especially for ploughing and maintaining irrigation systems, are also an issue. In addition, there is a lack of information on the quality of the seeds obtained and poor quantity of inputs in production by producers and very low yields per hectare. As a result, the products obtained do not meet market demand and consequently market and negotiation prices of goods. In some cases, like mangoes, the lack of access to credit also leads to a lack of investment in irrigation, leading impeding the acquisition of raw materials, the purchase of processing inputs, and access to improved technologies.

Despite these barriers, in terms of access to micro-credits, the country benefits from a favourable institutional framework as well as a strong policy environment. The project will therefore benefit from the existence of the Fonds National de Développement Agricole FNDA, a public entity, under the guardianship of the MAEP, facilitates access to loans from banks and MFIs (microfinance institutions) for producers through technical support capacity

The Government of Benin has committed to repay the MFI in case farmers are unable to: To FDA also ensures bank interest rate at 12% (while regular interest rate is 30%). In addition, the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (MAF) will provide a guarantee for loans from the support FDNA has committed to provide to OCRI farmers will increase MFIs confidence to provide loans to OCRI farmers. The Government of Benin has also signed letters of intent with several banks and MFIs present in the Upper and Middle Ouémé have also signed letters of engagements confirming their support to project beneficiaries.

Farmers and cooperatives can acquire the necessary production input, processing equipment and other inputs through micro-credits or loans, since they have increased access to these financial tools. The loan will then be reimbursed under project conditions. In addition, the Government of Benin has committed to provide a guarantee for loans from MFIs that contribute to the development of value chains are in particular credits for the maintenance of plantations (case of cashew trees), credit for the purchase of inputs and equipment (e.g., tractors, ploughs, etc.), credit for the production of maize varieties resistant to climate change), credits for ploughing and maintenance of crops, credits for storage, credits for processing, credits for marketing, etc. The Government of Benin has also signed letters of intent with several banks and MFIs present in the Upper and Middle Ouémé have also signed letters of engagements confirming their support to project beneficiaries.

### Strengths

- Cashew nuts: 
  - Main cash crop (especially in Upper Ouémé)
  - High value (Ghana)
  - Can be grown on orchards or within existing crops
  - Agroforestry can contribute to soil conservation
  - Combines well with locally available crops
  - Increasing global demand for cashew nuts
- There are regional cooperatives in the Ouémé, which collect and sell cashews through village-based cooperatives (limited quantity)

### Weaknesses

- Farmers at local level have poor negotiation capacity
- Cashew trees require expensive equipment and technical assistance to ensure communities, particularly women, can benefit from cashew production
- Cashew nuts are amongst local crops that are not suitable for business based on export
- Lack of storage facilities for cashews
- Widespread illiteracy among farmers, lack of decision-making power and limited access to trading companies, currently most cashews sold to Indian traders.

### Opportunities

- Opportunity to establish communities for establishing value chains
- Opportunity to collaborate with local processors (e.g., the Agroforestry Research Institute) or on private landowners (private/communautaire)
- Cashew nuts are amongst local crops that are not suitable for business based on export
- Potential to improve local value chain
- Potential for international export of cashew nuts, especially if the market is large and there is high potential to CAF 350,176 ha/yr

### Threats (including CC- threats)

- Inflation of foreign capital for investment in cashew production
- Deterioration of local processors' capacity to process cashew nuts that marginalize women
- Gender-based violence: 
  - Women's limited access to decision-making power and limited access to trading companies, currently most cashews sold to Indian traders.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND

## B

In addition to providing training on financial management and ensuring support from FNDA for loan repayment and preferential credit rates, the project will address the above-mentioned issues, together with the need for technical capacity building in the areas of gender equality and women's empowerment. The project will also build technical capacity, which is supported through training in farmer field schools, (ii) financial support with the establishment of savings groups, and (iii) provision of a VSLA model that has been successfully used by the CGIAR Research Program on Roots, Tubers and Bananas (RTB), where group members are trained and accompanied to discuss and address social or environmental issues that they encounter. The VSLA model is an innovative approach to financial inclusion for smallholder farmers, which was developed by farming communities in many countries of the world, will be used to cope with these difficulties and facilitate the development of rural economies.

Through the integration of the VSLA into the groups already organized in FGSs, this approach has proved extremely effective. The VSLA model creates self-managed and self-capitalized savings groups that use members' savings to provide loans for small-scale agricultural investments. This model is particularly suitable for groups of farmers large enough to create a pool of useful capital, and small enough to keep meetings manageable. Groups typically consist of 10-25 members who meet weekly or biweekly to save money in a common account. In other countries, around 90% of groups continue to operate longer than five years after receiving training. On average, the VSLA model has been found to increase savings by an average of 68% on the first year following training (see Section 7.3.2 of the FS for further details on the VSLA approach).

Finally, the Farmer Business School (FBS) approaches introduced by CGIAR will also be integrated into the project. The FBS is a well-known and tested approach based on long-term capacity building in smallholder farmers' communities. It has been successfully implemented in many countries around the world. FBS follows the same methodology as FVS, with a particular focus on building the capacity of farmers to develop businesses, access to credit, cooperatives and increase market linkage.

In contrast to other organizations, a positive impact has been found worldwide in terms of improving economic conditions and income generation by carrying out actions based on the principles of microfinance. The results have shown that the participation in FBS was associated with increased productivity and financial capital of rural communities. Financial capital was enhanced through increased income and profits, savings and loans schemes, which contributed to reduce poverty.

In Kenya, it has been found that their participation in the FBS was associated with increased productivity and, on average, a 6% increase in household income[1]. A study in Kenya suggested that the FBS on tea increased family income and the number of income sources. Increased Income was also reported in studies from Ecuador.

**Ethiopia:** The study in Ethiopia and the Philippines Another study investigated on the perceived impact of FBS) among smallholder cocoa farmers in Ondo and Osun States, Nigeria. It was concluded that the implementation will be strengthened by the promotion of the Dimitra Clubs approach and methodology detailed below.

## 7. Gender issues and safety

Women and men experience the impacts of climate change and extreme weather events differently, with women being particularly vulnerable to these changes due to their weaker access to and control over land, water, services and technology, and the heavy workload they shoulder inside and outside the household.

In the project area, women are involved in the production of crops, however, women’s in fields are lower mainly because of lack of access to inputs such as seeds, fertilizers, pesticides, extension services that can support them with advice on pest control, water, fuel wood, and the drying and storing of produce. In addition, floods or droughts affect the availability of water for irrigation.

Women are also involved in processing and distribution sales activities. However, on the post harvest activities carried out by women are often affected by lack of access to inputs such as seeds, fertilizers, pesticides, extension services that can support them with advice on pest control, water, fuel wood, and the drying and storing of produce. In addition, floods or droughts affect the availability of water for irrigation.

Overall, women in particular have limited access to climate-resilient technologies and practices to cope with extreme weather events such as flooding and drought. This is because they are often excluded from decision-making processes and gender inequalities that affect their economic and decision-making capacities. At low level, there is also limited information on the impact of climate change on women’s livelihoods and how this can be addressed in order to plan and implement climate changes that effectively address and respond to women’s needs. These challenges are particularly important for the project area where women play a critical role in providing inputs in relation to developing institutional capacity for gender budgets and gender-responsive planning.

The success of climate change adaptation initiatives largely depends on the meaningful engagement of all social groups, including women, in decision-making processes. The Dimitra Clubs are a flagship community-driven and gender transformative approach pioneered by FIDA to facilitate greater participation of women in decision making at household level. This participatory approach has achieved success in a variety of areas, including better nutrition, wider adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices, improved access to markets, and increased income generation.

The Dimitra Clubs have been used as an effective tool for promoting gender equality through dialogue for social cohesion and peace at community-level. Furthermore, the approach has stimulated changes in both men’s and women’s attitudes towards each other and their roles within households. This is reflected in better household diets and sanitation practices as well as concrete advancements towards more equal gender relations and equal access to resources.

### Baseline projects and complements with OCFR

Several past and ongoing projects, programmes, and initiatives focus on promoting climate-resilient, sustainable production systems in Benin. However, these development initiatives do not implement an integrated, climate-resilient approach to basin management in Benin, which considers the links between the upper, middle, and lower parts of the river basins and the impacts of upstream activities on downstream communities.

Programme for integrated Development and Adaptation to Climate Change in Benin (PIDACCB/NB)

* PIDACCB/NB is funded by GCF/4IDB
* Total funding provided: USD 209 million [ongoing]

Agricultural productivity and diversification project in the World Bank – Phase III

* Phase III started in 2017-2023
* Additional funding provided in 2017: USD 58.6 million [ongoing]

Targeted area overlapping DCRi project: n/a

The following interventions are implemented in the 12 Departments of Benin:
(i) development of irrigation systems (including water harvesting and storage, processing and storage), including climate smart production systems; to reduce post-harvest losses and increase productivity;
(ii) diversification of agricultural activities;
(iii) development of production and market infrastructure to enhance productivity and competitiveness in the agricultural sector. The project will also support the value chain market through warehouses and other facilities; and (iv) support to value chain actors, including farmers' associations, cooperatives and producer organizations. Through development instruments, OCRi will share the lessons learned and best practices on land irrigation and climate change adaptation with farmers and other stakeholders in Benin. The project is at phase 2 or P0 stage, to support relevant value chains and access to finance for agricultural cooperatives and smallholder farmers. A dedicated website will be built as part of OCRi platform.

Transforming Financial Systems for Climate (TFSC) programme*)*

* TFSC started in 2019-2026
* Total funding provided: USD 58 million [ongoing]

Targeted area overlapping DCRi project: national level

This regional project aims to create a market for investments in clean technologies, especially sustainable energy, in 14 countries including Benin, by removing the financial and institutional barriers that prevent access of small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) and private sector. To this effect, TFSC will provide loans, via local finance institutions, to borrowers who are unable to access other sources of financing; and will also support SMEs in accessing TFSC support and increase the financial resources of FNEC. To facilitate knowledge sharing and synergies with TFSC, the project manager will be invited as guest on OCRi platform.

## Support for the Development of Market Gardening in Zogodzemy and Zagradzani 2018 - 2025

### Co-financing amount: USD 9,740,000
* Agricultural Development and Market Support Project IFAD
* Total project cost: USD 13,971,797
* Co-financing amount: USD 10,381,797
* Co-financing proportion: 75%
* Co-financing amount: USD 1,730,000

### Regional Programme for the Integration of Market Gardening Markets - Prima
2021-2026
USD 4.8 million
Co-financing proportion: 90%
USD 10 38M 280

**Targeted overlapping OORI project:** Oueme and Zou | Zogodzemy et Zagradzani.

The overall objective of PAGAM, a USD 9.74 million project, is to contribute to the development of market gardening in the Oueme and Zou regions by increasing the income of vegetable farmers sustainably, while improving their resilience to the effects of climate change. The project will implement several interventions in 7 departments out of 12 in the country, namely: the Atlantic, Couffo, Littoral, Mono, Mono-Sanaga, Ouémé and Zou.

More specifically, PAGAM aims to:

* Improve access to agricultural inputs
* Support market linkages between producers and buyers
* Increase market access for produce
* Promote processing and value addition of produce

The project PAGAM is directly contributing to several interventions identified in OORI. Under component 1.2 of PAGAM, support will be provided to the implementation of a seed multiplication site located in Modene Basin. Under Component 2 of PAGAM, support will make available to farmers and producers the necessary inputs for improved water management between producers and buyers, improving market access, and providing processing equipment. The project will also contribute to the dissemination of knowledge products developed by PAGAM as value chains will be useful to contribute to the development of market gardening in the Oueme and Zou regions.

The following OORI activities will be implemented in coordination with financial support from PAGAM:

* Activity 1.1: To improve water mobilization and management will be supported by PAGAM's sub-component 2.1 which will organize training in FFS.
* Activity 1.2: To strengthen the Oueme banks will be financed by PAGAM's sub- component 2.3 and through planting of nuts and fruits on the riverbanks using PAGAM's sub-component 2.4.
* Activity 2.1: To improve quality inputs will be supported by PAGAM's sub-component 2.1 which will enhance access to quality inputs.
* Activity 2.2: To support the production, processing and selling of key agricultural products will be supported through PAGAM's sub-component 2.2.
* Activity 3.1: To improve market linkages between producers and farmers will also be co-financed by PAGAM's sub-component 1.1 that will focus on maize producers and farmers.

PRIMA aims to stimulate the regional markets in Benin (Dippoucou and Gogouno) and Togo (Goumou and Kpalime). The project will support the development of infrastructure for processing and storing equipment, training for cooperatives and improved access to market information. In addition, PRIMA will provide technical assistance to farmers on market demand. PRIMA will likely include OORI's supported crops with regional market potential.

The following OORI activities will be implemented in coordination and through financial support from PAGAM:

* Activity 1.1: To improve water mobilization and management will be supported by PAGAM sub-component 2.1 which will organize training in FFS.
* Activity 2.1: To improve quality inputs will be supported by PAGAM's sub- component 2.1, which set up tree nurseries in the 2 municipalities within the Oueme region and will also support the development of its sub-component 2.2 providing training in agroforestry.
* Activity 3.1: To improve market linkages between producers and farmers will also be supported by PRIMA's sub-component 1.2.

### Intervention areas

The project will be implemented in 5 municipalities on Benin Republic: Coppargó, Djougou in the Upper Ouémé - Glazové in the Upper-Middle Ouémé; and Zogbodomey and Zigzagnanro in the Middle Ouémé. The Upper and Middle basins of the Ouémé River are characterized by a population density ranging from 20 to 150 inhabitants per km², and vulnerability assessments for each commune has been carried out via a vulnerability matrix evaluation based on the combined scoring of the (IT) vulnerability index, the (EN) exposure index, and the (SN) sensitivity index. The project will be implemented in the Ouémé River Basin (RBO), and the recent and current attentions of climate resilience building interventions and projects (current and previous).

### B.2 (a): Theory of change narrative and diagram (max 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

The theory of change is based on the assumption that the project will contribute to the achievement of its objectives by establishing a multi-stakeholder platform for coordination of land restoration activities in the Ouémé and the development of a robust financial strategy to provide funding for CCA and ecosystem restoration. # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 29

will address communities (see Section B.1 and table below) to enhance the resilience of the population in the Quéné Basin, reduce emissions and ensure activities persist over time. The OCPF project will break the vicious cycle in which climate change vulnerability increases poverty and environmental degradation, leading to deforestation, soil erosion, low emissions integrated climate resilient management (ICRM) in the Upper and Middle Quéné Basin; this will enhance ecosystem services and increase resilience of communities against climate change impacts. This will reduce flood risk upstream areas spreading to downstream areas. To initiate this transformative change, the OCPF project is comprised of three components: Component 1: Integrated Climate Resilient Crop production enhanced and Ecosystem services restored in the Upper and Middle Quéné Basin with government co-funding; an integrated climate-resilient management (ICRM) strategy will be developed to address the drivers of deforestation, soil erosion and low emissions. This will reduce flood risk upstream areas spreading to downstream areas as well. Floods are reduced especially in Middle and Lower Quéné Basin, soil erosion is reduced by 50% in Upper and Middle Quéné Basin, forest cover increased by 10%, poverty and climate change vulnerability reduced in the whole Basin. This component will support reducing emissions by 73 Tg CO2e over a period of 20 years.

## COMPONENT 2: Value chains supported that provide climate-resilient livelihoods and income to farmers in the Upper and Middle Quéné Basin with government co-funding; combining the use and planning of diversified high-value crops, livestock, fishery and agroforestry systems, improved soil fertility management, climate-resilient farm planning, marketing techniques, access to private sector and finance through support from the FNEC and other financial institutions. This will increase income for farmers by 20% in Upper and Middle Quéné Basin; income can be re-invested in CRA and watersheds for scaling up and expansion of the interventions. The demonstration of climate-resilient value chains will also provide a basis for private sector investment, which also unlock additional private investments to sustain CRA in the long term, materialized under Component 3.

## COMPONENT 3: An enabling institutional and financial environment established to promote and upscale the implementation of ICRM in the Upper and Middle Quéné Basin. This will be achieved through the establishment of a multi-stakeholder coordination mechanism; to support on the ground the farm planning and ecosystem services restoration, as well as the scaling up of climate-resilient value chains. The strategy will also ensure the long-term implementation of ICRM in the Quéné Basin. Moreover, FNEC’s strategic policy will be revised and strengthened to provide a conducive environment for the implementation of ICRM. In addition to FNEC’s implementation, FNEC will provide fund as a financing to cooperatives and organisations willing to implement CRA and other climate-resilient interventions in Upper and Middle Quéné Basin. The strategy will also support the development of an enabling policy framework that will facilitate the scaling up of ICRM interventions is also promoted through various awareness raising events, as well as a rigorous M&E system to demonstrate the impact of the project on poverty reduction, environmental degradation and climate change vulnerability.

### Causal pathway of proposed interventions
The proposed OCRi project will effect a paradigm shift that results in enhanced climate resilience, low carbon emission and improved livelihoods for smallholders in the Ouémé Basin. This will be achieved through institutionalizing the use of agroforestry systems, which are more resilient to climate change impacts than monoculture crops; providing local farmers and cooperatives, operating in key value chains (namely shea, cashew, mango and maize) to sustainably manage their resources; and ensuring that the benefits from these interventions are equitably shared among smallholders and assure the reduction of emissions from upper to lower Ouémé in the long term: vegetation cover will be restored and degraded land will be rehabilitated. This will result in reduced vulnerability to climate change impacts, and income will increase (see Business model annex 3). The project fully integrates gender equality principles into all aspects of its design and implementation.

OCRi project is comprised of 3 components which will support the main goal:

The first intervention (Component 1) aims at restoring degraded land and riverbanks along the Ouémé, using infrastructure, tree plantations and CRL as well as improved soil and water management practices, with impacts over OCRi’s three components. Component 2 will restore and secure water access in the context of climate change, while increasing agricultural productivity through the use of agroforestry systems and other climate-smart technologies that communities against climate change impacts. Currently, interventions under this component are undermined by the lack of institutional capacity to manage these resources effectively and efficiently.

## Limited technical capacity to implement CRA. These barriers will be addressed by the proposed activities under Outputs 1.1 and 1.2.

Under Output 1.1, grey infrastructure will be built to capture run-off and rainfalls and enable irrigated agriculture; while developing land management practices that will reduce soil erosion and improve water infiltration, thus improving soil moisture and soil nutrient. Tree planting on field will favor value chain species, namely cashew and mango. New irrigation systems will be installed in the uppermost sub-catchment of Upper and Middle Ouemé. Although the impacts of climate change have many similarities in Upper and Middle Ouemé, there are also differences between these two areas. The uppermost sub-catchment is characterized by a higher rainfall intensity, more frequent flooding in the uppermost sub-catchment, and more frequent flooding in the middle valley. Hence, protecting the uppermost sub-basin from deforestation will be essential to reduce soil erosion and improve water infiltration. In contrast, the middle valley of Upper Ouemé has a lower rainfall intensity, less frequent flooding, and more sedimentation in the river basin area of the basin upstream with an expected substantial effect on the reduction of downstream flows and floods in the middle valley. Hence, protecting the middle valley from deforestation will be essential to reduce soil erosion and improve water infiltration. Irrigation management practices (IMP) will be especially implemented in the upper Ouemé, which suffers from direct climate change effects such as increased rainfall intensity and more frequent flooding. In contrast, IMPs will be mainly implemented in the middle Upper Ouémé to counteract the mobilized water and control the climate effect. Protection of antelias forests (which are highly vulnerable to deforestation) is also essential for the implementation of IMPs and control floods, which happen more and more often in this area. In land management valley will be developed equally in both areas.

Overall Output 1.2: All secure access on field, rehabilitated degraded soil and protect plots against flood risk (Output 1.1). However, there is a risk that local communities cut out newly planted trees to use as fuel wood or timber. To avoid this problem, the project will promote agroforestry systems in which farmers plant fruit trees (e.g., mango) as well as some very degraded riverbanks, which are located near the plots and can therefore be controlled by the farmers. The project will also promote the use of improved seeds and fertilizers. These practices will be implemented with local communities, through the FFS approach (Output 1.2), which will increase project ownership. The construction of water harvesting and retention infrastructure in the uppermost sub-catchment of Upper Ouemé and the construction of water harvesting and retention infrastructure will induce a change of 1 ha from vegetation to non-vegetation land, leading to an additional carbon sink of 115 tCO2-e over 30 years.

Such interventions contribute to CO2 reduction in a sustainable way as farmers are incentivized to manage their land sustainably. The project will also promote the use of improved seeds and fertilizers. This will reduce emissions thanks to the project intervention farmers will be less incentivized to carry out high emission practices and clear additional forest areas for agriculture. In addition, the project will promote agroforestry systems in which farmers plant fruit trees (e.g., mango) as well as some very degraded riverbanks, which are located near the plots and can therefore be controlled by the farmers in the project area practice full tilt, burn their crop residues and use the input. project, to identify the best combination of trees, crops and vegetables that ensure high productivity, while responding to market demands.

Third, Output 2.2 will create a favorable environment for private sector investments in low carbon climate-resilient value chains through the development of enabling conditions (see business model in Annex 3). Information products underlying the economic benefits of CAFR and the project’s impact on poverty reduction will be disseminated to key stakeholders including government officials, civil society organizations, including MFIs, agri-businesses, private donors and investors. Finally, match-making municipal forums for potential buyers and producers will be organized in the five target municipalities. These are no is a limit of buyers for these key value chains as they are already present within the targeted areas, and a list of potential buyers for each value chain was prepared (see Section 8.10 of the FIP).

Outputs 2.1 & 2.2 together allow for the development of robust, climate-resilient value chains and income streams in the Queré Basin. The project will support the development of these value chains through a series of enabling conditions within Component 2 is key as there is a significant risk that women cannot fully participate, and to benefit from the additional economic opportunities created by the project. This is because women are currently underrepresented in the Queré Basin are currently engaged in different links of these value chains – for example processing maize into flour or selling produce at local markets. Women also face barriers such as lack of access to credit, limited land ownership, organized, have less decision-making power than men, and are more burden to access micro-loan and credits to buy equipment, seeds, etc., and to participate in climate-resilient value chain. women and women-led agribusinesses and cooperatives will be purposely targeted by the project through a gender-sensitive approach. In addition, women farmers will be trained on how to improve their productivity and income generation from their agricultural activities. Interventions to improve loan access will have a focus on women’s access to credit, who face issues due to lack of collateral, limited financial literacy, and other barriers. Women will also be supported in accessing land titles, which is critical for women in the targeted area. In addition, in order to support their entrepreneurial potential, women farmers will be trained on how to improve their productivity and income generation from their agricultural activities.

The third intervention (Component 3) will ensure a long-term enabling institutional and financial environment to support the development of climate-resilient value chains. This is because there are limited technical and institutional capacities in Beni, and more particularly, the Queré Basin to plan and implement government policies and programs that address climate change adaptation and mitigation issues. The project will work with regional and local governments entities to implement CIRM in the Queré Basin and to foster climate-resilient value chains under the umbrella of the National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS). This will be done by engaging key institutions represented at the national, regional and local level. To enable this and address both barriers, the OCRI platform will be established as a permanent mechanism for the implementation of CIRM in the Queré Basin, in the long-term (beyond the project’s lifespan). Establishing a robust, long-lasting platform tackle the existing risk that the project will not have an impact on climate change adaptation and mitigation issues. This is because it is too soon, because they lack the capacity or resources. This risk is also reduced as the project will develop tools to guide CIRM implementation in the Queré Basin. The OCRI platform will be a permanent mechanism for the participation of mainstream ICRM as well gender concerns. These plans are key to guide future public investment and development initiatives in the Queré Basin (Beyond OCRIP project).

## Figure 14: Farmers benefiting from OCRI interventions

Capacity building of local authorities and extension workers for adaptation planning will also be implemented to further create an enabling institutional and technical environment for adaptation in the Oueme Basin. Field missions have also confirmed the willingness and interest of local farmers, particularly women's groups and cooperatives, to participate in implementing sustainable and resilient land and water management.

Beyond an adequate technical and institutional framework, GCF also lacks financial resources to support low cost climate change adaptation projects. The project will build on existing efforts by supporting the development of a strong partnership with FNEC, Benin’s GCF accredited entity. FNEC is already implementing several projects in the Oueme Basin that are funded by international funds; there are opportunities to leverage FNEC's resources to upscale OCRI in Benin. The project will also support the capacity building and institutional strengthening of FNEC as a key partner, supported by GCF funding. This will be done through training workshops, mentoring, and financialized adaptation in Benin. Moreover, FNEC’s capacity to develop, implement, monitor and report on climate change adaptation projects is already well established; it has been working with FAO for several years and financed by adaptation in GCF using its own resources, as well as monitored with support from FAO. This will strategically contribute to the project's success.

The project will also seek to leverage existing resources from public or private fund (e.g., Adaptation Fund). It will strengthen the role of FNEC as Benin’s accredited entity and will provide a strong platform for OCRI in Benin, which is currently not well known. This will foster larger scale awareness and knowledge of ICRI in the Oueme Basin and its benefits; all knowledge and best practices will be shared with other stakeholders through workshops, training sessions, and awareness raising/sensitization campaigns. This will contribute to addressing the existing lack of awareness of the benefits of OCRI in Benin: currently, OCRI is not well known by farmers or local authorities. The project will also support the development on project sites to support project replication and upscaling. Finally, the OCRI symposium will be organized to showcase best practices and lessons learned from OCRI projects implemented in Benin; this will provide a platform for public and private sector stakeholders and incentives upscale of successful project interventions across Benin. These activities will increase large scale awareness of OCRI as well as a robust institutional and financial environment for

## Barriers to access and pathways to overcome them

Without grant financing, the GEF and improved rural communities in the Upper and Middle Guinea will not have sufficient resources to make the investments required to reduce the impact of climate change. There are currently five main barriers to implementing DRM in the Guinean Basin, and significantly increasing vulnerability to climate change. These barriers can be addressed through a multi-sector strategy, as indicated below:

| Barriers | How to be addressed by project |
| --- | --- |
| Technical capacities | The communities of Upper and Middle Guinea are characterized by low technical capacity and awareness on the use of improved agricultural techniques, crop diversification, and water management practices that improve productivity in the face of climate change. As a result, they are not able to implement effective adaptation strategies against negative effects of climate change, including erosion, floods and droughts. |
| Financial capacities | The Baseline Study (included in Annex 2) conducted in the Upper and Middle Guinea Basin highlighted the lack of financial resources for implementing climate-smart agriculture and hydropower systems that are resilient to climate change. As a result, farming communities have limited access to credit facilities, which limits their ability to invest in climate-resilient agricultural practices. |
| Financial capacities | The communities have restricted or no access to micro-credit or other financial instruments (e.g., savings accounts) from the formal sector to invest in climate-related practices, improve their livelihoods and reduce vulnerability to climate change. |

Farmers and cooperatives will learn how to plan and implement climate-resilient agricultural practices on their farms; they will also be trained on marketing techniques to increase their income from these practices.

| Financial capacities | The communities have restricted or no access to micro-credit or other financial instruments (e.g., savings accounts) from the formal sector to invest in climate-related practices, improve their livelihoods and reduce vulnerability to climate change. |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 38

## Institutional coordination

Land or water management in the Ouémé Basin is not organized and there are no coordinated strategies or approaches. Therefore, degradation of the natural capital is observed. The basin is characterized by a lack of restoration and protection of critical ecosystems especially along the riverbanks where deforestation has been very high.

Under component 3, the OCRM platform will be established to coordinate among all four agencies that join their efforts to improve management in the Ouémé Basin. This will be done through the Ouémé Master Plan (SAGE), and implemented through the National Climate Change Strategy (SNCC). The basin is also invested in climate-related interventions in the future. Local communities will be involved.

Under component 1 such strategies of protection and restoration, sustainable adapted production will be implemented and farmers will be trained to do so.

## Weaknesses

One additional weakness is the lack of coordination mechanism or platform among all four agencies that are responsible for water management, including the Ministry of Water Resources, Agriculture, and Agri-markets, the National Office for Rural Development (ONADER), the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries (MELAF), the National Fund for Environment and Climate (FNCC), FIDA. The Ouémé Authority, farmers cooperatives and other stakeholders are not involved in coordinated efforts towards CFM in the Ouémé Basin.

As a result, climate change impacts on water resources are not integrated into basin-specific management plans or strategies without integrating climate change concerns or basin-specific management plans or strategies without integrating climate change concerns or basin-specific management plans or strategies. This results in negative impacts on downstream communities and landscapes. As a result, the natural capital of the Ouémé Basin is not resilient to climate change and the community’s livelihoods are vulnerable to water scarcity, droughts, shifts in seasonality and floods.


---
**Page 42**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 39

The government of Benin and its local extension services do not have the knowledge and technical capacity to implement integrated climate change adaptation (ICCA) approaches, and therefore cannot provide the necessary technical support to the project beneficiaries. The project will build the country’s governance capacity to plan and implement this approach in a coordinated manner.

As underlined in Section 6 of the FS [Annex E], there is a need for a national strategy on climate change adaptation into local and regional development plans. While at the national level, Benin has already developed a national climate change adaptation into relevant policies and strategies, it still lacks a clear framework for the integration of climate change into local development plans. This will be addressed by the project through capacity building in the areas described above and limited availability of climate change information to inform policy decisions and demonstrate strengthening the socio-economic benefits of ICCA approaches.

Awareness

The government of Benin and the private sector are not aware of the socio-economic benefits to implement an integrated approach for climate change adaptation (ICCA) at basin level. In other words, there is no incentive for the private sector to invest in climate resilient agriculture value chains as their benefits are not demonstrated yet.

Benin’s economy is mainly based on agriculture, trade and services. The agricultural sector has been growing at a rate of 4.2% per year since 2008-2013. This growth was driven by the increase in the number of farmers and investment in climate resilient infrastructure, such as irrigation systems.

The public sector, within its very limited capacity, is the main actor responsible for promoting agriculture, irrigation, and livestock production. However, there are no specific programs or projects to promote adaptation to climate change in the field of irrigation; for example, are almost non-existent as well as the promotion of water use efficiency. There is a lack of knowledge products, shared, in particular towards farmers, on how to adapt agricultural practices to climate change and implement ICCM in basin area in Benin.

While there have been some efforts by the public sector (for instance at the National University of Agronomy in Porto Novo) to promote adaptation strategies for increasing productivity under existing climatic conditions rather than focusing on climate change impacts, there is no platform where information about climate change impacts and potential adaptation options are shared. There is a lack of readily available information to inform policy decisions and demonstrate strengthening the socio-economic benefits of ICCM approaches.

Under Component 3, local extension, forest and water services will be trained to implement ICCM in the basin, including the promotion of climate resilient agricultural practices. They will contribute to develop them. They will also be trained under the project to use knowledge products on climate change and knowledge on CRA, agro-forestry and watersheds to other actors involved in the project. This will ensure that the expected project beneficiaries after project completion.

Under component 2 A & B, the economic and environmental benefits of project interventions will be carefully monitored through a participatory monitoring system. The project will also develop knowledge products, and shared, in particular towards farmers, on how to adapt agricultural practices to climate change and invest future investments in resilient farming activities. In addition, the project will provide training modules, e-learning modules, lessons learned and best practices for ICCM in Benin. These will be disseminated through workshops with communities surrounding the projects sites, in order to promote replication of ICCM approach along the Ouémé River. Finally, field visits and training sessions will be organized by the project team to promote replication of ICCM approach across Benin’s watersheds.
