# B.3 Project programme description (max. 2000 words, approximately 5 pages)

The proposed interventions will contribute to achieving the GCF Fund Level impacts: A1.0 increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the most-vulnerable people, communities and regions; A2.0 increased resilience of health and ecosystems; and A3.0 reduced greenhouse gas emissions. The project will focus on three eco-regions in Benin for example, the communes of Upper and Middle Ouémé, which were identified as highly vulnerable to climate change (see Box 5). Specific GCF outcomes are expected from the following components:

- **A1.0 Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems for climate response planning and development of adaptation strategies**: This component will strengthen the capacity of local authorities in Upper and Middle Ouémé communes to develop and implement adaptation plans.
- **A2.0 Strengthened resilience of health and ecosystems**: This component will focus on improving water availability, reducing soil erosion leading to less floods and fish floods in Middle and Lower Ouémé; agroforestry and CRA, long-term vegetation management, and the protection of wetlands and mangroves.
- **A3.0 Reduced greenhouse gas emissions**: This component will reduce deforestation and land degradation, increase the production of ecosystem goods and services, to benefit the whole population of the Ouémé Basin.

Several GCF outcomes for adaptation will also be achieved through the proposed project, namely:

- A5.0 Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems for climate response planning and development
- A7.0 Improved access to water resources in Upper and Middle Ouémé communes
- A8.0 Strengthened awareness of climate trends and risk reduction processes

In addition, the proposed project will achieve one GCF outcome for mitigation:

- M9.0 Improved management of land or forest areas contributing to emissions reduction.

## Project description

The project aims to enhance the climate resilience of the communities living in the Ouémé Basin of Benin, while contributing to the GHG emission reduction objectives of the country. This will be achieved through the implementation of three components that are designed based on the results of extensive field missions conducted in 2018, 2020 and 2022. The results of the missions were used to identify climate change impacts at the commune level (see Annexes 3 and 4). Components will directly address climate change impacts on key value chains. Table 3 proposes a range of CRA measures that can be implemented in Upper Ouémé, Middle Ouémé and Lower Ouémé communes with the aim to support agriculture and productivity in particular for the selected VCs as part of GCR. These examples were drawn from best practices identified during the field missions (please refer to Annex 2 Section 7.2 and 7.3 for more information).

### Table 3: Climatic hazards, climate change impact on crops and effective adaptation measures by municipality

| Climate change event | Upper Ouémé | Middle Ouémé |
|---|---|---|
| **Category** | **Upper Ouémé** | **Middle Ouémé** |
| **Climatic hazard** | **Climate change event** | **Climate change event** |
| **Irregular rainfall** | Early arrival and late start of rainy season | Early arrival and late start of rainy season |
| **Short or long agricultural calendar** | Shortening of growing period | Shortening of growing period |
| **Early drying of leaves** | Early drying of leaves | Early drying of leaves |
| **Heavy wind** | Occasional heavy winds causing flooding of fields | Occasional heavy winds causing flooding of fields |

### Please note that a detailed market study was conducted to support the project. It includes a detailed review of mode and scale of operations, financial analysis, and an assessment of credit access barriers faced by MFIs and smallholder farmers, including barriers/problems faced by farmers. The barriers analysis also integrates the point of view of MFIs, which include lack of awareness about climate change, and FSHI interviews demonstrate an interest in accessing microfinance credits, providing training and support to farmers, and improvements to the production side as failure is a key cause for the non-repayment of the credit.

### Barriers to credit access
- Lack of awareness among MFIs about climate change
- Lack of awareness among smallholder farmers about climate change

### Solution proposed by OCHR
- To provide loans to individual farmers and register farmers into local, municipal and regional cooperatives to organize production and sales, and to develop business.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 44

## MFIs have indicated that farmers often lack financial education, limited capacity to put together bankable business plans, and to provide regular repayment (Output 2.1)

### MFIs and farmers indicate the difficulties for farmers to provide the required collaterals for a loan
- **Financial access to credit:** Farmers are worried about the lack of credit facilities.
- **Training in financial management:** Training is needed to ensure that farmers develop bankable business plans, close follow-up with banks, and implement their financial and business plans (developed through Output 2.1) on a following scheduled loan repayment (Output 2.1).
- **Loan amount for OCR farmers:** The loan amount should be at least 50% of the total loan amount for OCR farmers.
- **Training in increasing productivity and quality control:** Training is needed to increase productivity, especially for maize, mangoes, cashews and shea nuts (Output 1.2).
- **High-quality climate-resilient seeds from national institutes:** Provide high-quality, climate-resilient seeds from national institutes (Output 1.2).

## Farmers in the target communes have access to limited financial services for agriculture
- **Training on climate-resilient production techniques and disaster risk management:** Training is needed to increase farmers' resilience against extreme weather patterns such as droughts, floods, winds and extreme rainfalls, among others (Output 1.2).
- **Create bi-annual forums to connect producers/processors with buyers:** Create bi-annual forums to connect producers/processors with buyers, facilitate crop sale, negotiate price, and ensure stable income for farmers while ensuring revenue but also ensure stable and increased income.
- **Provide FNEU grants to finance the implementation of micro-projects (Output 3.2):** Provide FNEU grants to finance the implementation of micro-projects related to soil and ecosystem restoration (Output 3.2).

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal V3.0 - Page 45

## Component 1: Low carbon resilient Crop production enhanced and Ecosystem services restored in the Upper and Middle Odem Basin

To enhance climate-resilience and reduce emissions on the Odum Basin, an ICM approach— including waterworks, reforestation, and agroforestry—is being implemented. The project aims to restore 105,000 ha of degraded land (Output 1) and CRA (Output 2), including the 53,500 ha under the MAPF funded projects by IFAD.

### Target Area

- **Upper Odem Basin**: 45,000 ha
- **Middle Odem Basin**: 60,000 ha

#### Key Interventions:

1. **Surface Water Collection Structures**:
   - 30 in Cipapara (11 in Djogouc; 8 in Glazoué; 2 in Zangamadou)
   - 23 in surface water collection structures are rehabilitated: 5 in Cipapara, 7 in Djogouc, 10 in Glazoué
   - 600 ha of irrigated plots are established: 200 ha in Cipapara, 170 ha in Djogouc; 160 ha in Glazoué; 25 ha in Zangamadou

2. **Water Resources Protection**:
   - 14 water sources are protected: 2 in Cipapara; 14 in Zangamadou, 42 in Zigodougou
   - 44 artesian boreholes built

3. **Land Degradation Mitigation**:
   - A total of 9,000 ha of degraded land are restored including: 5,700 ha in Cipapara; 1,750 ha in Djogouc; 2,500 ha (DCR) and 3,600 ha (MAPF) in Glazoué; 800 ha (DCR) and 1,960 ha (MAPF) in Zigodougou

4. **CRA and Agroforestry**:
   - The CRA will be implemented on the land that either belongs to the project or is managed by the local municipalities (including villages), as per Article 7 of the law and Section 1.8 of Annex B for details on land tenure systems in Benin.
   - A baseline assessment was conducted on each project site during the FP development phase (see Annexes 2 Sections 4 and 5). The results will be refined with a site-specific assessment of resilience in the project sites to be selected by the project team. This tool will help identify the most appropriate adaptation measures for improving livelihood conditions of smallholders, as well as their resilience and adaptive capacity levels.
   - CRA and agroforestry techniques will be implemented to increase productivity of maize, cassava, cowpea, chillies, okra, green vegetables, tomatoes, yam, shea, mango and cashew. As indicated in the table 2, most suited CRA practices have been identified for each project site based on local conditions, including soil type, rainfall patterns, crop growth strengths and weaknesses.
   - In general farmers tend to limit use similar adaptation measures in Upper and Middle Odem Basin. However, in Zigodougou and Zangamadou, where the climate is more arid, some farmers are already making on what and where the proposed waterworks and CRA are most needed and identify who need them the most.
   - The project will support the refinement of the M&E strategy and set a baseline project for key M&E indicators with primary up-to-date data.

5. **Land Degradation Mitigation**:
   - 30 surface water collection structures: 8 in Cipapara, 11 in Djogouc; 2 in Glazoué; 2 in Zangamadou
   - Rehabilitation of 23 surface water collection structures: 5 in Cipapara, 7 in Djogouc, 10 in Glazoué
   - Establishment of 600 ha of irrigated plots: 200 ha in Cipapara; 170 ha in Djogouc; 160 ha in Glazoué; 25 ha in Zangamadou

6. **Water Resources Protection**:
   - Protection of 14 water sources: 2 in Cipapara, 14 in Zangamadou
   - Construction of 44 artesian boreholes

After-project cover:

- Farm type: semi-commercial

## Agroforestry and CSA systems also directly contribute to reducing emissions through tillage practices and deforestation, better water management techniques, reduced deforestation caused by agriculture expansion due to low productivity; and increase carbon sequestration through enhancing existing tropical moist forest cover.

The activities under component 1 will support reduced emissions as follows: (a) improved CRA practices to enhance agricultural productivity, including the development of small irrigation systems, corresponding to emission reduction by 527 tCO2e over 20 years; (b) agroforestry systems and SLM/SMF practices, resulting in a direct reduction in emissions of approximately 1489-3075 tCO2e over 20 years; (c) improved water harvesting and tree planting to protect river banks and secure water access, contributing to a reduction by 29.3 tCO2e over 20 years; and (d) reduced deforestation through the implementation of agroforestry systems, contributing to a reduction by 252 518 tCO2e eq over 20 years.

## OUTPUT 1: Water harvesting and tree planting to protect river banks and secure water access

Please refer to Annex E, F.2S, Section 7; as well as Annex 17 for the specifications of the waterworks and their location. An O&M plan is also presented in Annex Z, section 3.

Under component 1, the following activities will be implemented: (a) land use change in the Quelwa Basin will be restored and enhanced; floods and water scarcity risks will be reduced, and scale-specific irrigation will be introduced; soil fertility and moisture will be improved; tree cover will increase by 20% over 20 years. The agroforestry systems and SLM/SMF practices have been identified as best suited to the adaptation problems in the Upper West and Middle Guinean.

The project will sub-contractually engage local communities to build hard and soft infrastructure, including wells, dams, and surface broaches, as well as contour ploughing and stone lines that can be easily reproduced and maintained by the communities (see training under Activity 1.2.3). ## Infrastructure projects (see Outline 1.2), in a context where water scarcity is increasing and seasonal shifts affect the agricultural calendar, especially with the late onset of the main rainy season, and the disappearance of the spring rains, which are critical for the germination of crops and the establishment of young trees during the dry season, as well as facilitate recharge of underground water. ### 1.2 Strengthen degraded river banks and restore land with trees

* **Size:** 5,000 ha
* **4,500 ha** in MFP co-financing (existing entities: FAO, MAAP, MVCD).
* **Well-functioning riparian forest provides essential goods and services to communities living along rivers and wetlands. These include timber for construction and fuelwood, non-timber forest products) to local communities. This will reduce risk of erosion during heavy rainfall events, which becomes more frequent due to climate change. The project will also contribute to the achievement of the following objective: this activity will especially focus on Upper Madi and contribute to reduce food risks in Middle and Lower areas, and all along the river banks. The project will be implemented in close collaboration with the project inception phase (year 1). Selection criteria are provided in previous studies (see Outline 8.3.2).

* **UNEP SAP and PADAM projects:** The OCRI platform (section component 3) will provide replicable good practices from these projects to upscale this activity.

* **UNEP SAP and PADAM projects:** The OCRI project will replicate good practices from these projects to upscale this activity, with the aim of reducing food risks in Middle and Lower areas, and all along the river banks (see lessons learned). Restoration sites will be determined at project onset and related land use and tree tenure practices will be identified. This activity will also contribute to the achievement of the following objective: increase forest cover and soil fertility, provide shade and protection against heat stress by heavy rainfall on falls or bare soils, reduce erosion risk, improve water retention especially during drought, and flood-prone areas; like Upper Madi, as demonstrated in previous projects (see Outline 1.2). The project will be implemented in close collaboration with the project inception phase (year 1). In addition, the overall improved management on 9 ha of degraded forest supported by the project will contribute to the achievement of the following objective: support will be granted for reducing emissions as a result of reduced tillage, better residue management practices and no burned residues.

* **Tree planting activities:** This activity will be coordinated by the MVCD and its local branches, through the OCRI platform (see component 3). The project will also contribute to the achievement of the following objective: this activity will especially focus on Upper Madi and agroforestry, to restore soil fertility, retain moisture, provide shade. CROPS will focus on maize, cassava, cowpea, other indigenous crops and legumes. Agroforestry will be planted along river banks and fields as well as in degraded areas; irrigation agriculture and counter-season horticulture will be implemented, supported by the water schemes set up through activity 1.2.4 (see below).

* **OUTLINE 1.2.5: 200 ha** targeted to implement climate-resilient agriculture, agro-forestry and sustainable land management practices.

Under climate change conditions, agricultural productivity in the Outeniqua Basin is projected to decline by 25%. Under the project, this will be reduced to a maximum of 10% through the implementation of climate-smart agriculture (CSA) and agroforestry, to restore soil fertility, retain moisture, provide shade. CROPS will focus on maize, cassava, cowpea, other indigenous crops and legumes. Agroforestry will be planted along river banks and fields as well as in degraded areas; irrigation agriculture and counter-season horticulture will be implemented, supported by the water schemes set up through activity 1.2.4 (see below).

Agroforestry will also support increase carbon sequestration and reducing emissions from additional tree planting and no tillage.

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 49

The proposal will use the FFS approach as key methodology for field activities, in conjunction with the Dimis Clubs approach. The FFS approach ensures a continuous process for updating the information base needed to cope with climate change and its impacts. It is based on the principle that farmers are best placed to provide knowledge on climate change adaptation and mitigation as it is flexible, focused on learning by doing, it respects farmer needs, it is participatory, and it builds on local knowledge and experience. The FFS approach also supports farmer decision-making and helps the community develop adaptation plans. Due to its flexibility, innovation, and focus on building capacity for climate change adaptation, the FFS approach has been adopted in many countries as a tool for local community priorities and farmer needs related to climate stresses and climate threats on production and natural resources. The FFS approach is based on the concept of Farmer Field Schools (FFS), which is an educational model that is the basis of the FFS curriculum development. FFS farmer experience different options in the field and adopt the best practices for their own use.

The methodological alliance between FFS and the Dimis Clubs has been tested in DR Congo, Mali, Niger and Senegal, mainly in the framework of climate-smart and resilience projects. This alliance has led to promising results in terms of increasing farmers' capacity to adapt to climate change and improve their livelihoods through better farming practices as well as enhancing the leadership and empowerment of rural women. The FFS enables Dimis Club members to participate in training sessions, where they learn about climate-smart agricultural techniques that are adapted to their management of natural resources and develop better marketing skills. This valuable knowledge and experience is then shared with other farmers within the community through farmer-to-farmer extension activities. The adoption of these practices at community level, particularly among the most marginalized groups such as rural women and youth.

The FFS approach has been designed in a way that it can be easily adapted to different contexts and cultures, and the adoption of more sustainable agricultural practices, thus making it more possible to effectively contribute to improve household food security. The FFS approach is based on the principle that farmers are best placed to provide knowledge about their needs, exchange information, and influence decision-making processes. Thanks to this approach, rural women have been empowered as they can now participate in training sessions where they learn about climate-smart agricultural techniques and share these practices with other farmers to enhance their capacities and knowledge on improved agricultural techniques so that they can become better informed and more capable of making decisions for themselves and the community.

The FFS Master Trainers and Facilitators are trained together and discuss issues that are important to them. The results of these discussions are also showcased at village meetings with the presence of the whole community, including men and women from different age groups and social strata.

Integrating climate change adaptation and mitigation into a FFS curriculum begins when FFS Master Trainers and Facilitators introduce the FFS programme to the community and the subsequent ground working activities which take place in the field. The FFS Master Trainers and Facilitators are trained by the GCF through a Community Resource Mapping exercise with the community. The assessment includes site specific considerations, such as climate change impacts, soil fertility, water availability, land use, etc., and is used to identify areas out at formulation stage. Baseline assessment exercises are used to identify site specific adaptation strategies and techniques that can be integrated into the FFS curriculum. These strategies and techniques should be simple (and deeper) and to be integrated in the curriculum. Regardless of the field studies that are chosen, it is important to include the following: a) climate change impacts; b) climate change adaptation options; c) climate change mitigation options which are critical, simple and cheap, and tools to be used by farmer to monitor key variable of weather patterns. At the end of each FFS training session, the Master Trainers and Facilitators will have an opportunity to share lessons learned from experiments in the field.

Lessons learned from the field will be applied to the targeted crops and sites identified in the formulation phase in the intervention areas, will be adapted to the different sites and improved through the FFS trainings in order to increase community capacity for climate change adaptation and mitigation.

Some examples of field studies and topics special on climate change adaptation and mitigation to be integrated into the FFS curriculum are: Seed germination. Mulching and mulches. Moisture stress during the growing season; Rooting depth and root systems. Water harvesting techniques. Crop rotation. Drought tolerant varieties. Heat-tolerant varieties. Adaptive Varieties – temperature and moisture stress tolerant and early maturing varieties. Impacts of extreme weather on crops. Soil fertility management. Fertilizer use efficiency. Organic fertilizers. Compost. Manure. Green manures. Fertilizer management and production. Reducing emissions from enteric fermentation. Sequestering carbon in soil.

1.2.1 Train 15 FFS Master Trainers and facilitators (women and men).
(Selection see feasibility study section 8.1.2 for selection criteria) and training of 15 existing FFS Master Trainers (mix of women and men) will be conducted in a 3-day training session (training session is organized to refresh and update the Master Trainers on the FFS methodology and on the key technical issues needed by project). These Master Trainers will conduct the trainings of facilitators (ToFs), which are described in the following activity.
Training of Master Trainers will include also basic finance and business matters – particularly related to farm income diversification, as well as gender equality. The training will be conducted over 3 days (see component 1.2.2) they will be supported by external subject experts on financial/business matters, similarly this external support will be provided for the facilitators.
Master Trainers and selected Facilitators (see activity 1.2.2), will create a network/association to operate as technical back-up for the project. The association will also provide a platform for sharing experiences and lessons learned which will assure the support of the future activities linked with the financial facility (see component 3), which will provide income diversification opportunities. These associations will be established through engagement during the project implementation to assure the sustainability of the support.
1.2.2 Training of 250 Facilitators (at least 40% women) – this is per 50 selected municipality- will be trained. First, a training manual will be developed for the facilitators and will include: an introduction to FFS; a validation workshop, which involve technical experts and FFS Master Trainers. Secondly, according with the curriculum, the training will consist of 12 modules (see Annex 3) covering the following topics: crop production systems, climate-resilient agriculture (CRA), for maize, cassava, cowpeas, chilies, chicory, green vegetables, tomatoes, yam, agronomic practices, post-harvest handling and processing of crops, water management, soil fertility management, agroforestry, animal husbandry, small enterprises (with operation and maintenance manual developed in Annex 4). Section III will provide a framework for FFS implementation. The training will be conducted over 3 days. In addition to the training from fresh soil and plant samples, the trainings will include practical sessions on composting, water harvesting from rainwater harvesting tanks, and the use of water and humus from deep in the soil. In addition to the soil organic matter content, the trainings will also cover the following topics: crop production systems, climate-resilient agriculture (CRA) for maize, cassava, cowpeas, chilies, chicory, green vegetables, tomatoes, yam, agronomic practices, post-harvest handling and processing of crops, water management, soil fertility management, agroforestry, animal husbandry, small enterprises (with operation and maintenance manual developed in Annex 4). Section III will provide a framework for FFS implementation. The training will be conducted over 3 days.
FAO has already designed several training materials for FFS, including on Land and Water management and Climate Change, which will be easily adapted to the local context in Benin. The material will include 6 modules10), with gender- sensitive content (e.g., women’s role in land and water management, post-harvest handling and processing of nuts and fruits); it will also comprise demonstration video. The training modules will be disseminated on the FFS website and will be available for download. In addition to the theoretical training, practical sessions will be organized at the end of each module. These practical sessions will allow participants to apply what they have learned from the project sites, new site technologies for CRA, and peer-to-peer review as the material will include a set of questions that can be used by facilitators to guide discussions. The practical sessions will also explain the various “starter kits”, with specific adaptation options and seed varieties, based on the needs and specifications of each community. At the end of this training, participants will receive their certificates.
The project will train 250 facilitators (at least 40% women) in 10 municipalities (50 per municipality) will be trained: the project will organize 10 Training of Facilitators (ToFs), for a duration of 3 months and will provide training materials to each ToF. The ToFs will then train their facilitators, who are selected from among the facilitators. Based on the country capacity, more ToFs can be organized simultaneously in the various municipalities. On completion of this training, participants will receive their certificates.
The project will also provide a 3-day training for each facilitator to implement 2-3 Farmer Field Schools (FFS) per year; lasting 4 to 5 months each. These facilitators will conduct FFSs with farmers in their communities and will be responsible for the follow-up of the trainings.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 51

The common selection criteria for facilitators are as follows: a) have agricultural training of some kind, formal or informal; b) have some level of advanced skills, knowledge and experience in agriculture/farming; c) be technically confident but not necessarily formally trained; d) be able to work independently and effectively with others within the community and connect well with other community members; e) have good people skills and an aptitude for intercultural and participatory learning; f) be committed to working with women farmers in their communities, particularly those who are less visible or more vulnerable; g) have a dynamic and confident personality. Priority will be selection given to women to proactively engage in increasing women among the facilitators.

### 1.2.3 Introducing climate resilient agriculture, including agroforestry, to enhance agricultural productivity under climate change and reduce emissions (MAEP/communities). Farmers in the larger sites will be trained and receive agricultural inputs to implement climate-resilient practices and technologies. The training will be conducted by facilitators dedicated to women. The 250 facilitators trained under Activity 1.2.2 will disseminate the practices through the following channels: a) train 3,750 farmers in 45 target municipalities; b) train 6,000 farmers in 9 target municipalities; c) train 8,000 farmers in 5 target municipalities; an additional 10,000 farmers will be trained in the FFS set up by MAEP. Beneficiaries of the training will be women who are interested and willing to participate in climate-resilient farming practices with a score >2 on their willingness to participate, their availability and their interest in developing climate-resistant farming; the willingness to adopt new technologies and practices; and the ability to access inputs and services. These selection criteria will be further refined at project level by FFS. Over 100,000 farmers will be dedicated to women. The training will cover a range of topics including: crop production (e.g., maize, sorghum, millet); livestock rearing; horticulture, processing, and storage of fish, fruits and crops like cassava.

The facilitators will use the term "gobono" which is used in the Zambian language to describe a basket. The facilitators summarize each day at session summary. Finally, at the beginning and at the end of each training cycle, a knowledge assessment will be conducted using a pre-tested tool. This tool will be used by the facilitator to estimate the improvement of general knowledge and technical skills acquired by each participant. Also, a final evaluation will be carried out after the completion of all training cycles.

The 16,250 trainers beneficiaries trained under the OCR FFS will also receive farmer's starter kits, including small tools, seeds, and other inputs to start their own farm. The facilitators will provide information on market interest and capacity of the members. 150 FFS will receive an introduction to the key elements of the business. Although the training is not a formal business course, it will cover topics such as marketing, sales, finance, budgeting, finance, marketing and business management concepts will be introduced into the curricula of the first cycle of FFS. Business planning will be covered in the second cycle of FFS. The facilitators will also provide information on farm and plant investment planning, with special focus on developing women's entrepreneurial capacity. In parallel, the facilitators will train the farmers to establish a savings group. This is an important element for the FFS as it allows members to take out a loan and develop small income generating activities. It retrieves traditional ways of saving and provides a platform for women to save money together.

When associated with Farmer Business Schools modules, it creates the initial conditions for improving farmers' business skills (see Table 2.3). The facilitators will also train the FFS on how to establish their own savings groups (Table 2 and 3).

In order to setting up and operating VLSAs are: a) Preparatory phase (4-6 weeks); b) facilitators assess community needs; select beneficiary communities, provide background information to local leaders and select VLSA groups to be trained. The facilitator will also train the group on how to elect their members, how they will manage their funds, elect their leaders, draw up their internal rules and define procedures that will govern their activities. They learn how to record transactions and keep accounts. The facilitators will also provide information on market interest and capacity of the members.

The VLSA consists of a development phase and a maturity phase, each lasting approximately 18 weeks. The facilitator continues to monitor the progress of the group during this period. At the end of the development phase, an assessment is carried out to determine how well the organization will support itself.

Introducing climate-resilient agriculture (CRA) through Activity 1.2.3 would contribute to shifting cropping practices on smallholder farms from conventional farming systems towards more sustainable and resilient ones by increasing yields through agroforestry practices and implementation of perennial fallow agroforestry systems. Emissions will be avoided through the use of agroforestry systems, which are known to sequester carbon in soil and trees. The project aims for a reduction of emission by 52.587 tCO2 eq over 20 years.

### 1.3.1 Introduction of climate-resilient agricultural practices and crop rotation material. For tree seeds and propagating material (e.g., shea), the pilot will rely on MCVO who has already established a national tree seed collection and propagation center in Lusaka. The project will also work with other organizations such as the National Seed Board to select seed sources information, mother trees selection, seed harvesting - cleaning - storage, control and certification of seeds. The project will also train farmers on how to collect, store and handle seeds. The collected tree seeds are characterized as recalcitrant; particular actions will be developed to ensure that the seedling producers receives viable seeds. Further for propagation through seeds, farmers will be trained to apply Farmer Managed Natural

## Green Climate Fund Proposal V3.0 | Page 52

### Component 1: Regeneration of degraded land. This will also include grafting operations on farm to shortened the production cycle and enable the production of desired fruit.

For Mango, Cashew, annual crop and vegetable seeds and propagation material, the project will collaborate with (National Agricultural Research Institute) NARI and DRC (Dakar Region) through the strategies that will be put in place by the OCS with the MAFP projects financed by IFAD to prepare the technical descriptions of the varieties that will be procured for the project. The project will also work closely with NARI, DRC and other partners, with a view to assist supply.

#### Technical description

The project will establish one tree nurseries per village upstream for the production of quality seedlings. In each village, the project will build one tree nursery of 10,000 tree/year capacity in each target commune; the existing nurseries attached to the DRC and NARI offices will be used as a base for this activity. The farmers who are involved with the project will benefit from the MAFP projects financed by IFAD experience on seed and seedlings supply. This will enable the project to establish a quality seedling production system.

### Component 2: Climate-resilient and gender-sensitive value chains, supporting farmers' livelihoods in the Upper and Middle Oussouye

#### Output 1: Farmers’ income diversification through improved agricultural practices (IAP) under FBS (under Output 1.2), 100 best productive farmers fields schools among the 150 which attended the training and set up VLSAs (see activity 1.2.3) and the 100 FBS dedicated to women, with priority given to women-headed households.

The project will work closely with the FAO farm business school (FBS) in order to reinforce their business capacity, create or consolidate cooperatives and reinforce existing ones, and provide them with training on business management. The project will also support the farmers by providing them with marketing techniques and access to finance – which will also be supported by MAFP through an additional 100 FBS and by reinforcing the role of women in the cooperative structure. This will contribute to increasing the productivity, improving farmers' livelihoods and income stream, thereby reducing poverty, food insecurity and climate change vulnerability.

The project will also support the development of a private sector for agricultural products, which will generate economic benefits; through rigorous M&E, it will also boost private sector's willingness to invest in CRA and ICRN in the Oussouye region. The project will also work with the FAO to facilitate the establishment of long-term agreements between farmers, agri-businesses, and sellers of agri-products which will be facilitated through organizing matchmaking sessions.

#### Output 2: Farmers' and cooperatives' income stream diversified, enhanced, and secured in the face of climate change

## Component 3: An enabling institutional and financial environment established to promote and upscale climate-resilient management in Benin's Basin

To further not the OICR approach in the Ouémé Basin, and upscale it to other sites, Component 3 will support the establishment of an enabling institutional and financial environment for climate-resilient management. This component coordinates the ICRM works. A communication strategy including newsletters and events will be designed for the platform. The communication strategy will include a newsletter that will be published every three months. Content for Component 1 & 2, and data demonstrating the benefits of project's interventions, will be updated. Information generated from the monitoring and evaluation system (M&E) will also be included in this newsletter.

The financial mechanism under Activity 3.1.1 will be specially packaged by a communication specialist for the private sector organizations, including the Benin Chamber of Commerce and Industry (CCBI), the Association des Entreprises de l'Alimentation et de la Transformation Alimentaire du Faso (AETA) in Benin and Benin Chapter of Commoners for agribusiness. A long-term strategy will be designed to ensure the sustainability of the financial mechanism. The platform will also be responsible for developing a communication platform, regulatory frameworks to be developed, and capacity building implemented for local extension officers. This will set up a sustainable financial mechanism that will allow the private sector organizations to continue their institutional strengthening with availability of finance, a market to expand FNEC's resources, which are currently limited by lack of funding. The financial mechanism will also ensure the sustainability of the project. The FNEC can use its resources to support projects and activities and what projects are eligible is ensured to ensure FNEC's resources are used for the intended purpose.

The project will also be linked with other climate change initiatives. The goal is to ensure that what we do in Ouémé Basin is replicable elsewhere, and that it becomes a model for other countries. A communication mechanism to promote climate-resilient initiatives. Together with the other communication products and events; the platform will be responsible for emphasizing on the importance of a gender-responsive institutional and financial environment and promote women's role and participation.

To ensure the long-term financial-representative, FNEC's capacity will be enhanced as DAE building FNEC's capacity is not sustainable. The project will also be linked with other climate change initiatives. The goal is to ensure that what we do in Ouémé Basin is replicable elsewhere, and that it becomes a model for other countries. A communication mechanism to promote climate-resilient initiatives. Together with the other communication products and events; the platform will be responsible for emphasizing on the importance of a gender-responsive institutional and financial environment and promote women's role and participation.

The Niger Basin Authority is a multi-country institution, created in 1964. It is in charge of integrated development projects in the basin.

* **The Volta Basin Authority** is a multi-country institution.


OUTPH 3.1: OICR project institutionalised through a multi-stakeholder platform; regulatory frameworks and capacity building

There is currently no coordination mechanism that allows concerted efforts to sustainably manage Benin's basin and its resources. The project will be implemented in the Ouémé Basin, which covers 20 municipalities across three landscapes. The OICR multi-stakeholder platform thus will be established to connect all relevant stakeholders engaged in climate-resilient management of the Ouémé Basin. These stakeholders include local governments (e.g., support OICR through signed reference letters), especially their local extension services for agriculture, waterworks and forestry; civil society organizations (CSOs) (e.g., support OICR through signed reference letters); private sector (e.g., support OICR through signed letter). These stakeholders play a key role in local development planning, they will receive training on climate-resilient management of the Ouémé Basin. The project will also support climate change management in the Ouémé Basin. This will support the replication of OICR's approach beyond the five target municipalities and to other sites in Benin.

The platform design will include a communication strategy to ensure the dissemination of knowledge, including on good practices and lessons learned from the project implementation. The platform will be managed by MOFAV with FAO support in terms of capacity building for project implementation. The platform will also be responsible for developing a communication strategy that includes specific role for financing, designing and implementing climate change projects. Women and women's organizations' participation is critical to ensure the sustainability of the project.

Another key step to ensure long-term governmental support to implement ICRM in the Ouémé Basin is to formally institutionalize the approach through local regulations and ensure their implementation, and to build local capacity for its continuation.

Implementing these efforts will be conducted through the OCCI platform; members will be trained on climate change adaptation planning and participate to the revision of key development plans for mainstreaming ICRM. This is a key element in ensuring that the five targeted municipalities are able to implement ICRM at scale within the Ouémé Basin. This, complemented with the strengthening of private finance investments in OCCI (see Output 3.2), will ensure the sustainability of the project.

### 3.1.1 Establish and activate the OCCI platform ensuring adequate and meaningful participation of women and men's organizations and civil society actors

The OCCI platform will be established to facilitate interactions between the platform members, including key stakeholders such as local communities, private sector, and government representatives. The platform will include a web-based (online) portal hosted by MCVD, and newsletter will be sent on a regular basis to platform members. The platform will also provide guidance for the implementation of ICRM in the five targeted municipalities.

The OCCI platform will be established through the following activities:

* **Output 3.1.2:** Implement regional and local climate-resilient development plans within the Ouémé Basin to ensure long-term sustainability of ICRM.
* **Output 3.1.3:** Develop a communication strategy for the platform that will facilitate interactions between the platform members, including key stakeholders such as local communities, private sector, and government representatives.

The implementation of the plans – including restoration along the Ouémé riverbanks by local forest services and community-based organizations funded under the OCB platform, beyond the project area – will be extended to other areas in the region. The CBA platform – OBA and extension officers - will be trained; the training will be extended to members of other basins' steering committees (e.g., Benin River basin) as well.

3.1.3 Implement riparian MAF and restoration in the Ouémé Basin: Staff members of MOFIV and MCVD, as well as extension officers from the forest department in the target municipalities – all members of the OCB platform – will be trained to implement the project’s activities on the ground. The training will include technical aspects related to the restoration along the Ouémé riverbanks. Moreover, they will be capacitated to implement the SHARP tool (or similar resilience assessment tools) and to conduct participatory workshops with local communities in order to assess their capacity in the resilience and adaptive capacity levels of smallholders in the selected municipalities, resulting from the project’s activities. The training will also include technical aspects related to the selection of tree species for planting and riverbank restoration will be selected based on: (i) their effective movement on forest and/or agriculture land; (ii) their ability to adapt to climate change (e.g., drought tolerance, resistance to pests and diseases, etc.), in local language); (iii) participation of women will be highly encouraged. Finally, extension officers will be capacitated to provide technical assistance to farmers in the implementation of the project’s activities on the ground. Technical support will be provided and ToFs of the extension workers will be adjusted to include financial/business/climate change continued education modules. The training will also include a module on gender equality and women empowerment (based on: i) their field experience on climate resilient agriculture, (ii) their duty status, which must be in one of five main categories (Couples, Spouses, Glacoué, Zingarhando, Eponidoms), (iii) priority will be given to female extension officers.

OUTCOME 3.2 STRENGTHEN FNEC’s capacity to ensure continuous support to climate-resilient farming in the Ouémé Basin

Under the OCB platform, GCF fund will be used to strengthen FNEC’s capacity to capture climate finance and to develop a financial strategy for the project. The funds will also be used to purchase equipment (e.g., tractors) and cofinance (co-finance) will be used to finance climate-resilient micro-projects. These grants will be accessible to FNEC farmers who are interested in implementing micro-projects (e.g., agroforestry, tree nurseries, etc.) as part of the micro-project proposal. FADF will work with FNEC to develop a project selection check list that, which ensures the selection of projects that are consistent with the project’s objectives and that can be implemented by FNEC farmers. FADF will monitor the implementation of these micro-projects on the ground, produce detailed progress reports, and ensure that the funds are used in accordance with the project’s financial strategy. The GCF fund will also be used to finance FNEC its increase current resources, some of which will contribute to covering the operational costs of OCB platform activities. This funding mechanism is expected to develop enable the continuation of climate-resilient agriculture and value chains beyond the project's lifetime.

3.2.1 Develop a robust financial strategy for FNEC:

Agricultural extension officers (AOs) will be trained on financial management, and sharpen its focus on supporting climate change-related projects, will be developed with the MCVD and MOFIV. This strategy will include: - A clear definition of the project’s financial goals and criteria which ensure the prevalence of CC concerns in selected projects.
- Financial roadmap, with a view to increasing FNEC's available resources to implement climate change-related projects, including: (i) the use of the GCF fund only provides loans but the possibility of aid loan will be explored;
The financial roadmap will provide pathways to increase the current green-bonds tax base that provides funding to FNEC, to leverage investments from the private sector (which would already be incentivized under Outcome 2.3), and to develop a business plan for the project. The business plan will include a detailed description of how the project will be financed by GCF, for which FNEC is a national accredited entity. Access to these funds will also be enhanced under Activity 3.2.0. Consequently, FNEC will have access to additional resources that can be used to finance its operations and ensure the facility remains operational to support ecosystem restoration and CRA initiatives will be explored.

The project is committed to verifying the institutional position of collaboration between the two fund (FNEC and PNUD) and the OCB platform. The two funds are envisaged to allow reciprocal advantages by sharing the experience and capacities of each other. This could be an avenue to explore to increase the resources available to the FNEC, particularly in terms of private sector involvement, expansion of access to credit (focuses on waste management and recycling projects).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 58

## B

### 3.2 Build FNEC's capacity to design, select, implement and monitor climate change-related projects

#### 3.2.1 Build the FNEC’s capacity to design, select, implement and monitor climate change-related projects
FNEC GCF fund will be used to provide technical assistance and build FNEC's capacity to design bankable climate change projects (including those that focus on climate-resilient agriculture and ecosystem restoration, or receive funding from national/ international funds and donors). At the end of this project, FNEC will have designed at least 10 bankable projects. The GCF fund will be used for technical assistance to design these projects. This support FNEC to develop such proposal (capacity building) and submit it to the most adequate fund by projects ends. FNEC will also use the GCF fund to hire a communication expert to provide FNEC's members to ensure the use of fund take into account women-specific needs and priorities.

#### 3.2.2 Provide finance to micro-programs that contribute to climate change adaptation and ecosystem restoration in the Upper and Middle Upland Quelimes
This activity will be funded by FNEC. A call for proposal will be launched by FNEC using its current procedures for project selection. The call for proposals will focus on micro-projects that focus on climate-resilient agriculture and ecosystem restoration and/or protection in the Upper and Middle Upland Quelimes. Micro-programs will be selected by FNEC based on the check-list that will be designed at project onset with FNEC partners. These projects will be implemented by local communities, including women's groups, and will focus on a strong climate change adaptation and ecosystem restoration angle, and gender-responsiveness; FNEC will also develop a monitoring framework to monitor progress of these micro-programs on the ground, produce reliable progress reports, and ensure financial transparency.

# B.4 Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

## Project Governance Structure

### Accredited Entity (AE): FAO

The Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) will be the Accredited Entity (AE) for the Project. As the AE it will be responsible for project implementation, including supervision and monitoring, project completion and evaluation in accordance with the detailed provisions outlined in the GCF Project Document. The AE will also be responsible for ensuring that all relevant parties involved in the Project are aware of their respective roles and responsibilities.

### Supervision Entity (SE): FAO

The AE will be supervised by the SE which is the FAO Regional Office for Africa (RAF), located in Accra with support by the Office of Climate Change, Biodiversity and Environment (OCCE) and other technical units. The SE will provide overall supervision to the Project, including oversight on project implementation, monitoring and evaluation, and reporting.

### Supervision Unit (SU): FAO Country Office for Benin

The AE will be supervised by a dedicated FAO-GCF Project Task Force (FTF) comprising relevant staff from the FAO Country Office in Benin. The FTF will provide overall supervision to the Project, including oversight on project implementation, monitoring and evaluation, and reporting.

### Supervision Unit (SU): GCF Secretariat

The AE will be supervised by a dedicated GCF Task Force (GTF) comprising relevant staff from the GCF Secretariat in Bonn. The GTF will provide overall supervision to the Project, including oversight on project implementation, monitoring and evaluation, and reporting.

### Supervision Unit (SU): FAO Country Office for Benin

The AE will be supervised by a dedicated FAO-GCF Project Task Force (FTF) comprising relevant staff from the FAO Country Office in Benin. The FTF will provide overall supervision to the Project, including oversight on project implementation, monitoring and evaluation, and reporting.

### Supervision Unit (SU): GCF Secretariat

The AE will be supervised by a dedicated GCF Task Force (GTF) comprising relevant staff from the GCF Secretariat in Bonn. The GTF will provide overall supervision to the Project, including oversight on project implementation, monitoring and evaluation, and reporting.

### Supervision Unit (SU): FAO Country Office for Benin

The AE will be supervised by a dedicated FAO-GCF Project Task Force (FTF) comprising relevant staff from the FAO Country Office in Benin. The FTF will provide overall supervision to the Project, including oversight on project implementation, monitoring and evaluation, and reporting.

### Supervision Unit (SU): GCF Secretariat

The AE will be supervised by a dedicated GCF Task Force (GTF) comprising relevant staff from the GCF Secretariat in Bonn. The GTF will provide overall supervision to the Project, including oversight on project implementation, monitoring and evaluation, and reporting.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 69

## B

**FINED:** The Fund co-finance the OCRM project and is executing entity for the activities it cofinances. FINED has been since 2003, a financial instrument placed under the supervision of the MCVOID. FINED is semi-autonomous legal entity with its own budget and accounts. It is responsible for financing projects in the areas of rural development, environmental protection and climate change. Using different sources of funds, in particular green taxes, FINED opens call for project proposals from entities that are eligible to receive financial support from the Fund and submit their proposal to the executing organisation. The projects must be aligned with FINED's strategy and list of project theme; these are only funding thematic areas that are consistent with the GCF priorities and aligned with the GCF with Direct Access Entities since 2019. FINED will also receive technical support to strengthen its mandate.

**MAEP:** The Government of Benin, acting through MAEP, will be the executing entity for the activities it cofinance through the OCRM project. The MAEP is a legal entity under the supervision of the Ministry of Rural Development (MVRD) and the Directorate of Rural Engineering (DGR) which is equally controlled by MAEP and the Lowlands Unit under its supervision. The MVRD is responsible for the implementation of projects in the areas of rural development, environmental protection and climate change. The DGR is legally controlled by MCVOID. The MAEP, with these local delegations - at the village level - is the institution responsible for agricultural and pastoral hydraulic, water and soil conservation, adjudication, forest management and reforestation.

### Project Management Unit (PMU)

A PMU will be set up by FAO and MCVOID. It will be established with office space procured by the MCVOID. A National Project Manager (NPM) will be responsible for project implementation and coordination with all project stakeholders, and operational leadership of the PMU will be recruited based on a competitive process; his recruitment will be validated by FAO. The NPM will be supported by a team of national staff who will be recruited from among the technical experts at the national/central level in close coordination with the EE. Under the leadership of the NPC, the PMU will also coordinate with the five municipalities to ensure that the project activities are implemented as planned and aligned with the implementation of activities in the five municipalities. The PMU full-time staff will include: (i) National

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND MEDIUM PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 70

## Local Project Implementation Structure

### MCVOID and FAG-Benin will initially jointly form the Project Implementation Units at two levels: (i) the national level, with a Project Unit Office (PUO) to be established in Cotonou, under the supervision of MCVOID; and (ii) the Upper Ouémé and Middle Ouémé, part of the OCP region. The PUOs will comprise government staff members, whose capacity will be built up over time through training and mentoring by MCVOID and FAG-Benin. The PUOs will provide technical support to the government and the LRMU. This arrangement will ensure that (i) high quality technical standards are maintained; (ii) a strong focus is placed on project implementation, governance and results; and (iii) government partners play a leading role in project delivery and capacity development.

### Two PUOs will serve as operational arms of the PMU, located in the Upper and Middle Ouémé, each headed by a Project Unit Director (PUD). The PUDs will be responsible for implementing projects at the local level under the supervision of MCVOID. Each PUD is headed by a Project Technical Director (PTD). They are recruited based on a selection process that includes technical expertise and experience in project implementation, as well as familiarity with the Focal Point in each municipality. The location of the PUO offices will be jointly identified in coordination with the Focal Points.

### Each PUD will have a team of staff to support its operations. This team may include:

- **LPU Staff:** which will be capacitated under Component 3 – and project subject matter specialists (e.g., EE, Service Provider, etc.) who are recruited based on their technical expertise and experience.
- **LPUF Staff:** which will be in charge of stakeholder coordination and monitoring of the LRMU.

The LPUFs will be “attached” to the OCP platform (set up under Component 3). LPUFs will be in charge of stakeholder coordination, monitoring of the LRMU, and reporting on project implementation progress and results. The LPUF may procure services of NGOs/CBOs or specialized structures with the necessary expertise to implement specific projects. The LPUF will also be responsible for coordinating the activities of the Upper Ouémé Basin Authority (OBA) will also be involved once operational, but through the OCP platform and the project steering committee.

### Project Steering Committee

The PUDs will follow the guidance and recommendation in the PMU. The primary functions of the PSG will be:

- (i) aligning project activities with GbG policies and priorities.
- (ii) ensuring coordination of the project among different stakeholders, including government partners and community members.
- (iii) approving annual work plans and budget, and reviewing project progress; and
- (iv) guiding the resolution of implementation issues. The PSG will meet at least once a month to review project progress and challenges with the EEIs and on an ad-hoc basis, to discuss key oversight and/or implementation issues. The Chair will have the responsibility for convening meetings and chairing them. Minutes from each meeting will be prepared and circulated to all Committee members and project stakeholders. They will also be posted on the OCP Platform site and the Focal Points will be responsible for disseminating information to their respective communities.

### Local Government Structure

Each municipality will assign a Project Focal Point, based within the municipality offices, to oversee and monitor the implementation of projects in that municipality. The Focal Point will liaise with the Mayor and local staff members from the local Agriculture, Water and Environment Technical Departments, as well as farmers organizations and other stakeholders at the community level. The Focal Points will be responsible for:

- (i) ensuring close coordination between the PUO and the LRMU;
- (ii) identifying and engaging government partners and among commune partners; (iii) monitoring project implementation at municipal level, identify problems and challenges, and provide feedback to the PUO;
- (iv) reviewing AWPB and progress projects at municipal level. The Focal Points will liaise closely with the PMU and LRMU to ensure that they are able to provide support when necessary or overcome any challenges on the ground.

### International/ National Technical Assistant Specialist

The project will recruit long-term and short-term international Chief Technical Advisor (CTA) and consultants, as well as national technical assistants. The CTA will be responsible for leading with FAG-led technical departments and for capacity development of EE and Service Provider staff. The CTA will also be responsible for the following positions: (i) Project Engineer; (ii) FFS Specialist; (iii) Capacity Development Specialist; (iv) Agroforestry Value Chains and Agribusiness

## B

Development Specialist, and/or GIS Monitoring Specialist, International Technical specialists, such as a Lead Safeguard Advisor (LSA) from FAO will be contracted from Rome and/or around the world on either a long-term or short-term basis to provide technical assistance in areas of expertise. The LSA will also be responsible for training, technical materials and on-trainings for FSN and/or CRA. ### OCCI Platform

Under Component 3, the OCCI Platform will be established. Platform participants will be selected according to their criteria for participation in the platform. The selection process will be based on a competitive tendering process with collaboration with food value chain actors. The OBA, MAEP and MCVD, local communities, CSOs and community leaders will also be invited as stakeholders to participate in the platform. The platform will serve as an information dissemination. In addition, the Ministry of Finance, CBFU, FNEC and GNFI and FAO will be invited to join the platform; more specifically, the Ministry of Finance will provide financial resources for the platform. The OCCI Platform will also be sought to increase the resources available to ENF. The platform will be a forum for developing public-private partnerships and other innovative financing mechanisms. It is expected that several pilot projects and initiatives and players will be identified in the inception phase together with the specific approaches and tools that will be used to implement them. A project management unit (PMU) will be established by key partners and ministries, and a financial strategy developed to sustain the OCCI platform beyond the project.

### Flow of Funds

For the funded entity (AE) will receive the funds from the GCF at HQ level. The Government of Benin, acting through MCVD, will be an executing entity to whom FAO will transfer funds through the applicable modality, which would be either a direct payment or a letter of credit. The AE will be responsible for ensuring that there are regular spot checks and audits to ensure financial management, procurement and other management requirements are met in accordance with GCF rules and procedures.

MCVD has been assessed in order to gauge its handling capacity and a mitigation plan has been agreed upon to address any identified weaknesses including the activity co-financing.

FAO, The Host Country and FNEC will be responsible for ensuring project financial expenditures against budgets and execute payments. Payments received under GCF proceeds through funds transfer in accordance with FAO procurement rules are organizations involved in the Project implementation as procured parties. Procured parties will be contracted in accordance with FAO rules and regulations of procuring goods and services (e.g. FAO Manual Section 507).

### Agreements

A grant is a project agreement (to be considered as subsidiary agreement) with the Host Country, covering: i) The Host Country obligations; ii) The role of the Host Country acting through MCVD and MAEP for the implementation of the Project; iii) The role of FAO in implementing the Project. The Project will be implemented by the Host Country, acting through one or more Ministries, who will sign the Project Agreement on behalf of the Government of Benin. The Project Agreement will be signed between the Host Country and FAO. This agreement will also be a subsidiary agreement.

### B. Grant Amount (max 100 million US dollars, approximately 2 pages)

A grant of USD 435 795 is sought by the GCF to invest in climate change adaptation and enable a paradigm shift towards ICRM implemented in Benin’s basin area.

Shifts in rainfall patterns will not be addressed through government expenditure alone. As a low middle income country, Benin has limited resources available for adaptation and mitigation measures to address the impacts of climate change. The proposed project will provide technical assistance from FAO and support MCVD to work with FAO to understand how climate change scenarios are going to affect ecosystems in water management systems. This will be done through a participatory approach involving stakeholders at all levels, which will be instrumental for enhancing the climate resilience of vulnerable communities and their ecosystems in the Upper and Middle Benin River Basin. The project will also provide technical assistance from FAO to support the Government with approximately USD 1 billion, of which USD 8 billion needs to come from conditional shareholding. Apart from its revenues from the port of Cotonou (which represents the most of Benin’s GDP), the GCF budget is heavily dependent on

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 72

## Benin has already benefited from a USD readiness program on GCF[1] that has helped strengthen national coordination and institutional capacity for mainstreaming climate change in national policies and accessing climate finance.

The prospect of GCF support has led to a commitment by the GoB to allocate USD 80 million to the Green Climate Fund (GCF) as its contribution to the fund. This will enable the resources from these stakeholders into a program with the outreach and potential impact offered by this project.

### Benin’s readiness for climate change adaptation

The country has made significant progress in building capacity, including through the GCF readiness program. The Gross National Income per capita (purchasing power parity) for 2018 is USD 879, and approximately 50% of the population lives below the poverty line. The majority of the population resides in rural areas and about 10% of its population are undernourished. The vulnerability of rural households is further compounded by their dependence on agriculture for livelihoods.

In the context of a changing climate, the country faces significant challenges. In the most likely scenario, the GCF funding will support the incremental climate change cost gaps borne by smallholder farmers to ensure that they can continue to produce food and other goods in the face of climate change impacts. This will help address some of the constraints into an opportunity to provide evidence of how a decentralized program initiative such as OCEM can be provided with additional resources for the Middle and Lower Ouémé Basin and motivate increases in budgetary allocation for adaptation measures in the future.

GCF support will be instrumental for establishing climate-resilient land-use planning and management as the central element of the project. The project will provide a framework for integrating climate change into national planning. By providing an opportunity to incentivize and scale up the measures to be implemented through the OCEM program, the project will also help to ensure that these measures are integrated in the broader development agenda towards resilient, inclusive development.

Given the GoB’s ongoing process of decentralization, the project also provides a unique opportunity for the country to mainstream climate change adaptation into regional and local strategies and plans.

The project will be implemented through a multi-stakeholder approach bringing together cross-sectoral landscape-level climate-resilient land planning initially across 5 municipalities with potential to replicate across the entire Ouémé Basin. The project will also provide an opportunity for the GoB to scale up its efforts in this area, as municipalities will provide a model for adoption in other parts of the country. Improved planning at the regional and local level will help ensure that climate change adaptation is integrated into land-use plans and strategies.

A holistic management approach is promoted that takes into consideration the inter-connectedness of ecosystems and human activities.

### A robust financial strategy will be developed for FNCI to crowd-in public financing and mobilize community-level investments for climate-resilient land use. Government co-financing in the project (under Component 3) demonstrates a commitment by the GoB to support the development and implementation of innovative projects, and support, encouragement and demonstration activities to encourage GCFs and farmers to invest in improved agricultural practices.

However, the most significant challenge is that farmers are not able to access credit or other financial instruments to access finance and invest in climate-resilient interventions (see barriers, presented in the project TCO). FNCI’s role will be to provide a platform for the mobilization of resources from various sources including public funds, value chains, and strengthen coordination with the public sector, farmers’ unions and agribusinesses. Access to microcredits is critical as it will enable smallholder farmers to invest in climate-resilient interventions.

Through FNCI, innovative funding will be sought to further support ecosystem restoration. Pathways to replenish the degraded land through afforestation and reforestation are also being explored. The results of the financial and economic analysis for this project also show that the project investments in capacity building, wider dissemination of climate-resilient agricultural practices, and the establishment of a robust institutional framework and mobilization of players across the agricultural value chains have the potential to leverage additional investments in climate change adaptation.

GCF support will contribute to increase FNCI’s capacity as Benin’s DAE. Under Component 3, FNCI’s capacity to access innovative funding for adaptation in the Ouémé Basin will be enhanced. First, the project will seek to increase FNCI’s capacity by providing training and technical assistance on how to access climate finance. Second, capacity building for FNCI will focus on: assessing projects that are submitted to access financial resources based on several relevant criteria; supporting the implementation of successful projects; and to disbursing funds and ensure fiduciary management, and enhancing FNCI’s institutional framework.

## Building capacity in CRA, finance and marketing

OCHA will promote the implementation of CRA and agroforestry, supported by hybrid and旱地作物，that will be easy to establish and maintain. # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 74

Economic competitiveness is achieved through the project's support for viable, cost-effective climate-resilience measures that provide tangible benefits. As shown in the financial analysis (Annex A), all of the proposed investments related to the project will be economically viable and generate positive returns on investment. The project will ensure a continued use of project-promoted farming, water management and agro-forestry models are modest and can be covered by the project's own cash flow. The project will also provide an additional income stream through the sale of a low-cost phone App, FarmTree, will enable bankable, climate-resilient farm planning in farms, which combines the best practices from the project’s agro-forestry models with the latest information technology (IT) tools. This will result in cost-benefit estimation on the market value of agricultural products. Finally, investments in CGIIM in Upper and Middle Ouéoué will be supported by the World Bank, while investments in CGIIM in Lower Ouéoué and the Benin Upstream will further be supported by inviting authorities from other Benin’s basin areas to observe project impacts and introduce CGIIM (through CGIIM platforms and financial facilitity), as well as offer them training on CGIIM approach for watershed areas.

Finally, the project has mobilized cofunding from the GCF ($30.000.000), FNEC ($12.039.584), FNEC and MAEP ($7.632.280).

## 3. An innovative multi-stakeholder platform providing solid roots for OCIRI

The OCIRI platform will be key to institutionalizing project interventions, through a wide multi-stakeholder coordination mechanism that will ensure the sustainability of CGIIM investments and activities. The platform will provide a wide communication strategy and an innovative financial strategy for FNEC (see p. 6 for details). The project’s reforestation and waterworks activities with CGIIM will be supported by the OCIRI platform, which will also facilitate the dissemination of CGIIM information, and fund mobilize from the GGB and through FNEC. Long-term investments from public and private funds will be further supported by the OCIRI platform. The OCIRI platform will also provide a wide range of services to CGIIM users such as the FarmTree App. The OCIRI platform’s online portal will support the dissemination of this information, packaged into interactive modules, which will be available on CGIIM’s online portal and will be updated regularly. These modules are already available on OCIRI’s online portal. This will support project uptake in the whole Ouéoué basin or other basins. Finally, regular meetings with CGIIM users will be organized to discuss the operational platform and its achievements, in order to motivate replication.

## 4. Support to climate-resilient value chains to strengthen and create income streams

The CGIIM investments in Upper and Middle Ouéoué basin will contribute towards CRA and agroforestry to enhance ecosystem services and increase agricultural productivity. One of the interventions to achieve this is the diversification of crops – a key element for increasing farm resilience, as it provides multiple benefits: provide shade and restore fertility. In addition, the project has carefully selected tree species (e.g., mango, shea, cocoa, etc.) that are well adapted to climate change conditions in the Ouéoué basin. The selection process was based on a local, national and international market demand. The selection of tree species has been informed by a market analysis conducted with CGIIM farmers. This diversification will also contribute towards increasing farm income streams through the diversification of agriculture – crops and agroforestry – towards high-value products, combined with building capacity to process these products into value-added goods. These investments in CGIIM will be supported by FNEC, thereby generating new income streams that can be re-invested in project interventions. Sale agreements between project beneficiaries and private sector actors have been signed for the sale of cocoa beans, which are aligned with, and supported by MAEP since early 2000, with the view to increase quality, efficiency and profits within CGIIM. The project will also support CGIIM farmers to diversify their income streams through the development of value chains; loans can be used to tackle current issues linked to the poor quality of agro-products, limited harvesting capacity, lack of storage facilities, lack of access to markets, lack of appropriate processing equipment, inadequate tools and labour to strengthen the production, storage and sale links of the selected value chains.

## 5. A financial strategy for FNEC to leverage funds from public and private institutions

By facilitating CGIIM investments in crops diversifying agricultural activities, increasing the market value of agro-products, and strengthening their value-chains through improved post-harvest processing and business, the project will attract private sector actors to invest in CGIIM. The financial strategy for FNEC will ensure that all CGIIM projects’ benefits from the project will be rigorously monitored; captured; compiled and disseminated as knowledge products, which will be used by CGIIM farmers to improve their livelihoods. CGIIM’s investments will also generate carbon credits to beneficiary farmers. The strengthened financial strategy of FNEC under Component 3 of the project will ensure long-term investments in climate resilient agricultural development, while at the same time reducing climate

change vulnerable to climate change in Benin’s main basins. FNEC has already committed fund during the project, to support 3 micro-projects that contribute to climate change adaptation in the Oueme Basin. A roadmap to ensure the facility's replenishment is being produced and a full-scale funding proposal will be developed with FNEC to ensure a first replenishment of the fund.

Finally, the project will leverage opportunity to be replicated in other basins of Benin. Indeed Benin include 4 basins: Oueme, Mono, Pendjari and Niger. These four basins are transboundary and have been identified as priority for multi-country. Authorities of these three additional basins will be invited to attend training sessions on ICIRM approach to climate change adaptation in the Oueme Basin. They will also receive technical assistance from FNEC to support their project preparation, implementation and monitoring.
