D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This document outlines the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Total Investment Framework.

### D.1 Project Objectives (approximately 3 pages)

The project aims to:
- Envision increasing climate change resilience, enhancing food security and improving livelihoods and income streams in the Ouémé Basin in Benin.
- Contribute to the achievement of the GCF’s objectives: reducing poverty and vulnerability; promoting sustainable development; and increasing sustainable, climate resilient development. Several GCF result areas will also be targeted, namely:
  - Most vulnerable people, communities and regions
  - Health and well-being, food and water security
  - Ecosystems and ecosystem services
  - Sustainable land use, reforestation and forest management

#### 1. Most vulnerable people, communities and regions

The OCRI project will be implemented in Benin. According to the UNDP Multidimensional Poverty Index, 60% of the population lives below the poverty line. This high level of multidimensional poverty is a major barrier to human development for the country as a whole, makes vulnerability to climate change particularly acute and constrains people’s adaptive capacity and ability to be resilient. The project will focus on the Upper Ouémé Basin (UOB) and Lower Ouémé Basin (LOB), which are home to 50% of the population in the 3 municipalities it targets.

The communities in the central and northern departments of Benin are particularly vulnerable due to their high poverty levels, low access to basic services, and limited access to land. The majority of these populations depend on agriculture for their livelihoods. During the past three decades, these regions have been affected by floods on several occasions. Intensification of rainfall patterns has led to increased flooding in the UOB and LOB. In addition to floods, crops are also negatively affected by increased air temperatures. As a result of this, the yields of maize and beans have decreased significantly in flood-prone areas. Climate change impacts will further reduce productivity in agricultural landscapes and forest ecosystems.

#### 2. Health and well-being, food and water security

The project will implement an approach combining climate-resilient agricultural practices, agroforestry, and waterworks, which will expose income to climate change, improve access to food and water for the most vulnerable people living in the target areas, and reduce poverty levels among these populations of the Ouémé Basin (UOB and LOB) (GADeC 2013). Indeed, the revitalization of riverbanks and waterworks will increase water availability during dry seasons. This will be particularly beneficial for women (who are farmers), women’s economic empowerment, and food security in the UOB and LOB. Moreover, water availability will be improved in the target areas through soft and hard waterworks that will provide access to clean drinking water and reduce the time spent by women and children (who are mostly women) on water collection.

In addition, the project will improve access to irrigation resources for 10,325 farmers (of whom 67% are women), empowering the population of the selected 5 municipalities living in the basin area – see FIS. This will increase food security and reduce poverty levels among these populations by increasing water availability to irrigated agriculture (including counter-seasons) in a context where droughts are more intense and frequent, and by improving access to irrigation resources for women farmers who have limited access to resources and 1320 ha from MAFOP financing). This will result in reduced poverty and food insecurity in the Upper Ouémé Basin and Lower Ouémé Basin.

#### 3. Ecosystems and ecosystem services

The proposed ICRM approach of the project (which will affect 65,000 ha of land) considers the interactions between climate change impacts on water, crops and population, from upstream to downstream areas. It aims to combine interventions in agriculture, agroforestry and waterworks to reduce vulnerability to climate change while reducing climate causes of ecosystem degradation. As a result, ecosystem functions are restored and enhanced, thereby protecting the population against climate change impacts, while at the same time ensuring the production of goods and services.

Food insecurity is especially prevalent in Middle Ouémé, affecting 10 to 20% households – and over 20% in Glazoué. See WFP: Analyse Globale de la Vulnérabilité et de la Sécurité Alimentaire (AGVSa), République du Benin, 2017.

services that enhance agricultural production. These soft adaptation interventions are combined with small water infrastructure to ensure water capture, flow and recharge, as well as the sustainable management to enable agricultural sustainability in the Upper and Middle Quinnie Basin. The project also includes a component of ecosystem restoration and protection, allowing for sustain crop-production while restoring the ecological functions that deliver critical services.

The watershed approach strengthens the basin's ability to maintain its functions (stabilizing soils through vegetation, storing water, reducing erosion) and to adapt to climate change by increasing resilience in the Upper and Middle Quinnie Basin according to the characteristics of the agro-ecological zones. The up-taking of climate resilient agricultural practices and technologies will increase food security and water services especially indirectly benefit a large part of the population residing in the lower basin areas.

### 4. Sustainable land use, reforestation and forest management

Although the project proposal focuses on adaptation, its interventions will bring additional impact and benefits via co-benefits. The project will contribute to the implementation of sustainable land and water management, reforestation and climate-resilient agriculture under Component 1. These interventions will enhance the ability of communities in the Upper and Middle Quinnie Basin to adapt to climate change by increasing resilience in the basin according to the characteristics of the agro-ecological zones. The up-taking of climate resilient agricultural practices and technologies will increase food security and water services especially indirectly benefit a large part of the population residing in the lower basin areas.

The benefits of the project will enable climate change adaptation, risk prevention and protection of lives, assets and livelihoods; increased resilience to climate change; improved access to markets for agricultural products; reduced vulnerability of women and men living in the Upper and Middle Quinnie Basin, resulting from reduced vulnerability to climate change and enhanced knowledge and understanding of climate change impacts on their livelihoods. The project will also contribute to gender equality by empowering women and men, women. The project design team used national and locally available statistics as a secondary source of information to determine the number of people living in the basin areas that are directly dependent on agriculture as main activity. This information was combined with information obtained through field visits, interviews and consultations to develop the project proposal.

Direct beneficiaries are people living in the basin areas of five target municipalities, and who rely directly on agriculture as main activity. They will benefit from: i) the installation of waterworks to protect against floods and droughts; ii) the establishment of climate-resilient agricultural practices and technologies that include soil conservation, land restoration and management to enhance the production of goods and services, protect against climate change impacts and ensure food security in the Upper and Middle Quinnie Basin; iii) increased access to credit facilities for smallholder farmers through strengthening of the Ouamie Main Project (SDAGE), revised Local Development Plans and improved capacity to manage resources at the local level; iv) enhanced access to inputs in their climate-resilient farms; and v) an enhanced understanding of climate change impacts and adaptation strategies.

Indirect beneficiaries will benefit from: i) protection against climate change impacts, in particular through reduced occurrence of floods through waterworks and reforestation on river banks in Upper and Middle Quinnie; ii) additional job opportunities for women and men living in the basin areas as a result of increased agricultural productivity; iii) improved capacity to manage resources at the local level; iv) increased awareness about climate change adaptation, through the awareness raising interventions supported by the project; and v) strengthened governance structures that will ensure sustainable use of natural resources. The project will also contribute to poverty reduction through strengthening of the Ouamie Main Project (SDAGE), revised Local Development Plans and improved capacity to manage resources at the local level.

### D.2 Project shift paradigm (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Without the proposed project, low yields, food insecurity and high poverty in the Upper and Middle Quinnie Basin, as well as the lack of access to markets for agricultural products, are major constraints on sustainable livelihoods and agriculture, drive the local communities towards unsustainable practices, which undergo the vicious cycle represented in Figure 3. The basin areas are characterized by a semi-arid climate with low rainfall variability. In addition, Upper and Middle Quinnie are increasingly vulnerable to floods and flash floods especially in Middle Quinnie, water scarcity exacerbating food insecurity and poverty.

The project will affect a paradigm shift that results in enhanced climate resilience, low carbon and improved livelihoods for smallholders in the Quinnie Basin. This will be achieved through three complementary components (described in its 3).

The whole project is underpinned by an innovative, integrated approach for basin management, which will not only improve water resources management but also contribute to poverty reduction and sustainable development of the basin development projects – but also considers the rippling effects of climate change from upstream to downstream basin

**Ouémé Basin Climate Change Adaptation Strategy (OBCAS)**

Creation of an enabling environment: The project’s low carbon KPI approach will transform current approaches which are largely restricted to single sectors and do not promote resilient basin management to reduce the vulnerability of small-scale farmers. The OCB project is promoting a shift away from conventional, short production cycles that are dependent on rainfall for irrigation, fertilizers, pesticides, seeds, energy, soil, land, and water, through the OCB platform; and with the private sector, to develop integrated solutions that include ecological agriculture, agroforestry, and sustainable land use practices. The project will also support the implementation of ecosystem restoration in basins to buffer climate change impacts and support local livelihoods is recognised as vital for the success of the project. By involving all stakeholders (government, civil society organizations, private sector, and farmers) in the Ouémé Basin Climate Change Adaptation Strategy (OBCAS), the project enables sustained participation of public and private stakeholders into its interventions. The OCB project will also support the development of a basin-wide, multi-sectoral, multi-stakeholder climate-related project by building on the Ouémé Basin Climate Change Adaptation Strategy (OBCAS) to promote integrated water resources management and sustainable land use practices that are climate-resilient and/or protect vulnerable ecosystems while making small-scale farmers climate resilient. The financial resources from FNEC will be used for the development of a basin-wide, multi-sectoral, multi-stakeholder, climate-related project through public and private sector investments.

The OCB project will create an enabling environment for adaptation innovations and market development in selected, climate-resilient value chains. ## Focus on market development for value-added products from agriculture will ensure that livelihood interventions are sustainable.

- **Contribution to the regulatory frameworks and policies:** The project will contribute to Benin’s regulatory framework and policy environment at all levels. The Ministry of Agriculture and Extension (MAE) in target municipalities will be fully involved in the coordination and implementation of the activities, through the Local Project Implementation Team (LPI). The National Forest Service (SNSF) and the Department of Environment and Forestry (DENFOR) will receive trainings on ICFM for basin areas, CRA and wetwaters. They will be involved in the implementation of the project’s activities related to land use planning and forest management.
- **Support to the development of a climate change adaptation strategy:** The project will support the development of a national climate change adaptation strategy (NCCAS) at the national level. Moreover, the SDAGE will be strengthened to ensure the promotion of an ICFM approach, that considers climate change impacts on land use and water resources in the basin areas.
- **Support to the implementation of the Benin National Development Plan:** The project will support the implementation of the Benin National Development Plan (PNDB) for the period 2017–2021. It will contribute to the development of a national climate change adaptation strategy, based on the PNDB, and will provide technical assistance in the preparation of sectoral action plans.
- **Support to the implementation of the Benin INDC:** The project is fully aligned with and supports the implementation of Benin’s INDC – through which Benin commits to implement adaptation action in three priority economic sectors: agriculture, forestry and fisheries. It will contribute to the development of a national climate change adaptation strategy (NCCAS) at the national level.
- **Support to the implementation of the SDAGE:** The project is fully aligned with and supports the implementation of the SDAGE – which commits Benin to implement adaptation action in three priority economic sectors: agriculture, forestry and fisheries. It will contribute to the development of a national climate change adaptation strategy (NCCAS) at the national level.
- **Support to the implementation of the project’s components:** The project is fully aligned with and supports the implementation of the project’s components – which are focused on improving livelihoods in the basin areas through improved land use, water security and food security. It will contribute to the development of a national climate change adaptation strategy (NCCAS) at the national level.

The project fully supports the implementation of SDG 2 in Benin, in particular SDG 2.1: End hunger; achieve food security and improved nutrition; SDG 3: Ensure healthy lives and promote well-being; and SDG 13: Take urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts.

Economic benefits: The ICFM approach will be implemented at the basin, through a mix of soft and hard climate resilient interventions (intercropping, agricultural diversification, increased tree planting of agroforestry species, improved water management practices, etc.). These interventions will contribute to increase food security in the basin areas by making them more productive. It will also restore land coverage and quality through agriculture and reduce soil erosion; improve environmental services such as carbon sequestration, biodiversity conservation, water retention, enhance biodiversity, contribute to reduced deforestation through improving income streams and food security for farmers, and provide ecosystem services (e.g., improved water availability). The project will focus on Section B.11, and enhance water reserves and availability. The project will intensify the provision of ecosystem services by increasing tree cover in the basin areas, which will contribute to increased carbon sequestration and fresh foods in the whole Ouémé Basin. More precisely, overall reduction of soil erosion will pass over 95,000 ha.

Social co-benefits: The project will contribute to improve well-being, health and save lives with up to 6 million people living in the basin areas (e.g., through improved water security for drinking; reduced food insecurity; increased access to Middle Ouémé; and reducing floods, flash foods and water scarcity risks in the whole Ouémé Basin. Moreover, it will contribute to improve women’s empowerment by providing them with new opportunities for income generation and training; and allowing further income-generating or household activities. Improved access to financial services (e.g., microfinance) is expected to increase the number of women involved in agriculture and provide a value addition in a number of food value chains. Local communities will have better access to water for agricultural and domestic use, which will contribute to improved health conditions and reduced mortality rates. The project has potential to increase the country’s exports. The project has an estimated economic rate of return of about 20%. All A1, D10, Discount Rate it has a Net Present Value of USD 45 million, and costs per beneficiary of about USD 330.

### D.4 Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### Vulnerability

Benin's total population now stands at 13 million people, 32% of which live in rural areas, according to World Bank figures. According to the UNDP-Multiplication Poverty Report, 68.5 percent of Benin's population lives in poverty. The low status in terms of human development for the country as a whole makes vulnerability to climate change particularly acute. The project area is located in the Upper and Middle Ouémé Basin. The total number of small poor farmers in 15 municipalities in the Upper and Middle Ouémé Basin meet. Most of the households are engaged in agriculture, with cocoa being the main cash crop. However, the majority of these crops were particularly serious in the communes of Dzougué, Glazoué and Coupang. The majority of the population in 5 municipalities is vulnerable to climate change. These communities have a high vulnerability index for their exposure to climate change. Rising temperatures, erratic and extreme rainfall events are major threats to livelihoods. Due to these climatic conditions, the project area has experienced significant crop losses over the past few years. Crops were severely damaged by the floods, with agricultural losses of -70% in some cases. In addition, to floods, crops are also affected by drought and pests. The main cash crops grown in Benin are cocoa, cotton, palm oil and other staple crops in Benin – have already decreased in the project area. Climate-change impacts will further reduce production capacity.

#### Financial, economic, social and institutional needs

Benin is a Low Income developing country in terms of human development, ranking 163 out of the world's 198 countries*. The government debt-to-GDP ratio has been increasing over the past decade, and now stands at 41.5%. Despite this, the government has not yet received any funding from international financial institutions (IFIs) or donors in Benin for grant funding to finance technical assistance, training and other investments needed to change skills, knowledge and practices of farmers and other stakeholders in order to adapt to climate change and its impacts of climate change. Yet neither the domestic private sector, government nor multilateral financial institutions are currently in a position to provide the necessary resources to support the development of the project area or to build resilience of these vulnerable rural communities in Upper and Middle Ouémé Basin, with additional benefits in the Upper Ouémé Basin. The GCF will allocate more than 15 percent of its project resources for capacity building and technology transfer to farmers and the organizations working with them such as FFS, input suppliers, credit providers, etc., and other related areas.

* UNDP Human Development Report 2019.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 85

### D. Country ownership (max. 260 words, approximately 1 page)

The project is in line with the Green Climate Fund (GCF) Adaptation Strategy, the adaptation measures of the INDCs emphasize food and water security via implementation of climate-resilient practices and technologies. The activities proposed are consistent with the GCF’s Strategic Plan 2014-2017 (SP14-17), the GCF Country Program Document (CPD) and PSDGA 2015 and PSNANAS 2017 - 2025. Interest in the project has also been demonstrated by the support of the Ministry of Environment, Energy and Natural Resources (MENR) and other stakeholders through various initiatives that are synergistic and could be scaled up. The Ouham watershed Masterplan (SIAGE, 2013), envisions a landscape approach to climate change adaptation in the Ouham Basin. This project will contribute towards better land management, similar to the implementation of climate resilient practices promoted through this project; an agro-pastoral system that is more sustainable and resilient to climate change. The project will promote adoption of practices and the promotion of sustainable livelihoods of climate-resilient agro-pastoal pastoral systems.

- restoration of agricultural land as planned through the dissemination of integrated land and water practices;
- integration of an adaptive approach in the development plans and capacity development, aligned with government priorities to support community resilience;

The project has been developed in response to a request from MEEP in 2017, together with key stakeholders, including INGOs, NGOs, local communities, and other relevant actors. The project is consistent with the activities mentioned above on the management and implementation of the project as outlined in the Feasibility Study. The NDA is fully aligned with the GCF’s Strategic Plan 2014-2017 (SP14-17).

The Ouham Basin Agency (OBA) will join the private-sector platform as main partner. OBA is a public scientific and social institution under the supervision of the Water Directorate. The mission of the Agency is to contribute to the sustainable management of water resources in the Ouham Basin, by ensuring the rational use of natural resources of Ouham Basin. It is responsible for: managing financial, material and human resources; managing water resources; implementing projects related to water resources; supervising the implementation of works; disbursing and repatriations of accounts; managing supplies and contracts; processing salaries and other expenses related to the management of water resources.

FNEC's capacity to design and manage climate change-related projects will be enhanced. FNEC is Benin’s National Accredited Entity for the AF and the GCF. Through OCHR-DCPI, FNEC will receiving capacity building support from the GCF. The project will also contribute to the development of a national framework for adaptation by leveraging additional resources through increased capacity to design and manage bankable funding proposal that will be submitted to the GCF.

The project design phase consisted of an intensive stakeholder consultation process including FFIC consultation with indigenous communities, transparent communication and easy access to relevant information. The consultations ensured that all stakeholders were informed about the project’s objectives, activities, expected results at national and municipal levels. Via the OCHR platform (see Component 3 in the project description) stakeholder consultations will continue throughout implementation.

### E. Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The main objective of the project called “Ouham Climate Resilient Initiative” (OCRi) is to promote the climate resilient practices and technologies that are already being implemented in the Ouham Basin in Benin. The project intends to enable the adoption and dissemination of adaptation and mitigation practices and technologies, to strengthen low emission development strategies (LEDS), and to contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals for climate-resilient management in the Ouham Basin. On-the-ground interventions in CIRA, agroforestry and waters are already being implemented by various actors including INGOs, NGOs, local communities and other relevant actors (see Section 7). Such practices are proven climate change adaptation and mitigation technologies to enhance high value crops production, diversify income streams under climate change. The project will promote use of an innovative financing mechanism that will provide financial incentives for farmers to adopt climate-resilient crop and tree planning while at the same time providing cost benefits estimates to farmers.

However, the project does not have the financial and technical capacity, nor the knowledge, to implement the proposed interventions and initiate a paradigm shift toward resilient integrated management in the Guinean Basin. The main reason for this is that the costs of implementing these interventions are very high. In addition, the costs borne by municipalities are the high cost of investments, the difficult mobilization of financial resources from public and private sources, and the lack of capacity to implement the proposed interventions. These factors make it impossible to disseminate proven low carbon climate-resilient practices and technologies, and lead farmers to generate additional income in a sustainable manner.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 88

#### Replicability
- **Medium**

There are however some CRAs being promoted that are currently promoted in the Oueme Basin. These practices will be replicated by the project as part of the ICRM approach, to ensure adaptation and climate change impact reduction benefits for the downstream population in the Oueme Basin.

This project will demonstrate the benefits of an ICRM approach applied to the Oueme Basin. The project will combine with other measures for such infrastructure exchange visits for farmers and extension officers, to assess impacts, and institutional strengthening will be provided to local communities/sites, and of ICRM in other basins (and possibly cross border basins).

The CRA interventions will be informed by methods that were collected during the field research. The project will combine with other measures for such infrastructure exchange visits for farmers and extension officers, to assess impacts, and institutional strengthening will be provided to local communities/sites, and of ICRM in other basins (and possibly cross border basins).

The CRA approach has not been implemented yet in the Oueme Basin areas within and outside of Benin.

## Risks in Middle and Lower Zambezi Province

### Mitigation Measures

- **Tree Planting:** Conduct technical and environmental assessments to identify the best site for the micro-dams and tree nurseries.
- **Tree Nurseries:** Establish tree nurseries and provide seedlings to produce high-quality seeds.
- **Seedling Production:** Produce 20,000 of quality seeds in seed banks and field nurseries.
- **Tree Planting:** Plant 4,000 ha of trees in Gwazulu, using the Micro-Aquifer Replenishment Project (MAP).
- **Community Involvement:** The managed tree nursery will be fully owned by the community to accommodate 15,000 trees/year.

- **Participatory Approaches:** All trainees are trained on gender-responsive and participatory approaches.

### Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)
#### Project monitoring and reporting
In accordance with the GCF project supervision team will oversee and supervise the implementation of this project in accordance with the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) signed between FAO and the GCF. As per the GCF Monitoring and Accountability Framework, and in accordance with the AMA, the project will be subject to an independent internal evaluation by the GCF Evaluation Office at the end of the project, a Project Closeout Report and an Independent Final Evaluation report. FAO will also provide semi-annual and annual Financial Reports throughout project implementation.

#### Evaluation
In accordance with the AMA between FAO and GCF, the FAO Office of Evaluation will be responsible for the independent internal and final evaluations for OCRM.
The evaluation will use a learning-driven approach and according to the GCF evaluation criteria. The interim evaluation will be instrumental in contributing - through operational and strategic recommendations - to improve implementation, setting out any necessary corrective measures for the remaining period of the project. The evaluation should also assess the extent to which the project has contributed towards poverty reduction, food security, environmental sustainability, gender equality, innovation results as areas, but also sustainability and scalability of results, and lessons learned. The evaluation should also assess the extent to which the project has contributed to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) in the Ouémé Basin, with a particular focus on climate-resilient sustainable development in Benin. The evaluations will draw on different evaluation methods and tools. In addition to primary data collected by the evaluators, secondary data from other sources such as project documents, reports, and databases will be used.
The final evaluation report is expected to be completed within 12 months after the end of the project. A total amount of USD500 000 has been allocated for this purpose. The costs associated with the evaluation will be paid to the disaggregation of data, results and outcomes by gender. The project will include an impact evaluation.
A budget of USD500 000 has been included to cover costs associated with the IE. Detailed design will be carried out during early implementation.


---
**Page 110**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.01 PAGE 187

Responsibility for project level monitoring will rest with a designated monitoring officer, located within the Project Coordination Unit. The PMU (established through the GCF) will be responsible for ensuring that the project is implemented in accordance with the approved implementation overview, including the implementation of the outputs, outcomes and impacts detailed in the results framework shown above in Section E Logical Framework. A dedicated monitoring officer will be appointed to oversee this process. Monitoring data will be compiled and displayed in a dedicated module of the project MIS to be developed and deployed by the PMU. To ensure regular monitoring on-the-ground measurements will be conducted by the PMU, supported by farmers and other stakeholders on the ground. This will contribute to ensuring project ownership. They will be equipped with smart phone that includes an innovative app FarmTree, under development by the PMU, for data collection and economic performance of project interventions. The project's M&E methodologies, procedures, systems and design will be established within the inception phase of the project.

Project monitoring officer will monitor AWPB implementation and the outputs that result from it, including those that are detailed in the results framework, on an on-going basis. They will use the data and information from this monitoring reported to them by project staff, project partners and beneficiaries to prepare the draft performance reports for submission to the PMU. The PMU will be responsible for ensuring that these reports are submitted to the GCF in a timely manner along with any other required documentation. The PMU will also be responsible for providing regular updates on progress of the project to the GCF. The FAC-GCF project supervision team will bear overall responsibility for ensuring that all relevant reporting is provided to the GCF in a timely manner and will provide guidance as necessary to ensure this happens. This will take place within the form of knowledge-sharing events and briefings between the PMU, PMU and PPU management teams.

# F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1 Weak collaboration between stakeholders

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

#### Description
Weak collaboration between stakeholders from different governmental ministries and departments, and from the private sector may negatively affect project implementation by limiting information and knowledge sharing on climate resilience practices and technologies, and ultimately impacts the overall long-term project impact.

The GoB including the various stakeholders to be involved in the OCR multi-stakeholder platform have been engaged through a series of meetings. The OCR team has developed a draft charter for the OCR platform that have reviewed and validated the project. The OCR platform will be designed, based on extensive consultations with all relevant stakeholders, and will provide a common framework for collaboration among different responsibilities on the platform. Participation on the platform will be on a voluntary basis, but encouraged through the development of an incentive structure. The GoB has also engaged with the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock, FAO, which has experience in multi-stakeholder engagement processes, and this will ensure project ownership by both stakeholders and the OCR team. This will be further discussed during the next meeting.

### Selected Risk Factor 2 Quality and quality of water availability downstream

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | Medium |

#### Description
The project will increase access to water and improve groundwater water supply through rainwater harvesting systems, and micro-dams for small-scale irrigation systems and ground-water recharge. This might lead to higher consumption of water resources, possibly affecting downstream users.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
In light of increasing water scarcity due to climate-change hazards, the project will place a strong focus on water conservation measures such as rainwater harvesting and ground-water recharge infrastructures to ensure that groundwater is maintained and increased. Tree planting and soil improvement will result in increased water infiltration and reduced runoff. The impact of the project on downstream users in the Lower Kosi, Upper Kosi, and Middle Outen will be mitigated through trainings and awareness-raising of smallholder farmers on agricultural best practices and eco-agro-techniques/technologies. Mitigation measures will shift the risk potentially towards 'Low'.

### Selected Risk Factor 3 Women implication

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | Medium |

#### Description
Women may not have fair access to benefits resulting from project interventions.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
There is a risk that women will not have fair access to the project benefits, such as income from sale of crops, nuts and fruits. Women play a major role in agricultural production in Benin, but they often have limited access to land, and other resources necessary for their livelihoods. This may result in unequal distribution of project impacts. The risk of unequal access to project benefits will be mitigated by developing women-oriented training modules providing information on the project and its potential impact on women's lives. Women will also receive training equipment for processing and storage of high-value agroproducts. Women's groups will be trained on the processing

**Page 112**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 109

## Selected Risk Factor 4 Limited technical and operational capacity

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Medium |
| Description | Limited technical and operational capacity will reduce the efficiency and effectiveness of the project's implementation. |

### Mitigation Measures
- FAO experts are specialized on climate change adaptation in agroforestry and waterways, they will actively participate in project execution during the first years.
- While building capacity within the Gulf: If expertise is missing among local actors, it can be provided by other organizations such as UNDP, or through implementation Agencies or to cover gaps where national expertise is not available and to provide technical backstopping.

## Selected Risk Factor 5 Political instability and weak decentralization process

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Medium |

### Description
The West African region is increasingly being impacted by extremist groups that are slowly moving southwards from the Sahel. Benin has borders with four countries of all: Burkina Faso, Niger and Nigeria are experiencing increased extremist activity. The identified project municipalities are not in areas immediately threatened.

## Selected Risk Factor 6 Lack of market demands for agroproducts

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Low |

### Description
Demand for certain crops and NTFPs on local markets may not be strong enough to absorb the increased supply.

### Mitigation Measures
- There is a risk that the increased production of agroproducts will exceed the existing demand on local and regional markets. In the case of food crops, many farmers will use much of their increased production for their own consumption or sale in local markets.
- The project will ensure that the increased supply of certain crops and NTFPs will be absorbed by allowing farmers to store excess production beyond harvest season until market prices improve. For NTFPs, the project will ensure that the increased supply is sold on local and regional markets where such products will be profitable and that overall demand for these products is strong.
- In addition, consultations between farmers and traders will be conducted in order to identify the most appropriate markets for the increased supply can be sold on local and regional markets. A market analysis, conducted as part of the project development phase, identified key agro-products which will be supported by the project.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 3.0 / PAGE 110

## Selected Risk Factor 7 Impacts on transhuman

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Low |

### Description
The project could affect transhuman moves across Benin.

|Mitigation Measure(s)|
|---|

There is a risk that transhuman access to the land will be restricted by project interventions on reforestation and land management. This risk essentially concerns Zogobodomey, which economy is based on agriculture and livestock. The project will have to ensure that the rights of these communities are respected in the implementation of its activities in fields. The reforestation interventions that will take place on degraded men banks will be carefully located in areas that are not used by these communities for their agricultural activities. These communities will be involved through local communities, chiefs and authorities. They will be inclusive of the existing local dynamics, including known moves of pastoralists. The project will ensure that the rights of these communities are respected during implementation.

The government of Benin as well as the project’s municipalities are also implementing social policies that promote shared governance in areas where the project is implemented. These policies aim to strengthen the capacity of local authorities and these governmental institutions not to compete cooperatively, but rather emphasize complementarily between OCCI partners and other actors in project areas. This will be reflected in the local adaptation plans (or revised LDPs) support under Output 3.1 of the project.

## Selected Risk Factor 8 money laundering and countering the financing of terrorism

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Low |

### Description
Risks of money laundering and countering the financing of terrorism

The Government shall maintain its commitment as set out above and as maintained by the Security Council Committee established pursuant to its Resolutions 1267 (1999) and 1889 (2009); it is also committed to comply with all other relevant resolutions adopted under Chapter VII of the UN Charter. This provision must be included in all agreements that may be concluded with third parties for the implementation of activities.

# G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk management (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

Overall, the environmental and social impacts of the project will be positive. The project is expected to improve the natural resources and agricultural land upon which farmers work, based on improved, climate-resilient agricultural practices and technologies. These improvements in soil health and water management will positively impact ecosystems and will positively affect human health and well-being in the long run. Socially, the project will engage women and men equally in all aspects of the project activities. The project will provide training to women farmers on how to use women with agricultural skills and knowledge. Livelihoods are expected to improve, based on increased adaptive capacity and resilience of smallholder farming systems. Improved livelihoods will reduce poverty levels. Increased agricultural inputs used-on farm-and off-farm are expected to reduce some of the adverse impacts of climate change on smallholder farmers. The project will provide training sessions and activities in farmer field schools and similar forums under Component I to ensure that farmers are equipped with knowledge, skills, and technologies for improved livelihoods.

The Project has been classified as moderate risk (Category "B") and it is expected that the project activities will not have significant adverse environmental or social impacts. The project will be implemented within the boundaries of the following protected areas: ESS1 (Plant Genetic Resources for Food and Agriculture), ESS2 (Pest and Pest Management, ESS3 (Estuarine Coastal and Marine Areas), ESS4 (Terrestrial Biodiversity Conservation), ESS5 (Freshwater Biodiversity and Habitats). ESS6 (Plant Genetic Resources for Food and Agriculture), ESS7 (Pest and Pest Management, ESS8 (Estuarine Coastal and Marine Areas), ESS9 (Terrestrial Biodiversity Conservation), ESS10 (Freshwater Biodiversity and Habitats). For this reason is the inability in the labour market and presence of landless farmers in the project area will be included in the social risk assessment.

Overall, the cumulative project environmental and social impacts are expected to be positive, and generate series of opportunities for beneficiary communities, as the overall objective is to increase climate-resilient agricultural practices and technologies. The project will also contribute towards reducing poverty levels by providing training sessions and activities in farmer field schools and similar forums under Component I to ensure that farmers are equipped with knowledge, skills, and technologies for improved livelihoods.

To comply with the core requirements of these standards and applicable national regulations in Benin, given that all of the above-mentioned project components will be implemented within the boundaries of protected areas, a risk management plan (RMP) has been prepared. The RMP will follow during project implementation once the physical footprints of the project activities have been formulated.

During project implementation stage, once the physical footprints of sub-project activities are known, to ensure that the identified risks are managed effectively and in accordance with the GCF policies and standards, a detailed Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) has been prepared along with an environmental and social risk management plan (RMP). The ESMF will be reviewed by the Benin National Environment Agency (BENA) and the RMP will follow during project implementation once the physical footprints of the project activities have been formulated.

The implementation arrangements as they pertain specifically to environmental and social safeguards will be led by the Project Coordinator, in collaboration with the Environmental and Social Safeguards Officer (ESSO). The ESMF is currently being reviewed by a Senior Environmental and Social Safeguards Officer (SESSO) and at local level, a "floor" of an Environmental and Social Safeguards Officer (ESSO) will be established. At national level, the Project Management Unit (PMU) will lead the implementation of the ESMF. In addition to safeguarding and gender mainstreaming, the PMU has led the review of all project-related Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) processes at both the central and local levels, as detailed in this ESMF.

## G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The gender assessment reveals that women in the project intervention zone have less access to soil land, fertilizer, extension services, capital for investments in income-generating activities and to information and decision-making processes), and therefore are less resistant to climate change impacts than men. Women bear responsibility for most of


---
**Page 115**
---

The households focus with title assistance from men have low literacy levels and face mobility barriers. As a result they have less access to training and information.

Women have smaller plots (often on less fertile and more degraded land) and less access to tools and animal traction before project implementation. Women also have limited access to credit, inputs, extension services and training by extension services for assistance on use of fertilizer, improved agriculture techniques and irrigation technology. As a result they are unable to improve their productivity.

Women are major actors in post-harvest processing, distribution and sales activities, which are affected by climate change events; product quality is declining because of difficulties drying and storing produce. Women’s access to credit was weak and women were not able to access the market for their products. The project will provide training and responsive initiatives and in community development projects.

Women farmers have started NTFPs (Non-Timber Forest Products). In the project intervention zone they collect and process Shea nuts. Locust bean fruits and vegetables, all sources of income and a coping mechanism when other crops fail. Women are also involved in the production of honey and beekeeping. The project will provide training and responsive initiatives to women farmers who have started tree nurseries. NTFPs are important to women to overcome shocks, source fuelwood and food in response to climate change.

While men opt for migration when drought or floods destroy household livelihoods, women are more limited because of their responsibilities at home. Women are also the main caregivers during disasters and are responsible for coping with the post-disaster situation and take care of the household, generating enough income for food and the welfare of children. The project will provide training and responsive initiatives to women who have been affected by documented. Increased conflicts concerning access to pastures, water and land will increase tensions and could lead to violence. Women are more vulnerable in these situations because they lack resources and information. Natural disasters will lead to higher household indebtedness, which may in turn contribute to more household-level tensions and conflict.

The Gender Action Plan proposes activities and targets for each project component and output, so that the project contributes to empowerment of women and girls through their participation, capacity-building, institutional and resource strengthening, and provision of services. The project will provide training and responsive initiatives, thus contributing to better adaptation to climate change. By its end, the project will have achieved increased resilience, having provided training and responsive initiatives to 80% of households in the project zone. This includes technical and financial assistance to gender-specific activities.

The project will provide training and responsive initiatives to 50% of field workers and 20% for technicians; the aim is 50% participation by women (taking into account that some activities may be 100% female or male). The project will develop a Gender Action Plan, which will include specific targets for gender equality and empowerment of women. Fuel-saving and reforestation are other subjects of project intervention and are of particular interest to women.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP187

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The establishment of the Ouidah Basin Climate Resilience Initiative (OCRBI) platform offers a unique opportunity to boost innovative climate resilient investments in agro-ecosystems, fostering an enabling environment for micro, small and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs), better access to finance, and microcredit in particular. |  |
| The partnership with the Global Fund for Environment and Climate (Fonds National pour l'Environnement et le Climat - FNCEC) is expected to foster further partnerships and access to finance for the OCRBI platform is a key strength of the proposal. |  |

1. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the respective term sheet and addendum vi, titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations."

## Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

### Project background

2. The Ouidah Basin Climate Resilience Initiative (OCRBI) Benin project will develop and scale up adaptive and productive capacity of agro-ecosystems and smallholder farmers in the upper and lower basin areas, with a focus on building resilience for poor and vulnerable smallholders and to scale up climate resilient agriculture and agroforestry practices in the Ouidah Basin.

3. The objectives will be achieved by securing resilience through the following activities: scaling up climate resilient agriculture and low emission agroforestry practices; building adaptive capacity of smallholder farmers, particularly women, for climate change adaptation in 89,680 hectares; and reducing the vulnerability of 330,100 small-scale farmers to increasing


---
**Page 122**
---

climate changes and extreme weather events in the Oubangui Basin. In addition the project is estimated to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 1783.63 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (tCO2e) over 20 years as a result of the improvement in farming systems and reducing deforestation.

4. The project targets the main food basket of the country, the Oubangui Basin, which is also Benin’s main economic cornerstone with a very diversified agricultural production. Agriculture continues to be the backbone of the economy, employing 70 per cent of the population and 85 per cent being smallholder farmers. The main farming systems in the target area are built around rice, maize, sorghum, millet, beans, yams and other value crops and fruits.

The local food systems of the upper and middle Oubangui Basin are very vulnerable to climate change impacts. These include increased frequency and intensity of droughts as well as floods that have a direct impact on the productivity of the food systems, translating into further challenges for smallholder farmers who are already struggling with poverty. Smallholders are forced to change their livelihood strategies to become more dependent on unsustainable land use practices such as slash-and-burn, and unsustainable and over-harvesting of forestland, leading to increased production costs and reduced yields, which in turn undermines food production in the area.

Climate change will continue to drive this vicious cycle, with heavy impacts on agricultural production. Estimates suggest that the impact of climate change could reduce the main crops such as maize and yam by around 10 per cent, leaving communities with very challenging livelihoods and increased vulnerability to poverty.

The total project financial need is USD 53.5 million with a request to GCF for grant finance of USD 27.8 million. The Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries (Ministère de l'Agriculture, de l'Élevage et de la Pêche, MAF) through an International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) project will contribute USD 12.6 million in the form of grants. The Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (Ministère de l'Environnement et du Développement Durable, MoESD) is providing USD 8 million in kind, supplemented by an in-kind contribution from the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) and grant support of USD 197.500 from the National Fund for Environment and Climate (Fonds National pour l'Environnement et le Climat, FNEC). The environmental and social safeguards category of the project is B.

2.2 Component-by-component analysis

Component 1: Low-carbon, climate resilient crop production enhanced and ecosystem services restored in the upper and middle Oubangui Basin (total USD 21.3 million; GCF cost: USD 12.1 million)

This component will boost the resilience of the target agro-ecosystems to enhance the climate change impacts on food security, livelihoods and ecosystems. It will include sorghum, cowpeas, chilies, okra, mango, shea and cocoa, and to increase restoration of degraded lands through reforestation.

Farmer field schools (FFS) will enhance access to resilient species and varieties to help diversify local farming systems while increasing the capacity of climate resilient agricultural techniques. The FFS will provide communities with the knowledge, training and resources needed for sustainable land management practices. It will also introduce innovative techniques following the concepts of climate resilient agriculture (e.g., diversifying the species mix with species and varieties that are more tolerant to drought), and increasing conservation farming, rainwater conservation and agroforestry.


---
**Page 123**
---

### Component 2: Climate resilient and gender-sensitive value chains, supporting farmers' livelihoods in the upper and middle Gambia (total cost: USD 5 million; GCF cost: USD 1.1 million)

#### 3.1 Impact potential

The project will directly benefit 330,000 vulnerable farming communities (and indirectly 5,470,000) in the key food basket of the country while transforming the local food systems and livelihoods in parallel through the restorative landscape activities and


---
**Page 124**
---

### Strength of agroecosystem systems which together will contribute to a mitigation impact of 1.7 MtCO2eq in 20 years.

- The project will contribute direct solutions and opportunities to transform the local food systems, increasing resilience and reducing the pressure on forest resources as well as sequestering carbon through improved land management efforts.
- These efforts will further support diversifying livelihoods and will offer opportunities for smallholder farmers to access new markets and market opportunities.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential
Scale: N/A

- The climate resilient adaptation interventions have been extensively developed through the collaboration with the national meteorological service (precipitation, temperature, evapotranspiration) and the hydrological service (run-off, discharge).
- The annual temperatures in the region are projected to increase by 1.5°C on average while generally decreasing with a shortening of rainfall events.
- Observations also show increased frequency of extreme weather events such as droughts and floods.

#### 3.3 Sustainable development potential
Scale: N/A

- The project will enable communities to increase investments in the local food systems, which are currently underutilized due to climate risks and constraints supporting a long-term transformation of the basin.
- The objective is to create an innovative environment for start-ups, smallholder farmers, and other stakeholders to develop new products that reduce food loss and waste at the production and post-harvest stages. It will also provide further opportunities for communities and MSMEs to diversify their income sources.

The project will be implemented through funds (NEFC, INDIA) and partnerships with local government agencies, research institutions, and private sector partners and networks.
- The project will provide opportunities for upscaling commercial, private-sector, MSMEs, LFs and microfinance to ensure continuity for innovative agriculture investments and solutions that cater to the current and future climate changes beyond the lifetime of the project.


---
**Page 125**
---

29. The project will further help smallholders gain access to finance and provides opportunities for them to establish their own micro enterprises thereby contributing to rural entrepreneurship and economic empowerment of women and youth beyond the lifetime of the project.

30. Finally, there is a strong focus on sustainable management of the landscapes from the first two components strongly focusing on boosting the capacity and investments in resilient and agro-ecologically diverse landscapes; boosting and conserving biodiversity; and restoring degraded lands and ecosystems. This will provide smallholders with alternative and more diverse options for investments in sustainable harmony with surrounding landscapes and food systems.

### 3.4 Needs of the recipient

**Scale:** N/A

27. The project targets very vulnerable and poor smallholder communities across the Ouidah Basin Watershed Master Plan area who are considered high by the target groups as highly dependent on subsistence farming for their survival and are currently facing numerous hardships from climate change and other current socioeconomic issues.

28. The project will provide these communities with additional opportunities for sustainable farming, such as providing resilience to climate change and adaptive solutions through access to finance, diversification of income generation, job creation and fostering entrepreneurship.

29. The OECI platform enables communities to reinvest in their local food systems and for community members to benefit from investments and local partnerships. These investments and locally catered solutions speak directly to the needs of the local vulnerable populations and their directly identified needs.

### 3.5 Country ownership

**Scale:** N/A

30. The project is well aligned with national strategies and policies in the agriculture and food security sectors and the Government of Benin (GoB) will be involved in the implementation of the project. The GoB will also provide strategic guidance on the overall direction of implementing the three components as well as the OECI platform during and beyond the project.

31. PNEC will receive strategic capacity-building assistance across the three components, enabling PNEC to grow in its role as a GCF accredited entity (AE), in particular focusing on its role in the implementation of the three components for the present and future. PNEC will be in a unique position to support scaling up to other basins and continuing to foster resilience and adaptation across the basin.

32. The project targets the high ambition of the nationally determined contribution, focusing on food and water security through the climate-resilient approaches promoted across the three components. This is further supported by the Ouidah Basin Watershed Master Plan, which describes the existing scope and opportunities for irrigation in the basin


---
**Page 126**
---

3.6 Scale: N/A

The project focuses on increasing resilience for communities through more sustainable farming practices and improved livelihoods through the CCR platform enabling to build long-term financial investments in the basin and the basin for the long term. Microcredit and long-term financial planning through FNEC, FNDA and the OCRI platform will ensure leveraged private sector finance and investment in the basin.

The combination of access to finance organized through FNDA's stewardship with the microcredit and long-term financial planning through FNEC, FNDA and the OCRI platform's strategic position to facilitate matchmaking between farmer cooperatives, groups, MSEMs and private and public partners is considered very innovative and will continue to drive innovation and entrepreneurship across the basin at the same time offering affordability.

### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

Project background: The project will scale up climate-resilient agriculture and agroforestry practices, build waterworks to reduce soil erosion and run-off, and assist in improving land use and management practices for the communities in the municipalities of Agbogbo, Gboghozohy and Zagazino in the Ouémé Basin. Among the project's environmental co-benefits are improved soil fertility, increased crop yields, reduced post-harvest losses through diversification, increased tree planting of agroforestry species, improved water recharge, protection against floods and droughts, and enhanced resilience to climate change. This will increase productivity of the farms as well as improved ecosystem functions and services and protection from climate-related risks like floods and flash-blooms, among others. Its social co-benefits include increased income for farmers through better yields and access to markets, capacity to diversify income generation and livelihood and capacity building activities.

Impact on GCF Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Standard: The GCF has classified the project as moderate risk which is consistent with GCF's environmental and social (ES) risk category B, to which the Secretariat confirms and that it is within the AEs E&S risk accreditation. The Project will be subject to a detailed Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) and will prepare elaborate site-specific Environmental and Social Management Plans (ESMPs). These ESMPs will include an environmental baseline for each of the selected 5 municipalities. It has likewise conducted a SIA and engaged stakeholders documented through the ESSM. The project is expected to comply with GCF's E&S standards.

Compliance with GCF’s Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Standards: The paragraphs below identify the key risks and/or impacts as regards the GCF E&S standards and how they will be addressed.

#### 4.1.2 ESS I: Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts

The project is itself an environmental and social enhancement project and does not involve new or additional environmental and social risks and impacts. The proposed activities such as surface water collection works (e.g., mini-dams, weirs, water retention etc.), large dams with spillways, and the construction of small-scale irrigation systems that will cover over a wide region are mostly intended to conserve water and soil resources, restore ecosystem services, and improve livelihoods for communities. The project is expected to result in good farming practices, access to micro-credit, and improvements in the value chain of their crops. The activities are expected to have potential adverse environmental and/or social risks and impacts that will be managed through largely known and readily available addressed through good management practices and adaptive strategies during implementation.


---
**Page 127**
---

Based on the proposed project interventions, there is a risk that transboundary livelihoods may be impacted and a Social Impact Assessment (SIA) provides a more detailed analysis in relation to local communities including plans to safeguard and promote opportunities for participation. The SIA will also provide guidance on how to address these risks during implementation which will undergo (i) screening, assessment, review, and clearance process; (ii) preparation of Environmental and Social Management Plans (ESMPs); and (iii) monitoring of the implementation of these ESMPs.

### ESS 2: Labor and Working Conditions
The AE has triggered its Decent Work Standard (SDS) for all project activities. The AE will ensure that all workers are paid at least the minimum wage, and families outside of school hours have also been observed. To mitigate this, the AE proposes to provide training on labor laws and working conditions to all workers employed in the farming value-chain works. It is also proposed to ensure that "all staff employed on the project will be hired consistently with the prevailing labour laws and working hours to which the country adheres". The AE will also ensure that no child labor or forced labor is used for activities which may have "Labor and working conditions involving harmful, exploitative, involuntary, hazardous, or other forms of abuse, including sexual exploitation, reproductive health and safety issues". While the country has ratified all eight (8) fundamental ILO conventions, it is proposed to conduct a baseline survey on labor laws and practices to ensure consistent implementation of labor and OSH audit protocols particularly for contractors of project funded works.

### ESS 3: Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention
Efficiency in the use of water is embedded in the project's water conservation activities. The project is not expected to use significant amounts of water, as it will be used only for irrigation of crops that are highly polluting waters. Anticipated waste would be crop husks and trimmings or rejects from small-scale milling operations which could be composted on site. Temporary sedimentation of water bodies may occur during construction of small irrigation systems and other civil works. There may also be issues regarding contamination of agrochemicals. While provision is made for the application of good agricultural practices (GAP) in the project, GAP practices will be conducted to farmer groups at demonstration sites; the project will recommend that farmers follow these practices. The AE proposes to conduct training on safe handling of pesticides will also be provided and based on The ESMP indicates that construction activities are expected to generate dusts and temporary sedimentation of receiving water bodies. These impacts have been addressed in the environmental impact assessment (EIA). The site-specific impacts of construction will be addressed in the construction ESMP.

### ESS 4: Community Health, Safety and Security
Potential issues on community health and safety include the risk of transmission of infectious diseases in the project site communities due to influx of workers or due to change in local population dynamics. The project proposes to construct small-scale irrigation infrastructure such as irrigation systems. Dams will be small scale and hence would not pose significant risks to downstream communities. The use of pesticides will be managed through training on safe handling, storage and disposal of agrochemicals and its accompanying technical guidelines. Waterborne diseases such as malaria, dengue and typhoid fever are unlikely to occur in the project area due to the small size of the proposed dams could enhance breeding grounds for vectors. The project should ensure monitoring of occurrence that disease outbreak or error may increase the rate of spreading it in the communities.

### ESS 5: Land Acquisition and Involvement of Beneficiaries
The project rules out any government acquisition of private lands or revenue or physical assets from landowners. Activities will mostly employ the involvement of communities on a purely voluntary and demand-driven basis. Project activities will use farmer-owned lands, thus, the project will cause


---
**Page 128**

**43. ES6: Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources**

The project area is explore with natural habitats such as forests, swamps, and other fragile ecosystems that are under threat from human activities. These areas are increasingly being threatened by population pressure, including overgrazing and slash-and-burn agriculture. The project will work to conserve biodiversity and promote the sustainable use of natural resources by improving productivity and restoring ecological services. To mitigate the risk in terms of tenure security, the project will support the development of a land-use plan for these habitats, the project will train farmers on environmentally and socially appropriate farming practices, and it will provide training on the conservation of native species using locally registered/seeded seeds. The project has further listed the following as deemed non-eligible:

* activities of any kind within natural habitats and existing or proposed protected areas;
* land use that is not compatible with the conservation of biodiversity (e.g., conversion to cash crops, livestock production);
* deforestation;
* commercial logging;
* large-scale industrial agriculture;
* genetically modified organisms;
* non-native species;
* products derived from endangered species;
* products derived from threatened species;
* products derived from protected areas;
* products derived from natural resources that are not renewable (e.g., minerals and fossil fuels);
* products derived from exploitable natural resources; * introduction of non-native species, including genetically modified organisms;
* conversion to other uses such as urbanization or industrial use;
* conversion to other settings so as to be non-native, and harmful to farmers livelihoods/resources;
* significant conversion of land for large-scale commercial agriculture;
* activities that are not environmentally sustainable and/or environmental and social gains do not clearly outweigh any potential losses.

**45. GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ES7: Sustainable Livelihoods**

The project has the potential to impact on the rights, livelihoods, or access of their members to lands and resources. A social impact assessment has been included to provide more detailed analysis in relation to the specific impacts that may be experienced by indigenous peoples as a result of participation. Actions include the inclusion of Fulani representatives in community meetings and other project activities, and the establishment of a dedicated focal point for indigenous peoples as part of the overall environmental and social management plan. As the specific location of proposed activities under the project have not yet been determined, a framework approach has been adopted to ensure that all potential impacts are considered. This includes consideration of pastoralist and vulnerabilities including collaboration with institutions implementing national and local adaptation plans (e.g., the National Livestock Development Plan) for inclusion of pastoral farmers and herders; inclusion of these risks and appropriate actions in the Local Adaptation Plans (or equivalent); and the development of a Field Herder Support System (FHSS) for pastoral farmers and herders to be included in the Farmer Field School (FFS) curriculum, based on success of this approach elsewhere. The project will also ensure that indigenous peoples are informed about the topic are promoted with other projects in the region and close monitoring during project implementation of risk and impact of mitigation measures. In line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, a dedicated focal point for indigenous peoples will be available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.

**45. ES8: Cultural Heritage**

The project has ruled out impacts on any cultural heritage site or structure and has assessed as less likely any encounter with archaeological artefacts during project implementation. Nevertheless, a "Chance Finds Procedure" has been


---
