included in the ESMF in case there would be such encounter during excavation works of some activities.

### Sexual Exploitation, Sexual Abuse, and Sexual Harassment (SEAH) safeguarding

The project has triggered AE’s ESS7 (Descent Work) and AE’s ESS8 (Gender Equality) as part of the social safeguarding component. The project will also address sexual harassment in the context of gender-based violence (GBV) and SEAH. The project will take specific protection measures to prevent GBV and SEAH, including through a dedicated Gender Focal Point (GFP) who is responsible for this task from AE and include GBV and SEAH in the sensitization training on safe, decent rural employment, which will be provided by the AE to all workers. The project will also ensure that village GBV and SEAH-related grievances through the AE’s GBM system and ensure the system is accessible to women and men. The AE will provide specific guidelines for GBV and SEAH, including confidential reporting with safe and ethical procedures and follow-up actions. The project will also establish GBV pathways and provide timely services and redress to survivors.

#### Implementation arrangements

The project will have a Project Steering Committee (PSC) which will be chaired by the FAO Representative in Goma. A Project Coordination Group (PCG) will be established at the FAO Representation Office in Goma, to be led by a National Project Coordinator (NPC). The PCG will be responsible for the day-to-day activities at the national level and will provide guidance on project implementation. At the sub-national level, Implementation Units (LPIs) each headed by a Project Technical Director (PTD), which will serve as focal points for the project in each of the 12 LPIs. Each LPI will be responsible to the municipality which assigns a Project Focal Point (PFP). The PCG will have an Environmental and Social Safeguards Officer (ESSO) while at each LPI there will be an Environmental and Social Safeguards Advisor (ESSA) who will provide technical support to the ESSO. The General Manager of the Goma Office (GM0), FAO’s Lead Safeguards Advisor will also be engaged to provide long-term or ad hoc support.

#### Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure

The stakeholder engagements during the project proposal development and the plans for future engagements are discussed in Annex 7. A grievance redress mechanism is being established during the project proposal development with various stakeholders, including the provincial government ministries, local governments, civil society organizations, academia, and the targeted beneficiary communities. The GMB will be accessible to all relevant stakeholders throughout the project areas. The project also provides for plans for further engagements of stakeholders during implementation.

#### Grievance redress mechanism (GMB)

Project will have a GMB that is accessible to all relevant stakeholders through written and oral means and allows for resolution of complaints first at the local level. If the complaint cannot be resolved locally, it can be escalated. If a grievance cannot be resolved at the project management team levels, where the complaints be elevated to the PCG. If the matter cannot be resolved by the General (GG), Project affected persons may also reach out to the judicial system as a last resort.

### Gender policy

The AE has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirements of the Gender Policy of the GCF.


---
**Page 130**
---

Rights, the constitution of Benin prohibits discrimination based on race, sex and religion, as a bid to ensure equal economic and social rights to men and women. Further, through its ministries, Benin also tries to ensure gender issues are taken into consideration when preparing the Strategic Plan for Climate Change (SPCC). However, in practice, most poverty reduction efforts to reduce poverty have considered the specific interests of women, starting with the composition of the national committee on climate change and the representation of women in organizations. However, despite the existence of a favourable environment, gender inequality remains an issue in the country.

Agriculture is the main economic activity based on land review and stakeholder consultations with various groups including civil society, individual women as well as women’s groups. Women are responsible for most agricultural activities such as planting, weeding and harvesting crops, and men disproportionately impacting women. The impacts are harder for women owing to the level of restrictions social norms that prescribe certain roles and women and restricts the level of access to resources. Women have limited access to land, water services and technology, while their roles involve activities within and outside the home such as cooking, cleaning, childcare and managing household care responsibilities, while outside the home they engage in agriculture and productive activities. Women are also responsible for collecting firewood and water from distant sources. Women have also limited access to and use of tools and traction compared with men; within the household, labour division is gendered, with women being responsible for domestic chores and men working primarily on men’s farms first which leaves limited time for women to tend to their own plots or to undertake other activities. Time constraints to fulfil all prescribed responsibilities is a burden to women. Women are also less likely than men to have access to credit facilities. Financial technology are also limited to women, which results in lower yields from cultivation. Women’s representation in decision-making processes at the local level and in national government and climate governance is also limited, affecting their ability to cope and manage climate-induced changes. Women’s participation in decision making on land use and forest management is low due to given consent for women to be engaged in activities. Access to education, income and resources in general are also limited to women. The gendered division of labour means that women’s post-harvest activities carried out by women in the sector are also affected by the impacts of climate change, because the quality of their products is affected by the difficulties in accessing clean water, firewood and other inputs.

These realities, however, do not undermine women's significant contribution and knowledge to forest management. Women play a key role in the collection and processing of crops, in processing, distribution and sales of their produce. They are mainly involved in collection of non-timber forest products, collection and processing of nuts, locust bean fruit and other fruits, honey, mushrooms, medicinal plants and other forest products. When crops fail. Female-headed households in particular are involved to a large extent in these activities, since they have limited access to land and resources and are more dependent on forest revenues than male dominated.

With climate change and its impacts, such as droughts, the engagements and activities of women are increasingly affected. The shortage of water for household consumption increases women’s time burden as fetching water is considered women's work. However, women are less likely to have access to irrigation facilities or other technologies that could help them. Women’s rights and participation in water governance are often linked to land rights.

The AfDB has provided a gender action plan and further complies with the principles of the Gender Policy. The gender action plan is being implemented through specific targets, indicators, budget timelines and gender experts to support with the implementation of the gender action plan and ensure that all projects under the SPCC are gender-sensitive. This action will work towards ensuring the empowerment of women and girls, through capacity-building, gender sensitive planning and development for improved management of land and forest areas;

### Legal assessment

#### 4.6 Legal assessment

The AMA was signed with the AE on 8 June 2018, and it became effective on 4 October 2018.

The AF has provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.

The proposed project will be implemented in Benin, a country in which GCF is not provided with sufficient privileges and immunities. The country is also not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to be further assessed. No draft opinion on privileges and immunities have been dispatched to Item at this stage.

The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism and Independent Integrity Unit both have expressed their concerns about the lack of understanding by GCF for investigations, as appropriate, in countries where GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. The legal opinions and agreements by GCF are made only after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP187

## Proposal name: Ouémé Basin Climate-Resilience Initiative (OCRl) Benin

### Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

### Country(lies): Benin

### Project/programme size: Small

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel (based on the submitted funding proposal dated 14 April 2022)

### 1.1 Impact potential Scale: N/A

- Rain-fed agriculture is the main economic sector in Benin, contributing 59 per cent of gross domestic product; 70 per cent of the population is engaged in agriculture, of which 85 per cent are smallholder farmers.
- The most vulnerable areas to climate change and variability are located in the Ouémé River basin (particularly the sub-basins of the Ouémé, Ouémé-Nord, Ouémé-Sud, and Zougbodomey), where 6 million Beninese live; these target municipalities have been neglected until now.
- The most vulnerable population is that living in the Ouémé River basin (of which 50 per cent of whom are women); 61 per cent of whom work in agriculture.
- The main crops grown by farmers include millet, sorghum, maize, yams, cassava, and beans; Beninese crops (e.g., maïne, shea, cashew, mango) and 7,231 ha land. In times of crop failure, farmers resort to fishing, beekeeping, non-timber forest products and the trading of these alternative products.
- As per the climatic data analysis combined with population interviews and in-depth farmer consultations, the Ouémé River basin (particularly the sub-basins of the Ouémé, Ouémé-Nord, and Ouémé-Sud) is located, particularly exposed to the late and violent rains, long period of dry spells and high temperatures. In this context, where climate change has been negatively affected, agricultural land situation is observed in the Ouémé River; leading to floods downstream, and existing hydraulic networks are damaged.
- In the transition area (Glasoase), intense droughts, late rains, and excessive heat are the most prominent climate-related hazards.

Footnote:
* See funding proposal section A.1.2, feasibility study sections 3 and 4
* See funding proposal section B.5

There also affect food and cash crop production (including Shea and cotton), while causing damages to existing infrastructures. Finally, in the Middle Chadzone (Zagazigoune and Zanghodzogho), floods, long droughts, and heavy rains are noted as key climate change impacts, with repercussions on the livelihood of farmers and pastoralists. The impact of these changes under a 4.5°C scenario (not the doomsday scenario of 8.5°C) will exacerbate these same agroecosystems, communities, farmers, livelihoods and AICS.

In order to mitigate this situation, the project proposes three components (components 1, 2 and 3). Component 1 seeks to increase the resilience of agroecosystems (ie, water, land, soil, forests, crops, plants) over 950,000 ha in Chadzone (Zagazigoune and Zanghodzogho), with a focus on the development of climate-resilient agriculture (CRA) for farmers on the production side component 1(ii); the implementation of water resource management measures and the construction of small-scale infrastructure, such as dams and ponds, to increase water availability in the dry season; and the development of business models and finance for the farmers and the CRA component (2); and (iii) institutionalization of climate change adaptation policies and strategies at all levels of government. Component 2 seeks to build the capacity of the National Fund for the Environment and Climate (FNCE) to finance climate action in Chadzone, with a focus on the development of an operational framework for financing and disseminating best practices with other municipalities in Benin (component 3). As highlighted in the funding proposal annexes 16 and 17, the project will address the following components of the above-mentioned issues and challenges, that is, the totality of the package and not just parts of it.

The funding proposal has included feasibility studies on the OCB funding proposal*, waterways feasibility study**, and a vulnerability analysis***. The results from these studies which to determine the impacts and subsequently the program shifts to be delivered under the following three components of OCB:

* Project area: In the current year, climate-resilient crop production enhanced, and ecosystem services restored in the Upper and Middle Chadzone*, is essentially focused on enhancing the resilience of agroecosystems. The project will focus on the restoration of degraded ecosystems include resources such as water, soil, land, river and forests. The vulnerable agriculture sector includes farmers and their crops (ie, maize, cassava, cowpea, chilies, etc.). The project will focus on enhancing the resilience of agroecosystems through adaptation and resilience measures for the ecosystems include (i) engineering measures vis-à-vis the construction of small-scale infrastructure such as dams and ponds to increase water availability in the dry season; (ii) rehabilitation of 20 old surface water collection structures (e.g. small earth dams), with a focus on the restoration of degraded ecosystems over 1,500 ha (600 ha with OCB and 930 ha with IFAD); (iii) construction of 40 new surface water collection structures (e.g., head of streams, arboristic berelses, etc.); and (iv) bioengineering measures for land restoration in degraded ecosystems over 150 ha (60 ha with OCB and 90 ha with IFAD). (b) Component 2, "Climate-resilient and gender-sensitive value chains, supporting farmers' livelihoods and promoting a 'middle thematic,' will focus on enhancing farmers' and cooperatives' income streams and incentivizing private sector investments in CRA and resilient agriculture. The component is divided into two parts: (i) the development of FPS through training of farmers, provision of technology for post-production processing of crops and livestock products, and access to credit; and (ii) financial inclusion and Rural Development (FIRD), especially through loan guarantees from lenders (e.g., village savings and loans associations, microfinance institutions, and commercial banks in the Alafia"). The funding proposal targets high-value crops such as cashew, maize, teea, cocoa, mango, and will be expanded to other high-value crops such as cassava, copra, chili peppers, and pepper. The component is training for farmers that will centre on marketing and finance for farmers, including the development of a business plan, financial planning, and building the business case for climate-resilient agriculture and food production using a market-based approach.

Component 3, "An enabling institutional and financial environment established to promote and upscale low-carbon climate-resilient land and water management in Benin," will focus on creating an enabling institutional and financial environment to promote and scale up climate-resilient management in the Ouémé Basin. It will include the development of a Climate Change Unit (CCU) within the Ministry of Rural Development, creation of a national platform for dissemination of OCHC success stories/best practices and outreach to farmers, mobilization of resources from public and private sources, and the establishment of continuous support to climate-resilient farming in the Ouémé Basin (i.e., de-risking farmer financial transactions through a combination of financial advisory and loan services). The funding proposal will also include the development of an information system on farmer loans by accredited MFIs. Other initiatives would cover strategy formulation, networking and capacity building for stakeholders, the establishment of an awareness campaign on integrated climate-resilient management among the relevant stakeholders in the AVC.

5.1.1 Adaptation impacts

The following sections present details on the climate change variability and extreme events that have been happening in the five targeted municipalities at Middle and Upper Ouémé, and how this has impacted the agroecosystems (e.g., water surface/water groundwater), crops


---
**Page 141**
---

The key AGFC. Using a combination of scientific, observed data and anecdotal evidence and testimonies from the climate farmers (i.e., stability of yields, quality of crop production, post-production commodity prices, etc.), the OCBI project has elaborated a clearer picture of the activities of the Quilmes farmer (i.e., tilling to harvesting using traditional practices), combined with the use of climate-smart agricultural practices (CSAPs) and the vulnerability of these farmers to climate change. The CSAPs include: crop rotation, cover crops, intercropping, agroforestry, conservation tillage, organic farming, integrated pest management, water conservation, etc.

The OCBI project measures are “no regrets” measures such as (a) the restoration of 53 per cent of the total land in Middle and Upper Ojuelo (190,647 ha) to 179,556 ha complete with agroforestry systems (e.g., fruit trees, shrubs, grasses, etc.) and other vegetation; (b) conduct of combined capacity-building measures aimed at instilling climate-smart agricultural practices in the farmers; (c) establishment of a network of 100 farmer-managed natural resources management units (FMRUs); (d) institutional capacities via mainstreaming climate into the Quilmes development plan, institutional strengthening and capacity building; and (e) mobilization of financial resources for support of these climate-smart measures for the agriculture sector.

Project impact calculations provided a snapshot of how the OCBI interventions impacted the farmers’ livelihoods. The total project impact amounts to a 13 per cent improvement from the USD $15 business as usual net present value (NPV) to USD $20 OCBI project NPV/ha for all areas (Esquina, Higuera, Glaseo, Esgatado, Zapudohoney).

OCBI complete package impact was calculated using a very simplified simulation run using the Agroforestry Yield Model (AYM). The model demonstrates that the total combined effects of the OCBI interventions (i.e. cleared cropland and agroforestry systems) lead to a positive NPV and an internal rate of return (IRR) greater than 30 per cent. However, when compared with other interventions, NPV is negative and IRR is negative, meaning that an investment in the OCBI project would be more profitable for farmers than any other intervention. The investments of GCF alone in increasing the resilience of vulnerable ecosystem resources and built infrastructure are not enough to reach such levels even on their own.

## Risk to adaptation impacts is assessed as low.

### 10. The non-sustainability of farmers' behavioural change to continue practicing CSA agricultural practices in the absence of additional support from cooperatives and Dimitra Clubs (see para. 47 below) could address these challenges as they are.

### 11. Adaptation impact potential based on the funding proposal package, responses to written questions and discussions during the meeting between the AE and the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP), is assessed as high.

## Mitigation impact

### 12. The OCB project is expected to mitigate 1783633 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2 eq) over 20 years, and 991813 CO2 eq per annum. The carbon sequestration will come from three main sources: the reforestation of degraded land banks, the restoration of perennial parkland with additional trees using agroforestry practices, and the development of small irrigation/irrigable systems. It is expected to sequester emissions of approximately 1496290 CO2 eq over 20 years. The second major mitigation source will be the reduction in GHG emissions from the conversion of degraded forest land, resulting in a sequestration of 2325181 CO2 eq over 20 years. The third relates to the avoided emissions associated with the development and implementation of a range of CSA practices, including the development of small irrigation/irrigable systems over 2000 ha land leading to a reduction in emissions of 525723 CO2 eq, and the discontinuation of mineral fertilizers and chemical pesticides. The total mitigation potential is assessed as high.

### 13. The risk to mitigation impact is assessed as medium.

### 14. According to the feasibility study, the current and future sources of farmers' GHG emissions are: livestock (50 per cent), rice production (28 per cent), waste (5.6 per cent) and industrial processes (1.6 per cent). The small success rate of farmers from improved harvests will increase their income by 34 per cent, which can be used for the purchase of inputs (e.g., food processing, driers, cold storage) and organic waste by-products on the AVGs; these activities will naturally lead to an increase in GHG emissions, especially if no mitigation measures (e.g. integration of climate-smart agriculture practices into the production systems) are implemented.

### 15. Mitigation impact potential based on the submitted document is assessed as medium to high.

### 16. Overall impact potential is assessed as medium to high.

#### Section 1: Economic and Financial Analysis (Annex A)

The paradigm shift for all agriculture sector stakeholders is clear and highly replicable, and suitable outside the OICR project areas.

- For component 1, especially regarding hydroagricultural development, innovations for control and efficient use of water, including innovative hydroagricultural developments, have huge potential to transform agricultural practices towards climate change protection and the restoration of springs or watercourses. These innovations show that there are no modern hydroagricultural techniques in the OICR project areas.

- The shift from traditional agriculture (TCA) to Climate Resilient Agriculture (CRA) would lead to long-term behavioral changes, which could serve as successful examples of CRA farming among other farmers and their adjacent areas. This shift from TCA to CRA is the paradigm shift.

- For component 2a per economic and financial analysis (annex A), overall farmers' incomes are projected to increase by 10%, with increases in income for components 1, 2, and 3.9%.

- For component 2b, there is a clear shift from mainstreaming climate in the OICR project areas towards the introduction of the OICR platform.

### 1.3 Sustainable development potential

**Scale: N/A**

#### Economic benefits:
If done correctly, the OCFI integrative approach will improve and transfer the economic benefits to smallholder farmers. The balanced combination of agreements/grading of traditional farmers on CRA plus the guaranteed loans for CSA initiatives will improve the livelihoods along the AVC. These measures will improve the agricultural productivity and income generation.

#### Gender mainstreaming:
The funding proposal includes a gender action plan, which seeks to mainstream gender equality in all project activities. The funding proposal also targets women who are women given that 50 per cent of the OCFI loans will be disbursed along the AVC.


#### Environmental co-benefits:
The funding proposal includes a safeguard package in the preparation of OCH and it is in environmental and social safeguards category B. Among the frameworks prepared are an environmental and social management framework and the stakeholder engagement plan (SEP), which will ensure that the project highlights the importance of and need for sustainable inclusion of vulnerable groups in the project implementation. The SEP also includes a reference to the United Nations Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) and GCF and the core requirements of Bank’s Safeguards.


#### Sustainable Development Goals:
The OCFI project can be linked to several Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), including No Poverty (SDG 1), Zero Hunger (SDG 2), Quality Education (SDG 4), Reduced Inequalities (SDG 5), Gender Equality (SDG 5), Decent Work and Economic Growth (SDG 8), Industry, Innovation and Infrastructure (SDG 9), Reduced Inequality (SDG 10), Responsible Consumption and Production (SDG 12) and Partnerships for the Goals (SDG 17).


#### Risk to sustainable development:
Assessment is as low.


#### Project risks:
The large number of smallholder farmers whose activities and subactivities creates the risk of failing to achieve these SDGs, the weak monitoring of the numerous smallerholder farmer activities may lead to environmental degradation or maladaptation and the risk of failure in the investment identified above.

34. Overall, based on the above, the sustainable-development potential of the project is assessed as high.


### 1.4 Needs of the recipient

**Scale: N/A**

#### Needs of the farmers and entrepreneurs (e.g., traders, buyers).


---
**Page 145**
---

10. Based on the in-depth stakeholder consultations that ran for four years until March 2022,** some needs pertain to the following: improving and long-term reduction of losses in agriculture and business value chain investments (e.g., from a baseline of 90 per cent crop damage due to climate extremes, to 50 per cent); reducing post-production costs; reduction in livelihood incomes in post-production AVCs; and strengthening the resilience of ecosystems and water resources (river basins) against the impacts of climate extreme events (i.e. flash floods, droughts, floods, droughts, salinization, changing seasons). Climate extreme events further exacerbate the fragile soil and land conditions from unsustainable agricultural practices (e.g., overgrazing, deforestation, poor crop rotation, excessive use and overuse of pesticides). As a consequence, the climate-vulnerable farmers constantly need immediate access to inputs (e.g. seeds, fertilizers, tools, water) in times of crisis (of crops, for loans, for businesses and new farm technologies, for financing skills and knowledge) and protection against price volatility (e.g., from high prices of staple crops and land that compete for scarce water resources). The OCB project (i.e. components 1, 2 and 3) will address these and all being well will lead to increased incomes.

1.4.2 Needs of the agroecosystems

36. As mentioned above, the funding proposal indicated how climate change, variability and extreme events are affecting natural resources, land, soil, forest, plants and crops that it will be exacerbated in the future; the OCB project will directly address this via engineering and biotechnological interventions (e.g., through the introduction of drought-tolerant varieties) as well as through OBC practices (e.g. no tillage practices, soil fertility management)**.

1.4.3 Needs of the agribusiness value chain

37. The funding proposal clearly indicated how climate exacerbates the issues and challenges in the agribusiness value chain, which is highly linked with a systematics that has hindered the development of a stable supply and demand value chain locally and internationally. The OCB platform will address this need as it is designed to create a network of production units (e.g., through the introduction of CSA models) and link them from post-production to the end markets. The funding proposal also presented the high-risk nature of the AVCs and the lack of access to finance for smallholder farmers, which are major constraints in this sector; the funding proposal addresses this via the introduction of FFS and farm business schools (FBSS) as well as through the promotion of CSA models (component 2) and the provision of climate finance to CSA projects by FNIE (component 3).

1.4.4 Needs of the Benin and Ouémé local government

38. OCB supports the intended national determined contribution under the Paris Agreement as published by Benin in June 2017**, which highlights the country's emission goals and targets for reducing emissions from deforestation, land use change and climate change resilience. The OCB project helps to reduce emissions, mainly from agriculture,** and increase resilience in agriculture. As climate is not integrated in the Ouémé master plan, the local


---
**Page 146**
---

government has agreed to mainstream climate in the plan, which will be effected under component I of the funding proposal.

If done properly, OCBII will squarely address these needs via a three-pronged integrated resilience approach: (a) climate proofing the agroecosystems (component 1); (b) enhancing adaptive capacity and resilience of smallholder farmers in the project area through training along the AVGs (components 1 and 2); and (c) mainstreaming climate among public and private institutions involved in the finance and investment policy planning and development (components 2 and 3).

### 1.5 Country ownership

Scode: N/A

OCB is aligned with Bosnia's nationally determined contributions. It is also aligned with national climate change initiatives, namely the National Adaptation Plan of Action [2008]/National Action Programme for Climate Change Adaptation, the National Law on Climate Change and the National Strategy 2016–2035. In addition, there are links to national development strategies, plans and policies like the National Development Strategy 2017, National Plan for Agricultural Investment 2018-2024, National Strategy for Sustainable Agriculture and Forestry 2013.

The letters of co-funding and engagement from the key government stakeholders show strong ownership of the projects. The Ministry of Living Environment and Sustainable Development (MLES) is the lead implementing agency. It has been supported by the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries (MAF). MAF supports OCB with USD 12,634 ZBO through the project for the development of agricultural sector in Bosnia and Herzegovina. The Agri-Agriculture Development and Market Access Support Project, Regional Program for the Integration of Agricultural Markets and Support Project: for the Development of Rural Sector, Gaziabad District, FYROM, and the National Fund for Sustainable Agriculture (NFSA) have co-financed USD 187,000 of co-financing over the six years of the project but only under component I. The NFSA follow-up letter dated 24 June 2020 confirms that the project has reached 35,690 direct and indirect beneficiaries in accessing loans, ensuring affordable loans at a rate of 12 per cent (lower than


---
**Page 147**
---

The GHG (24 per cent market rates) and de-risking farmer transactions or DCA production via the provision of a loan guarantee of up to 50 per cent on CCOA loans among MFS.

### Strong multi-stakeholder ownership is the OCO project's strength, capitalizing on its wide and deep stakeholder engagement. From the government side, there are the key line ministers responsible for agriculture, environment, rural development, forestry, fisheries, Glazov, Zagranado and Zagodobnye. From the ground, there are the targeted beneficiary communities, including farmers, women's groups, youth, and local authorities. Civil society organizations, academia, individual consultants and indirect contributors, also represent a wide range of interests, from environmental conservation to biodiversity, civil society interest, with producers, buyers, traders, financiers and investors along the AVCS. Stakeholder consultation began as early as the project formulation phase in 2016, culminating at a virtual meeting on 11 April 2017. The project is currently supported by statements of engagement from the following entities: the Association for the Promotion of Community-Based Savings Credit Societies (APSCS); the Association for the Promotion of Women's Empowerment and Advice (MIF group); Africa Green Corridor (crop buyers); and Association Karite Benin, Interprofessional de la Cacao et du Caoutchouc (IPCAC), the National Union of Farmers and Plate Forme Nationale des Organisations Paysannes et du Producteurs Agriculteurs du Benin (NUPAB).

### The project has very strong support among women. As highlighted in the funding proposal, the gender assessment and action plan and the feasibility study, women are the most climate-vulnerable group in Benin due to their limited access to land, credit, water, arable land and gain access to farm inputs and finance. Yet they contribute most to the agricultural production of food crops and cash crops for household consumption or livelihood; it is the women who will most likely remain within the home and community to ensure that day-to-day needs are met. Therefore, the project aims to empower women groups in timber forest projects to take whatever available measures possible to ensure access for food and other needs.

### One of the unique features of the OCO project is the strong participation of the Dimitra Clubs*, a network of community-based organizations composed of men and women that have been at least on discussions on gender, social and economic issues, village economics, local development and climate change. Potential lessons can be learned from utilizing existing social networks like the Dimitra Clubs in ensuring the strong understanding of the project's goals and objectives. The presence of a women group or movement ensures that women’s issues and challenges under a changing climate will be heard and addressed by the project team. This is one of several project components to ensure the project's practicality and sustainability.

### Risks to country ownership in particular are low, as:

* There is strong support from stakeholders coming from the public development agencies, community organizations and the private sector mitigates the risk to country ownership through the failure or shortcoming of one or more institutional stakeholders.
* The engagement with women groups ensures that they will be able to influence and promote behavioral changes among the target farmers and entrepreneurs along the AVCS, leading to a shift from traditional CA/CC practices, also ensures the sustainability of such CCA, FTS and

## Ratio of co-financing

Total funding is USD 35,314,576 million. The GCF grant component is USD 18,423,795 or 52.26 per cent of total project cost. The GCF funds attracted are able to leverage additional financing from other sources, including the private sector, for a total cost: USD 12,634,200 million (35.78 per cent) comes from MAPF (funding received from IFAD non-proceeds of loan), USD 9,000,000 million (25.53 per cent) comes from the co-financing from FAFD and USD 187,500 (0.53 per cent) from FNEC. GCF grant financing is able to attract additional funding from other sources for a total cost: USD 9,000,000 million (25.53 per cent) from FAFD and FNEC at 0.01x. Such funding support, be it in cash or in kind, further strengthens the commitments of each role of the accredited entity and the executing entity.

### The project

The project is implemented under the overall context of the OICR project. Under output 1.1, GCF funds are used to increase the resilience of the hard infrastructure and critical economic activities against climate change impacts. This includes: (i) water harvesting and retention infrastructures (activity 1.1.1), amounting to USD 16,632,736 million or 45.0 per cent from GCF funds for building water harvesting and retention infrastructures (activity 1.1.1) and another 6.12 per cent for strengthening the capacity of local communities in the use of these infrastructures; (ii) similarily, under output 1.2, of the total funding cost USD 4,872,300 million or 75.67 per cent comes from GCF funds and USD 1,590,000 million or 24.33 per cent comes from other sources via training systems under activities 1.2.1 (7.36 per cent) and 1.2.2 (86.27 per cent); and (iii) degraded land rehabilitation and reforestation (activity 1.2.3), amounting to USD 4,059,000 million or 30.08 per cent from GCF funds for establishing climate-resilient seeds and plants under activity 1.2.4 (10.39 per cent fin.), with 25,250 farmers enabled by the project to plant 76,250 ha of trees and shrubs in degraded land over a period of 10 years.

### Overall

The overall implementation of the project will increase the mitigation potential of the area. The total GHG emissions sequestered is estimated at 17,810 t CO₂ eq per year. With 43.65 ha of trees and shrubs planted under activity 1.2.4 (10.39 per cent fin.), the carbon sequestered from 2 to 3 t CO₂ eq between the baseline scenario (without project intervention) and the planned scenario (with project intervention) is estimated at USD 6,587 million or USD 1,317 per ha of trees and shrubs. The total amount of carbon sequestered by the project over a period of 10 years will be about 24 t CO₂ eq/ha per year. The cost of carbon based on the GCF grant cost is USD 18.52/carbon tonne; if based on the total project cost, it is USD 19.68/carbon tonne.

### Financial analysis

The project has a positive net present value and an internal rate of return of about 10 per cent. At a 10 per cent discount rate this has a net present value of USD 49 million and an internal rate of return of 25 per cent. The positive internal rate of return and net present value indicates that the OICR project is a viable one.

### Social benefit

The social benefit of avoided carbon emissions is valued at USD 20/t CO₂ eq.

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

Based on the assessment above, the independent TAP recommends this funding proposal for approval by the Board.

Proposal name: Ouémé Basin Climate-Resilience Initiative (OCR) Benin

Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

# Introduction

The proposed Ouéma Basin Climate Resilience Initiative (OBCRI) project aims to reduce farmers' vulnerability to the impacts of climate change and extreme weather events in the Ouéma Basin through an integrated approach that will enable a transition towards low-emissions agriculture while fostering sustainable and climate-resilient agro-ecosystems and rural communities. In the Upper, Middle, and Lower Ouéma River Basins and surrounding rural communities in the Upper and Middle Ouéma River Basins, the project will develop and scale up the adaptive and productive capacity of smallholder farmers to reduce their vulnerability to climate change.

Bamenda economy is based on rubber production which contributes 40 percent to GDP and over 80 percent to export earnings. The adverse impacts of climate change heavily affect the agricultural sector, hitting hardest the poorest and most vulnerable groups and individuals in communities. In Bamenda, as well as in other rural communities in the Upper and Middle Ouéma, rural communities experience high levels of poverty and food insecurity, which are exacerbated by climate change.

Women and men experience the impacts of climate change and extreme weather events differently due to their different social roles that shape women and men's roles, relations and access to resources and opportunities. Women are particularly disadvantaged due to their weaker access to land, credit, extension services, inputs, markets, and information on new technologies they shoulder inside and outside the household. Women are also under-represented in decision-making in agriculture, natural resource management and climate governance, which in turns affects their ability to influence change.

In the project area, women have smaller plots usually characterized by less fertile and more degraded land. They also have less access to small tools and animal traction because family fields and male-owned plots are prioritized. Moreover, women have little access to extension services on new technologies such as improved seed varieties, fertilizers, pesticides and irrigation technology. As a result, yields in women's fields are lower.

Nonetheless, women are involved in the production of crops and play a key role in processing, distribution and marketing of agricultural products. Women also face additional constraints often affected by the impacts of climate change with regards to the quality of the product and/or difficulties in access to clean water, fuel wood, and the drying and storing of produce.

Women are involved in forestry, and manage a collection of non-sustained production (NFP) and, in the project area, they collect firewood and gather and sell shea nuts, locust beans, vegetables which are a source of income and a coping mechanism when other crops fail, particularly during periods of food scarcity. Even though women's role in forest land management are often male-dominated, some women have started tree nurseries thanks to different development initiatives.

Floods or droughts affect the availability of water for the household (HH) and exacerbate women's time burden as fetching water is considered women's responsibility. However,

1 Source: MAEP. Réalisations campaign 2015-2016


---
**Page 158**
---

women are less likely to be formally involved in decision-making around water management as water rights and participation in water governance are often linked to land rights.

The combination of lower yields and income and climate-induced disasters can lead to higher indebtedness of households, which in turn can contribute to more tensions within the household. Women are often responsible for managing water resources and may be more vulnerable in the aftermath of disasters and crises. The district development plans of the project intervention areas highlight barriers to women’s participation and increased conflicts around access to pasture land and water sources.

This gender assessment provides an overview of the gender context in Benin, and in the project areas, with a focus on gender issues that are relevant for the proposed project. Specifically, it highlights key gender inequalities and barriers to women's empowerment as well as key gender considerations are effectively mainstreamed throughout the project proposal and opportunities for gender-responsive and transformative results are identified.

The analysis highlights key barriers that were identified by the team through the technical studies, consultations with stakeholders, and desk research. These include: (i) limited capacities to address climate change impacts in the Ouémé watershed; (ii) limited access to climate-resilient agricultural technologies and services; (iii) gender inequalities in land ownership and use with extreme rainfall and floods and pressure on water sources during droughts; (iv) social and cultural norms and gender inequalities that affect women's economic and decision-making capacities; and (v) limited institutional capacity to plan and implement climate change measures.

### 1.1 Methodology

The assessment is based on desk review of relevant literature on gender, agriculture and climate change in Benin and reports by different international and local institutions as well as by international organizations. The data was collected through a combination of focus group discussions, consultations and interviews with a variety of stakeholders, including representatives from relevant technical departments of counterpart ministries, civil society organizations, women’s groups, farmers’ associations, and other actors. Various views on various issues were collected through focus groups and discussions with authorities.

A total of 157 stakeholders in Glazoué were interviewed, out of which 77 were women. The list of interviewed participants is attached to this report.

Consultations with key stakeholders were guided by a questionnaire, which included the following guiding questions: How are men and women impacted by climate change in the project area? What are their perceptions about climate change? What are the social norms (in and outside households)? What are the power dynamics between men and women? What impact does gender have on land use, access to land, access to inputs and access to finance? How do men and women participate in decision-making around agricultural activities? How can the project address the barriers and consider gender in the design of the activities?

Women groups (active in processing of cassava, maize, shea, and active in vegetable production), and members of national farmers organisation, local groups of the ATDA’s municipalities, representatives of the women council (municipality and national level) were


---
**Page 159**
---

involved in the consultations at national and municipality level. Representatives of women’s groups and professional organisations were also present at the stakeholder consultation meetings.

### 1.2 Organization of the report

The report is intended as a ‘living document’ whereby the assessment data and Action Plan may be updated periodically based on new information obtained during the inception and implementation phases. It is expected that the project will monitor its progress, evaluate its impact, and provide feedback to further adjust and refine it. The gender expert assigned to the project will be responsible for a review of this document on an annual basis.

Following this introduction, the report is organized into six major sections:
- The first section provides an overview of the legal and policy framework for the promotion of women’s rights and gender equality in Berlin.
- The second section presents a general overview of the status of women and gender equality in the country.
- The third section consists of a gender analysis in the context of the project.
- The fourth section identifies major gender constraints and opportunities for the project.
- The final section lays out the strategy and action plan, which proposes specific strategies/actions to advance women’s empowerment and promote gender transformative action as part of the project.

## 2. Legal and policy framework for promotion of gender equality

This section describes the legal and policy framework for the advancement of women’s rights and gender equality in the country. The OICRI Gender Action Plan is aligned with this framework, ensuring the country's efforts to promote and mainstream gender considerations in policies and development.

### 2.1 International frameworks and conventions on gender equality

Berlin has acceded and ratified numerous international and regional conventions including:
- The Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) ratified in 1992;
- The African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights, ratified in 1996, which in Article 8 advocates for the protection of the family and the rights of women and men and child;
- The Additional Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights on the Rights of Women (ratified);
- The 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development, which commits States to “reform our economic systems in order to ensure that they promote human rights, gender equality, and fair ownership and control and other forms of ownership, financial services, inheritance and natural resources” (SDG 5);


---
**Page 160**
---

The Paris Declaration on the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change in 2015, followed by COP21 in Lima (2014) and the G7 Action Plan for Gender Equality of 2017 which aims to (i) ensure the participation of women in COPs; and (ii) develop gender-sensitive climate policies and implement gender-sensitive adaptation and capacity-building actions.

### 2.2 National legal framework for the promotion of gender equality

The 1999 Constitution of Benin prohibits discrimination based on race, sex and religion, and grants men and women equal rights in all areas of public life. Article 30 of the constitution enshrines the general principle of equality between men and women, and Article 6 proclaims the equality of Beninese citizens of both sexes.

In 2006, Benin adopted the 2008-2011 Strategic Guidelines for Development that promotes, among other things, gender equality, women's empowerment and improved social protection. In October 2007, Benin ratified the Law on Rural Land Tenure, which explicitly guarantees the right of women to inherit their ascendants or spouses' rural land.

In March 2010, Benin adopted a National Policy for Promoting Gender Equality, which aims to achieve by 2025 equality and equity between the sexes with a view towards sustainable human development. In January 2012, a law on the prevention and punishment of gender-based violence was adopted.

Furthermore, Benin Alafia 2025, Benin's long-term strategic vision, promotes gender equality through the following strategic axes: education and training of women; economic empowerment of women; promotion of women's participation in decision-making processes at all levels to ensure equal social status of women and the enhancement of cultures and traditions that favor the development of women; management of calamities and major environmental phenomena.

### 2.3 National and climate-related policies

Benin plans to integrate gender and rights-based approaches in the implementation of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and related strategies.
- The National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) includes measures to protect children under five and pregnant women against malaria in areas most vulnerable to climate change in order to contribute to the reduction of mortality and morbidity related to malaria. The 2013 update of NAPA indicates that gender has been taken into account in all activities but that the introduction of innovative techniques and practices requires extra support, despite the know-how already existing at the local level; external funding is required for development councils.
- The first Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) under the Paris Agreement sets as one of its objectives to "improve the performance of Beninese agriculture for it to ensure sustainable food and nutrition sovereignty, contribute to the economic and social development of the Beninese men and women and achieve the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). In particular SDGs 1, 2, 12, and 13 as well as promote


---
**Page 161**
---

# 2.5 Sectoral policies

## Agriculture

The Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries (MELF) takes gender into account in the preparation of the Strategic Plan for the Development of the Agricultural Sector (PSDSA). The overall objective stemming from the vision of the PDSDA is "to improve the performance of Benin's agricultural sector with a view to contributing to the improvement of living conditions in the economic and social development of Benin, and achieve the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs)".

This objective is broken down into three specific targets, namely: (i) contribute to growth and food and nutritional sovereignty through efficient production and sustainable management of farms managed by both men and women and by women and young people; (ii) ensure the competitiveness of agricultural products in Benin's export markets while ensuring that they are produced by women and vulnerable groups through the promotion of agricultural value chains; and (iii) build the resilience of vulnerable populations, including family farms (plant, animal and fish). The PDSDA is a strategic document for the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries. It includes actions aimed at improving the conditions of women and their access to resources, with a specific component on promoting gender in the agricultural sector.

Additionally, the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries has developed a national plan that revolves around the following objectives: (i) strengthen the institutionalization of gender at all levels for better management; gender mainstreaming in policies, programmes and projects in the agricultural sector; (ii) promote the participation of women and young people in the agricultural sector through civil society organizations and other private actors in the agricultural sector to promote gender; and (iii) materialize the principles of equality between men and women in the agricultural sector in Benin.

Finally, the Plan for the Development of Women in Agriculture and the Sector (PDDA), aims "to contribute to the improvement of the living conditions of rural people by giving women and men equal opportunities so that all participate in the same way in Benin's development process".


---
**Page 162**
---

# Water resources

Two main documents, the Water Code and the National Water Policy, were analyzed for the purpose of assessing gender mainstreaming in water policies.

In the Water Code, no special emphasis is placed on gender, but the document highlights the equity and participation of all social strata. Article 5 of the Code promotes the sustainable management of water resources by local authorities. It states that the competent authorities, each in their respective areas of competence, will ensure that users have equitable access to the available water resources. Article 6 on public participation also makes recommendations that the Minister of Energy and Water Resources should make use of when consulting stakeholders to implement participatory water management at all levels: national, basin, local authorities and village communities.

The basic principle of the National Water Policy is that "water must be managed in a holistic way, taking into account all its uses and functions, in the interests of efficiency, equity and transparency." Among the three objectives of the policy, the first is to ensure equitable access to water for all users. The second objective is to promote gender equality in the management of resources in Benin; no emphasis is placed on gender in terms of their functioning and composition.

# Forestry

The overall objective of the National Forestry Policy is the conservation and rational, integrated and sustainable management of forests, wildlife and other natural resources in order to contribute to the development of rural areas. The policy recognizes gender as its implementation, the State, through the CPR (Rural Promotion Centres) and commitment to social equity, ensures that forestry-related programmes and projects take into account the specific interests of women, starting with the composition of intervention teams and by encouraging their participation in actions in organizations representing the beneficiaries.

# Social protection

In the CPRS, Benin has made social protection one of the six priority areas for interventions during the period 2011-2015. The Social Protection Policy, developed by the Inter-Ministerial Social Protection Committee with the support of the United Nations Children's Fund (UNICEF), was adopted in December 2013 and is being implemented through the CPRS. The policy aims to bring Benin, by 2025, a nation of solidarity and social well-being that protects people, especially the most vulnerable social strata, against economic risks.

In the area of social protection, non-governmental organizations (NGOs) have created a national network for women's rights and gender protection and implemented concrete actions focused on: (i) the right to women's financial empowerment; (ii) the right to promote the decision-making power of women in decentralized and national bodies; and (iii) the institutionalization of gender through the designation and nomination of gender focal points.


---
**Page 163**
---

3. Overview of the status of women in Benin

### 3.1 Poverty and demography

Women represent 51.2 percent of the total population, against 48.8 percent of men. At the national level, 22 percent of households are headed by women. The proportion of woman-headed households is significantly higher in the south than in the north. In Cotonou, the proportion is 31 percent (AGVIS).

From the socio-cultural point of view, women heads of households are not easily accepted by society, and they often have to face social discrimination. This explains why there are more female-headed households there. This is the case of the Littoral, Atlantique, Ouémé, Collines, Mono, and Zou regions. In these departments, more than 20 percent of households are headed by women.

According to the RGPH 4 (General Census of Population and Habitat) 4), there is a greater disparity in income in rural areas between women and men. The Integrated Modular Survey of Households (IMSH) shows that 39.9 percent of female-headed households were more affected by non-monetary poverty (39.9 percent against 25.2 percent for male-headed households) than by income poverty (27.6 percent versus 38.5 percent for male-headed households).

### 3.2 Food security

In Benin, 11 percent of households are food insecure (1 percent severe; 11 percent moderate). These households have a deficiency in food consumption or cannot meet their minimum food needs. Food insecurity mostly affects households in rural areas (15 percent) rather than those in urban areas other than Cotonou (8 percent).

Households headed by women and men are equally affected by poverty (11 percent of the population) but often the poorest. About 74 percent of food-insecure households belong to the poorest (48 percent) and poor (26 percent) quintiles of surveyed population (AGVIS). More than half (56 percent) of households headed by women are in the lowest income quintile.

The national survey on Global Analysis of Vulnerability and Food Security (AGVIS) highlighted that very little disparity exists between female-headed and male-headed households in terms of food security. However, there is a significant difference with regard to the proportion of men (23 percent) and women (24 percent) with poor/limited food consumption.

This lack of disparity between households headed by men and women as measured by these indicators does not mean that there are no differences in food insecurity or nutritional status between households headed by men and women, and this difference has been observed only in rural areas.

According to the 2012 Demographic and Health Survey (DHS), 70 percent of women decide for themselves how to use their earnings and in 18 percent of cases, decisions are jointly made by the couple.

The women's knowledge of dietary diversity and a healthy diet is important for the nutritional security of family members. In terms of the impact of an incomplete diet on health, women of reproductive age and children under five will be the most affected. Socio-cultural constraints on the distribution of meals and certain food taboo areas (chimpanzees) affect women. Women are also more affected by the fact that they spend more hours on work every day. Among others


---
**Page 164**
---

# 3.3 Voice and representation

In Benin, society is generally patriarchal. Decision-making rests with men in households, local and institutional bodies.

The presence of women in the local councils (communes) is very limited and in the project areas, it is only in the Council of Glazoué that out of 256 councilors (3.5 percent) are women.* In other councils (Copargo, Djiguou, Zanganaoudo and Zogbodomey), women are not represented.

Traditional law, reinforced by the interpretation of the texts by some of the local religious, gives women and youth little access to voice and decision-making. Even decisions about women are made by men in their capacity as heads of households or chiefs of villages. Women's participation in community development. For example, in the Ouémé Basin, a woman’s participation in cultural, political or economic activity is subject to the authorization of the husband. The segregation of women in the decision-making process has led them to be excluded from access to information and decision-making bodies as well as their relative lack of opportunities to strengthen their knowledge about climate change adaptation strategies.

# 3.4 Literacy and education

One of the most relevant causes of poverty in Benin is literacy, which affects 67.4 percent of the total population. Overall, illiteracy affects 78.1 percent of women compared to 44 percent of men.* Women are more likely than men to be illiterate or have a very high level of illiteracy (30 percent in the 45-49 age group), those living in rural areas (85 percent) and those living in the poorest households (94 percent) (INSEE, 2002). In rural areas 68 percent of the rural population is literate whereas only 17.3 percent are illiterate.

Thanks to policy efforts in the education sector, school statistics show that girls are massively more enrolled than boys in primary schools (118.7 percent in 2013). However, girls fail to complete their schooling and drop out of school at a higher rate than boys after marriage and poverty. As a result, child-related educational and learning burdens weigh more heavily on women than men, and their incomes are relatively lower than men's.

# 3.5 Access to and control over resources

The inequality between men and women is particularly strong in the context of access to and control over natural resources, especially land.

In Benin, land tenure is regulated by both statutory law and customary law. The majority of land is still managed under customary law either religious or religious law. Customary law consists of a set of traditional rules determining the position of people with regard to the allocation, management and exploitation of land and related resources (water, pasture, forests, etc.).

* Source: Development Plan 2018-2022, Dassa Zoumey.


---
**Page 165**
---

While there are different modalities to access land, they all presents challenges for women:
- access by occupation, which tends to disappear (women have no right to access in occupation in the project areas);
- heritage, which women continue to be excluded from in some parts of Benin;
- purchase, which is difficult especially for women who often do not have an income;
- gift or inheritance that allow their child daughter or their wives to access part of their real estate heritage.

In general, women gain access to land by purchase, inheritance, alliance and gift; men by patrilocal inheritance and purchase. Seventy-three percent of available areas occupied by men belongs to them while only 54 percent of the land used by women actually belongs to them. In the project area, women have no right to inherit or sell land. As a result, most of men in the project areas, women rely mainly on the willingness of men to provide them with land for their personal use (Rapport final sur l'accès des femmes à la terre, MCA-Bénin, "Projet Accès au Foncier" 2008, 105 pages).

Although women have access to land through inheritance, especially in the Upper Benin, they have no control over it because they are expected to leave their families and live in the family of the husband. In addition, their rights over these lands are precarious, and they have little financial autonomy.

Moreover, the control of resources is generally the responsibility of men as heads of households. The factors influencing this situation include: the weight of tradition, the low levels of education of women and lack of knowledge of their rights, traditional reproductive responsibilities of women and girls, non-regulation of marriage and the many divorces.


# 4. Gender issues in the project implementation sites

A number of gender gaps in access and control of land, water, inputs for agriculture, knowledge, financial capital, participation in decision making processes, workload and education levels are observed between men and women. These gender gaps need to be taken into consideration when designing and implementing the activities of OGRi. This section of the document highlights the most important ones that the project will address through its Action Plan.


## 4.1 Crop production

In Benin, all social groups are involved in crop production. In the cropping systems, men, women and youth have different roles. To maximize the use of family labour, family members participate in different ways in working in the family fields. In households, women are involved in sowing, marketing operations and post-harvest activities but they do not plough for cultural and social reasons. As for men, they are more involved in soil preparation (tilling, digging) and mounds and ploughing the lowlands. Some crops are managed by men from land preparation until harvesting (yam); for most other crops, men prepare the land; women assist with planting, weeding, harvesting and men usually are responsible for treatments with pesticides.


1 Rapport final sur l'accès des femmes à la terre, MCA-Bénin, "Projet Accès au Foncier" 2008, 105 pages.


---
**Page 166**
---

If a family owns animals for plowing and weeding, women are less involved in weeding; but all other weeding work that cannot be mechanized is done by women. However, all decisions concerning types of crops, techniques to be used, organization of the work, management of the resources, etc., are the prerogatives of the head of the household, who is usually a man. Women in Glazoué have no say on these issues and their participation in decision-making for this reason is minimal. The decision is influenced by choices made from the family plots and takes into account women's access to land, personal resources, and social status. Children are involved in all of the activities following gendered patterns of distribution of roles.

The cultivation of cash crops is largely dominated by men (70 percent), while women prioritize food crops for family consumption. However, women also contribute to cash crops through the provision of labor. On average, 45 percent of the area under legume cultivation and 31 percent of the area under vegetable production are managed by women.

For most crops, yields obtained by women are lower than those of men. The pressure on land is high and land degradation and loss of soil fertility is one of the problems faced by women and men. Climate change is affecting production due to uneven distribution of rainfall, floods, droughts, and pests. Women have limited access to credit and inputs; they need to be adapted to new production conditions, a critical lack of water management facilities and of technical know-how.

As head of household, the man is also in charge of the field, holds the ownership title or lease and ensures the organization and the management of the plot. The few women who are owners of land or farmers in their own right are here, widows or urban women who invest in agriculture. In Copto, for example, 20 percent of the plots are owned by women. This is more common for women to be mobile since buyers come and collect the produce or the crops can be processed and stored and sold by women during lean seasons. However, in Glazoué, market gardening is not developed and its promotion could contribute to food and nutritional security of households.

In Glazoué, women are involved in the production of rice, maize and soja. Cooperatives have been established for these crops but they are not very successful because most of the time men control the income and the expenditures at the household level. The cultivation of rice has been affected by climate change and women have adapted by starting the planting season some weeks earlier.

Women are disadvantaged due to the quality of the land (generally depleted) and less access to inputs. In gardening, the reason for lower yields is the difficult access to pesticides and lack of funds to buy them. Women produce 25 percent of the production in the fields of vegetable gardens and one-third of the production once for onion cultivation (23 percent) (Statistics from MAEP 2015–2016 campaign data). The constraints of arduous agricultural work and the lack of access to inputs are also a problem. Women have to spend time in their husbands' fields at the expense of time spent on their own income-generating activities.

Cotton farming is the most important cash crop in Benin, followed by cashew. Despite women's strong involvement in cotton production (80 percent), the sale of cotton provides a direct income to women (from the sale of cotton). Furthermore, women and children face health risks while working in cotton fields where pesticides are being applied. Some women use their own money to buy insecticides for their crops or they buy them at local gardeners'), which exposes them and their families to high levels of toxic residues. In Glazoué,

16


---
**Page 167**
---

### 4.2 Producer organizations and cooperatives

The weak representation of women in farmer organizations is justified discursively by men with reference to women’s limited engagement measured in terms of the total surface cultivated by women, and the average plot size. Women hold only 0.7 percent in decision-making posts in farmer organizations. In Benin, the National Federation of Farmers’ Unions (FENAF) has formed a National Association of Beninese Female Farmers, and are present in the decision making processes for the entire agricultural sector. FUPRO (Federation of Producers’ Unions) has set up a Women’s Committee as part of the National Platform of Farmer Organizations and Agricultural Producers (PNOPPA).

In Glazoué, women have organized strong cooperatives, however, more elderly women are active in these organizations than younger women. The most successful organization is the Union of Women Parboiled Rice Processors. The cooperative is registered and holds regular meetings; it is technically supported by PAEEFR (Support Project for Farmers’ Cooperatives) and financially supported by the Glazoué Department. Other organizations are the Union of Rice Producers of Glazoué and the Benin National Rice Processors Organizations Framework (CNTRB). Overall, the representation of women is 52 percent and of men 48 percent.

### 4.3 Livestock and fisheries

The situation of women in the livestock sector varies among ethnic groups. For the Puhas of northern Togo, more than half of the herd is the responsibility of men. The herd (sheep, goats and cattle) belongs to the head of the household. Women can benefit from the milk and own sheep and goats. The Futan woman will process the milk (cheese and butter) and market it. The women also keep chickens for their eggs which they sell or use them as a source of dairy products; women use these resources for the purchase of food and for personal needs.

Men are responsible for grazing, migration (transhumance), watering, milking, breeding, stock keeping and selling livestock. Women’s participation in this sector is limited because reserves are reserved for adults and young boys whose level of involvement is around 60 percent and 35 percent, respectively. In some ethnic groups, women are the main managers of small livestock (sheep, goats, chicken, and duck).

Fishing is largely dominated by men, but women play a dominant role in the processing and marketing of fish.


---
**Page 168**
---

### 4.4 Post-harvest activities

Women are often responsible for storage of food stocks for the family and, depending on the crop they manage, processing and marketing. In Benin, post-harvest activities have been underfunded and harvest management is often based on traditional know-how and technology.

Several institutions, including the Agricultural and Food Technology Programme (PTAA) work in support of the sector. Since 1992, the PTAA has been one of the national research programmes of the National Institute for Agricultural Research of Benin (INAB). The PTAA's mission is to: (i) improve agricultural productivity; (ii) increase access to market facilities; (iii) ensure the conservation of soil and improve farmers' working conditions; (iv) adapt, and if necessary develop new technologies in order to reduce post-harvest losses caused by damage by microorganisms, insects and rodents; and (v) adapt the existing technologies and develop new ones for the processing of food products.

### 4.4 Rural finance

Agricultural credit and loans are contingent on owning land, thus women are disadvantaged by their lack of access to land. However, a national policy for the advancement of women in agriculture was adopted in 2007 with a view to address this issue, widening the criteria for loan applications and encouraging medium- and long-term loans. In general, women are much more likely to be excluded from credit than men.

According to the latest edition of the Benin Women's Social Indicators Yearbook, women had access to 26 percent of bank loans but obtained 60 percent of available microcredit, compared to men, who had access to 74 percent of bank loans but obtained only 35 percent of microcredit.

Systems of access to credit organized by "tontine" groups are very numerous in Benin and allow a large number of women to access the capital needed to expand their enterprises and the working capital for their agricultural activities. By contrast, commercial financial organizations have been able to circumvent structural barriers to access finance through formal institutional channels, such as banks, which often require collateral such as land titles. Furthermore, they have developed a system of active solidarity, which could be further supported.

Several initiatives have been put in place to develop microfinance and promote youth and women's entrepreneurship. These include the creation of a national fund for microcredit through a presidential Microcredit Programme for the Very Poor (PAMCPV) in 2007, and the allocation of a substantial budget in 2008 aimed at supporting women who comprise the majority of workers in agriculture. The government has also provided support to commercial credit institutions such as the National Microfinance Fund (FNM) and the National Fund for the Promotion of Entrepreneurship and Youth Employment (FNPEYE).

Funding is provided by public funds, including those from the national budget and private funds. Public funds are constituted by resources of the national budget and external resources provided by Technical and Financial Partners (TFPs). Private funds relate to investments made directly or through financial intermediaries such as NGOs (such as the agricultural workers organisations (OPA), NGOs and the private sector.

* CEDAW (2013), p. 22.


---
**Page 169**
---

The evaluation report on the implementation of the Strategic Plan for Agricultural Sector Recovery (PSRSA), 2011–2014, analyzing the funding over 4-year implementation of the PSRSA mentions: (i) the creation of 50 new financial services agencies (ASF) with a total capital of KOF 1.5 billion held at 33.52 percent by women; (ii) the granting of about KOF 17 million in loans to women and youth; (iii) the increase in the volume of appropriations by various projects of the APRM and the National Microfinance Fund (FNAM) for producers, including women and youth; and (iv) the creation of the National Fund for Agricultural Development (FNDa) created by decree in January 2014.

The report concludes that the financing of the agricultural sector has stalled because of the constraint of high risk associated with agricultural activities for moribundance structures.


### 4.5 Land use and ownership

Women have less access to land, which reduces their capacity to adapt to the impacts of climate change and affects tenure security is a key factor influencing decisions about investing in soil protection and improvement measures.

Land ownership also drives decisions to invest in tree crops* and in soil restoration through agro-forestry and cover crops. Water rights and forest ownership are interlinked to land ownership, and women’s access to these resources is limited by their lack of secure tenure over land. Women have less access to secure land, water and forest would be an important motivating and enabling factor.

The implementation of the land policy is already decentralized and much of the work to register and provide security for land rights has been done at the commune level. The Rural Property File (FPR) were elaborated in Atsoraa-Dongo (Copargo and Dospougo) and 49 Village Land Management Section (SVGF) trained and equipped to improve the implementation of the land policy. The district directorate of agriculture is responsible for coordinating with other actors on specific strategic actions on securing land tenure for women and youth. The project could raise awareness of authorities at the district level to facilitate secure access to land for female and youth participants.


### 4.6 Access to training, information and technology

Traditions and the burden of care and household work often limit women’s availability and mobility, which reduces their capacity to participate in extension services.

Local leaders (men and women) and participants might be asked to transfer the technical information provided during trainings to their wives but this transfer of information, if it all done, causes a lot of problems. The participation of women is also limited by the fact that they attend meetings and trainings and to participate in cooperatives is restricted by gender norms. Women’s participation is controlled, and has to be authorized by men. Having female trainers and extension officers will be key to engaging women in the project sites.


*It should be noted that the Mutual Agricultural Insurance of Benin (AMAB) is functional but is experiencing difficulties due to low membership from farmers.
*In Copargo and Dospougo, women who are given land by their husband are not allowed to plant trees or to make major investments on the land.

There are other additional programs available for women when French is used during trainings, technical leaflets and in radio and television messages.

Additionally, men prefer that women receive training and extension services by female extension workers; however, the national extension services are male-dominated. According to FAO (Food and Agriculture Organization) data from 2013 (State Permanent Extension Officers [APE]/Rural Development Technical Officers). It is believed that they are more sensitive to women's needs and are more likely to create opportunities for women.

The access to the Internet through mobile phone connections has improved between 2013 and 2018 because of the penetration of mobile money services and apps for health services.
