Taking into account higher literacy rates of women and time constraints and socio-cultural barriers, the project should use communication tools that facilitate access of women at their own time and in the vernaculars women are comfortable with. Radio programmes and videos make relevant information accessible to women.

When the project will formulate its communication strategy (Component 3, Output 3.1) and identify the different knowledge products, it will provide details on the content and the targeted audience. The gender analysis should be used as a basis for identifying at least a minimum of 30% of communication channels that present the case for women's participation in agricultural, organisational and institutional domains. A communication strategy including newsletters and events where women can share their experiences and knowledge with other women is essential, where knowledge products such as training material developed under Component 1 & 2, and data derived from the gender analysis will be shared. The communication and outreach strategy will be designed to ensure the platform's continuation beyond the project, especially to continue to reach out to women who are not directly involved in the project. Live action programs featuring their exchanges on success stories and challenges. These lived experiences should be captured by the Dimitra Bureau should be a source of information and content for the communication team.

As part of the communication strategy, communication channels will be identified in consultation with the communities and stakeholders where women will be able to express their needs and concerns. The communication strategy will be designed to ensure that it is gender-sensitive so as to best address any specific gender-based constraints (e.g., social and cultural norms preventing women from accessing and using mobile phones, mobility and time constraints etc.).

### 4.7 Value chains

The crops most affected by climate change and selected for interventions on climate resilience include maize, cassava, cowpea, chilies, okra, green vegetable, tomatoes, yam, shea, mango and cashew nuts. The practices and responsibilities of women and men in these crops and their value chains differ.

Of the total surface cultivated by women, the largest share is cultivated with cereals, with 35 percent for maize (10 percent), cowpeas (8 percent) and yam (7 percent). Women cultivate 24 percent fruit/vegetables. Men cultivate mainly cereals (53 percent), with mostly maize (49 percent), followed by cassava (16 percent) and sorghum (12 percent). Women cultivate mostly cassava (12 percent) and yam (11 percent); pulses (17 percent), with mostly peanuts (10 percent), soyas (6 percent) and cowpeas (4 percent); and vegetables (3 percent of surface cultivated).

Women are not involved in yam cultivation. As for maize, sorghum, rice and cowpeas, women are involved only as post-harvest workers. Women are also involved in the majority of the post-harvest processing, storage and hedging and trade activities. In their own fields, women perform most of the tasks (except land preparation) except for some heavy tasks (except land preparation for which they would hire labourers – or their husbands would do on behalf as is the case in Gbogolo).

Women cultivate a wide range of crops and use a higher number of varieties, taking into account cooking preferences. Because of knowledge on the variety of crops and their performance in their fields, women are able to contribute relevant know-how for adaptation and resilience in the context of nutrition security.

When compared with crops where men use inputs (pesticides, fertilizers) the yields obtained by women are higher than those obtained by men in all cases except cassava. Women obtain higher yields than men in cassava (but with little difference), soja, peanuts, voandzou.

### Agroforestry
Forestry resources are an important part of the livelihood strategies of rural populations and provide alternative incomes during lean seasons. In river basins, forests are of great importance in protecting soils and regulating hydrology as well as reducing and stopping the impact of floods.

Women use NTPs, process the products and sell them on the markets. In the project intervention areas of Gologo and Dougou, women collect shea nuts to produce shea butter, new fruits such as açaí, cocoa beans, coffee, cashew nuts, palm oil, palm leaves and flowers and juice and oil from seeds (PAGEFCOM, AIDAE Evaluation Report 2005).

In Zangarano and Zogodoum, the local government promotes planting of indigenous tree species such as mahogany. The women collect these trees to produce charcoal and harvest quantities of wood energy for cooking and collect or buy from male and youths involved in charcoal production. The problem of deforestation is very important in Gologo and contributes to aggravating soil erosion.

Fertil are often involved in NTP trade to complement income and overcome shocks, and, as documented in the coping strategies, charcoal production and fuel wood sale provide income for men in Zangarano and Zogodoum. The strategy would not contribute to increasing income but would increase the availability of NTPs, which can be collected, processed, and sold by women. The strategy would not only contribute to increasing income, thereby enabling access to more resilient

*Direction générale des forêts et des ressources naturelles, 2012. Politique forestière au Bénin.

source of energy and reducing unsustainable activities like charcoal production, but would also restore land in the Ouémé Basin, thereby reducing climate-change vulnerabilities.

### 4.8 Women's work burden and time use

Women play an important role in food and nutrition security for the household and investing to build their capacity is essential for food and nutrition security. The project should take into account women’s specific needs and constraints, for example in terms of availability of time to attend extension activities. Climate-resilient practices and new technologies will not benefit equally all women because they are often responsible for water collection and have less access to information, technology and training and productive resources and finance.

However, these need to be supported by initiatives to promote social change and transformational changes in gender roles and responsibilities that are also influenced by climate change. For example, during interviews in the field, men mentioned that if rain falls more frequently, women will have less time for other activities because they will be required to replace more rains also mean more weeds and more work for women and youths who are responsible for these activities. Both will increase the time women have to spend on fetching water.

Women are scarcely engaged in water-management activities while water scarcity affects mostly women in terms of time needed to stand in a queue to fetch tap or borehole water. Water collection is often done by women, especially during dry seasons when water becomes scarce. Water resources management should take into account women’s roles and time invested in fetching water and firewood.

# Table B: Barriers to adoption of climate-resilient practices by district

## All districts

### Copargo

- **Institutions:** Women's groups
- **Barriers:**
  - Quick gain strategy of men and women in agriculture, which can be built on through production of crops that generate income-generation.
  - Yam is man's main crop and with climate change, which means loss of land for yam cultivation. Cultivation of yam are heavily dependent on priority crops (maize, rice, etc.)
- **Men:** Man expects incentives to contribute to community development projects and overall poor mobilization

### Djougou

- **Institutions:** Women
- **Barriers:**
  - Time constraints for women, who have less access to land during the period of time, which burdens them with household chores.
  - Women have more difficulties in accessing credit from the household because of pollution of water sources and conflicts around water sources (e.g., lack of boreholes).
  - Social-cultural barriers and discrimination lead to women's under-representation in decision-making processes and their marginalization in the public sphere.
  - Women do not participate in income-generating activities, which is confronted with lack of information and technical assistance in their areas of activity.
  - Women work mainly in value chain activities that are seasonal and suffer from deficiencies and lack of access to credit.

- **Women:** Women have less access to land and opportunities in agricultural development.
  - Traditional attitudes determine women's low participation and lack of decision-making power.
  - Lack of long-term training and extension services.
  - Women have no control over waste water evaluated on the farm.

Female Community-Based Organisations were subject to an inventory by the Ministry in charge of social affairs (MTPFAS) and its division on the promotion of women and gender and senior citizens in July 2017. In the project areas, the groups that are functional were 69 in Djougou (Copargo and Djougou districts), 9 in Zangandogo and 6 in Zogbodomey at the time of the field survey conducted by the authors.

The total membership for groups in Djougou was indicated as consisting of 1,495 women members. The groups in Djougou exist since 2010; 19 were started between 2010 and 2015 and one group was started after 2016. The groups are active in the following sectors: 38 in agroprocessing (29); 4 worked in agriculture and gardening, 4 were working in the livestock sector (poultry, pigs); 10 were savings and traditional finances; and 4 groups were active in storage and marketing of agricultural products.

The processing activities of the groups were mostly on multiple processing activities but the specialized groups worked on soya (10 groups); peanut oil and cakes (8), shea (5), cassava (3), locust beans (2) and others (1).

The groups in Zangandogo worked in processing for 11 years, for 4 cassava (5 and for other products); 3 were in gardening; 4 had activities in agriculture. 2 groups were in production and marketing. In Zogbodomey 4 groups worked in cassava processing and 2 in peanut oil and cakes.

There are no details on the membership of the groups in Glazoué but most of the groups have activities in the sales and production of cassava, soya, rice, pineapple or are specialised in processing products (7 groups) and others (3 groups).

### 5. Strategies to Integrate gender in OCHR

In order to create transformational change, the project considers women not as victims of climate change or beneficiaries of the project, but as key contributors and equal actors in project activities and climate change adaptation efforts. As seen in the assessment, women are highly involved in agricultural production and other income-generating activities in their productive and livelihood activities and strategies and in the household. By recognizing and valuing their skills and capacities and promoting their roles, the project aims to engender a process of transformational change that will enable women to participate more fully and increase their influence at different levels and their participation in climate action and governance.

This proposal, which address gender dimensions within the project design and implementation, identifies strategies for integrating gender into the project. These are based on lessons learned through the Dimtra Clubs approach combined with farmer field schools and other activities, particularly in upper Ouémé where traditional social norms make it more difficult for women to participate fully in agricultural production.

The gender-responsive approach recognizes and takes into account the particular needs, priorities, and realities of diverse groups of men and women, including the specific constraints they may face, and adequately addresses these in the design and implementation of the activities. The Dimtra Clubs approach is a transformative approach that goes beyond the symptoms of gender inequality. It tackles the underlying causes of inequalities (norms, attitudes, behaviours) and generates positive change in areas of (i) awareness, consciousness and confidence; (ii) values, norms and practices and in (iii) policies, laws and institutions.


---
**Page 181**
---

Woman headed households are not easily integrated in and accepted by society, especially in rural areas. According to the EMRGAT survey (2011), women heads of households in Benin are more affected by non-monetary poverty. The specific gender-based constraints of women headed households (e.g. access to productive resources, services, time burden and limited participation) are addressed through a multi-sectoral approach called the "Dimitra Club" approach by proactively promoting their participation in FFS and in the Dimitra Clubs. The Dimitra Club approach is based on the principle that women's empowerment is achieved when local institutions and leaders are involved in community dialogue on a voluntary basis. Women heads of households are able to participate in the Dimitra Clubs and express their specific needs as well as those of other members of their household. This will contribute to increasing the participation of women heads of households in the Dimitra Clubs, FFS and BFS and take measures to encourage their participation, as needed.

The project will also contribute to the prevention of forms of GBV in its intervention zones through awareness-raising activities. As indicated, all districts (see table 8) have highlighted the risk of increases of cases of GBV due to the impacts of climate change and the lack of an agriculture insurance system.

The Dimitra Clubs through social dialogue promote discussions around discriminatory norms and customs that are root causes of forms of GBV such as early marriage, forms of psychological violence, food taboos, excessive workload for women and girls), with communities. The Dimitra Clubs will also work in collaboration with NGOs (Social promotion centres, NGOs) the Dimitra Clubs will also undertake prevention activities (awareness-raising, community dialogues) and provide advice to victims.

In Benin, several institutional actors are active in the prevention of GBV (quote actions such as Social Promotion Centres, NGOs, etc.). The police and judicial authorities are the front-line service for grievance and provision of help to victims. Several International NGOs and CSOs also engage in preventing GBV through awareness-raising activities, community dialogues, group discussions, media campaigns, artistic activities (cinema, theatre, graffiti, slam, puppetry, etc.), trainings and peer education (peer-to-peer), radio TV programmes, etc.). The actors involved are the entire community, women, men, girls and boys, religious and traditional leaders, artisans, teachers, opinion leaders, local authorities, students, etc.

The grievance and redress mechanism in Benin is organized as a digital platform for denouncing GBV. It allows any person to report an incident of GBV or witness to report a case of GBV without having to go to a social promotion centre. There also an option to send a message to 'green telephone numbers' or an email to: denounced@stop-vogbenin.org Social


---
**Page 182**
---

promotion careers and police services are the proximity institutions handling the reporting of GBV.13 Specific attention will be paid to the integration of gender in all capacity development activities. The gender expert (BLN 1.2.1) will mainstream gender in each of the training modules. The gender expert will train master trainers on gender analysis, gender transformative approaches and gender-based violence (GBV). The gender expert will also provide technical support on and provided basic training on the Dimitra Clubs approach as they will be working with FFS in the communities where the Dimitra Clubs will be established.

The Training of Trainers (ToT) will include financial and business matters. The gender expert will work together with the external experts on financial and business matters to adapt the content and design and deliver specific training modules that respond to women's needs and interests, as well as their economic empowerment. The gender expert will also participate in the training of master trainers to provide technical support on gender issues.

The gender expert will accompany the creation of the network/association of the Master Trainers (BLN 1.2.3) and the Gender Focal Points (BLN 1.2.5), as well as the creation of a network/association to operate as technical business advisors will secure its future scale-up and sustainability of the project.

At least 40 percent of the facilitators will be women. As facilitators will work in their municipalities, training will be conducted in the area; the mobility constraint for women will be overcome. As a result, it is expected that more women will apply and shall be encouraged to become facilitators.

The Master Trainers will conduct the trainings of facilitators (ToFs).

First, a training curriculum will be developed based on the key priorities of the Quibéin Basin. The gender expert will mainstream gender in the training curriculum and develop specific modules that respond to women's needs and interests, as well as their economic empowerment. Services responsive services and value chain upgrading. The gender experts (national and international) and gender focal points (GFPs14) will participate in the validation workshop, which will also be attended by the master trainers.

The thematic areas covered include: FFS methodology; climate-resilient agriculture (CRA) for maize, cassava, cowpea, chilies, okra, green vegetables, tomatoes, yam; agro-forestry with shade, micro-enterprises and livestock production (main focus on milk dairy); and construction and maintenance of small waterworks. For each theme, the gender aspects to be mastered by the facilitators will be highlighted.

13 The mechanism for supporting victims of GBV includes three main components that revolve around the identification of the case, the reception and consultation with victims and the provision of care. Several services are available, including: medical assistance (e.g., emergency obstetric and neonatal care, postnatal care, legal certificate, follow-up, etc.). This support is provided by health facilities and by specialised NGOs; (ii) psychological support (counselling, psychotherapy, etc.), which can be provided by specialized NGOs or the police; (iii) security, prosecution, reparation, etc., offered at the level of the Communities, Courts, and (iv) social support also to victims of GBV. This last group includes emergency accommodation, family or social reintegration, socio-professional reintegration, etc.

Furthermore, both the content and the organisation of FFS and FBS will take into account women’s specific constraints.

The gender expert will work together with the external experts on financial and business matters to adapt and provide specific training modules for women related to women's specific needs in relation to financial services. The gender expert will also participate as a gender expert in the training of Master trainers to provide technical support on gender issues.

Gender-responsive services and amenities of FBS will include: services and trainings will be adjusted to the time availability of women; if internet and mobile telecommunication services are used (input supply, marketing, etc.), then the project will ensure that women have access and cultural norms do not face when using phones and internet; when safety during mobility is an issue there will be measures taken to reduce distance and exposure to violence.

To maximise coverage and impact of FBS, women should be informed on the services and amenities of FBS and will address at the community and district level the gender constraints and put in place the actions needed to overcome them in order to guarantee equal benefits and access for men and women.

The project will integrate objectives and indicators disaggregated by gender. The gender dimension must be cross-cutting throughout all the areas of intervention, it should not only incorporate specific measures for women and men separately; objectives and actions should therefore already be pre-defined in the logical framework to ensure that actions are included in the annual work plan.

The project will ensure that the stipulated institutional provisions take gender into account, enable implementation of strategies, and measure the proposed gender integration.

The project design will take into consideration the following gender implications:

- Women’s role as primary homestead and resource managers;
- Gender differences in vulnerability and impacts of climate change on women and men;
- Analysis of gender division of labour (e.g. gender-differentiated roles, responsibilities, and needs); women's time burden;
- Women’s access to and control over natural resources and the goods and services they produce;
- Women’s access to services, information, technology;
- Women’s voice and decision-making power and collective action;
- Women’s economic empowerment;
- Assessment of gender mainstreaming in national and local development plans in relevant sectors;
- Financial budgeting for gender-related initiatives;
- Monitoring and reporting for continuous improvement;
- Systematic collection of sex- and age-disaggregated data and gender data;

The project implementation will take into consideration the following:

- Division of labour on farm, off farm and within the household;
- Investment of a gender specialist as part of the project;


---
**Page 184**
---

* Awareness raising and capacity development of counterparts and all project stakeholders;
* Development of gender-responsive soft and hard climate-resilient technologies supported by gender-sensitive strategies and approaches for dissemination and uptake;
* Development of gender-responsive value chains based on in-depth gender analyses;
* Approaches to incentivize and promote women's meaningful participation in all project activities;
* Promotion of female extension officers and trainers;
* Promotion of gender-responsive regulatory frameworks and institutional capacity-building;
* Implementation of gender-transformative approaches to support social and behaviour change.

In monitoring and reporting, the project will collect sex- and age-disaggregated baseline data that will be monitored throughout implementation and evaluation. Qualitative assessments will also be periodically conducted on the gender-specific benefits that can be directly associated to the project. This will be incorporated in the annual Project Implementation Report, Mid-Term Report, and Terminal Evaluation.

Quantitative indicators for the achievement of project objectives in relation to gender equality will include among others:

* Number of women and men as beneficiaries;
* Number of female- and male-headed households as beneficiaries;
* Change in livelihoods of targeted groups:
    * Female and male engagement in agricultural livelihoods
        * Women and men engaged in alternative income-generating activities;
        * Positive change in the consumption of food by household consumption and income-generation by women.
    * Time saved due to climate-resilience projects;
    * Business formation and expansion of women entrepreneur groups;
* Number of women and men engaged in agricultural processing;
* Number of women and men participating in natural resource management;
* Number of women and men participating in credit access groups;
* Number of women and men in farmer groups, farmer cooperatives or farmer associations;
* Number of women and men in leadership positions.

Qualitative indicators:

* Role of men and women in agricultural livelihoods;
* Opportunities for men and women to generate additional income;
* Time saved by women as a result of the reduction of labour hours required for agricultural activities;
* Contribution to better self-esteem and standing of women in households and communities;

### OUTPUT 1.2: 25, 250 farmers capacitated to implement climate resilient crop production practices and sustainable land management over 95,000 ha
Under climate change conditions, agricultural productivity in the Ouadi Basin could decline by 25%. Under this Output, selected facilitators will be trained on climate-resilient crop production techniques. A Climate Resilient Crop Production (CRCP) manual will be developed and a seasonal harvest rotation will be implemented, supported by the schemes set up under output 1. Agroforestry will not only stabilize soil, especially in degraded areas but also provide additional income to farmers through tree products such as fruits, nuts, honey, etc., and other new income streams for the beneficiary communities. Indeed, high-value, climate resilient tree species – like shea and cashew trees – will be promoted alongside indigenous species, to improve crop productivity and support sustained income – to be valued under Component 2 of the project.

### Output 2.2: Private sector investment for climate-resilient agriculture and sustainable land management techniques in Quimbi Basin

This Output aims to boost private and public sector investments in QCR activity – to secure long-term funding for climate-resilient management in the Upper and Middle Guineas, thereby reducing climate change vulnerability - by demonstrating the significant benefits of such investments. It will also provide a platform for dialogue between investors and farmers and set up a discussion forum on market opportunities and demands, setting out the basis for the first PPP in the Quimbi Basin.

Component 3: An enabling institutional and financial environment established to prevent and upscale climate-related risks in Benin’s Basins

Output 3.1: OCR project institutionalised through a multi-stakeholder platform with complementary frameworks and capacity building

There is currently no coordination mechanism that allows concerted efforts to sustainably manage Benin's basins, taking into account climate change-related risks. Moreover, there is no platform where producers and sellers, institutions from the public sector, private sector actors, civil society organisations (CSOs), and other stakeholders can meet regularly and promote innovations for climate-resilient agriculture and agroforestry. The OCR multi-stakeholder platform will thus be a key element in the implementation of the project. The design of this platform will be based on the lessons learned from the previous projects, with particular attention to the governance structure and communication strategy that will ensure sustained activity and the dissemination of information. The establishment of the OCR multi-stakeholder platform (MSP) will also take place via the platform, and inform future investments in project's interventions, as per Component 2.

# Output 3.3: Awareness of OCRM in beyond benefits raised among farmers and public and private institutions

There is limited knowledge in terms of how to sustainably manage basin areas while providing socio-economic benefits from upstream to downstream communities, and reducing climate change vulnerability. A lack of understanding of the environmental and socio-economic benefits of OCRM - which mixes watersheds with CCA and resilient land management adapted to upstream and middle basin areas - impacts farmers' willingness to participate in OCRM activities. To address this issue, a multi-stakeholder approach is needed. This includes communication strategy of the OCR platform, all training materials, knowledge products and M&E data from project implementation will be shared with stakeholders. The sharing of information on best practices and lessons learned through the use of gender-sensitive approaches to climate change adaptation and mitigation practices will be encouraged to further enhance the climate resilience of farmers, and attract private sector investments in climate-resilient

# ANNEXES

## Annexe 1: Analyse SWOT liée au secteur d'activités des femmes dans la zone d'intervention du projet


| Forces | Faiblesses | Opportunités | Menaces |
|---|---|---|---|
| Existence d'associations dynamiques des femmes dans quelques localités de la zone du projet | Accès limité de la part des femmes aux technologies modernes, ce qui limite leur accès à des informations d'alerte précoce | Intensification du niveau national du genre dans les politiques publiques et le changement climatique notamment l'agriculture | Manque de données pour la mise en œuvre des stratégies nationales axées sur le genre |
| Forte implication des femmes dans les activités de gestion de l'eau et du sol | Accroissement de la fécondité pour les femmes, ce qui limite leur accès à la terre au détriment de la sécurité alimentaire | Fortes implications des organisations dynamiques des femmes dans la zone du projet | Variabilité et changement climatique (pluviométrie inégale, inondations, etc.) |
| Forte implication des femmes dans l'économie alimentaire de la zone d'intervention du projet | Absence d'organisations dynamiques des femmes dans la zone du projet | Fiable représentativité des femmes aux instances de prise de décision | Existence de programmes de développement dans les zones rurales qui ciblent les femmes et les filles |
| Forte capacité de mobilisation et d'animation communautaire des organisations de femmes | L'économie de la zone du projet fortement basée sur l'agriculture, ce qui réduit la subsistance qui réduit la participation des femmes en période de crise | Existence des PDC prenant en compte les femmes dans le cadre de vie (reconnaissance et développement économique) | Dimension de genre incluse dans le CDN et PAN-A |
| Manque de moyens financier des femmes pour l'achat de biens améliorés pour la production de GES ainsi que les intrants pour améliorer la production agricole | Manque de représentativité des instances de prise de décision | Existence du plan d'action genre au niveau national dans le cadre de vie (reconnaissance et développement économique) | Meilleure capacité de gestion et de promotion des intérêts durables |


---
**Page 200**
---

| Forces | Faiblesses | Opportunités | Menaces |
|---|---|---|---|
| Fâble équipement des assesseurs de fermiers pour une transformation en quantité des produits agricoles | Manque d'opportunités des femmes pour reprendre leurs connaissances techniques dans les stratégies d'adaptation au changement climatique | Fâble capacité de maîtrise de l'eau par les femmes pendant la saison sèche | Difficulté d'accès des femmes et des jeunes aux ressources financières pour l'approvisionnement des semences |
| Fâble productivité des femmes (mariages, nés) pendant la sécheresse, ce qui engendre le manque de moyens financiers pour garantir une sécurité alimentaire des ménages |  |  |  |
